7. **Lives** (`src/story.ts`, twenty fresh worlds 2481–2500 with and without): dead lives not up; repeats not up by more than 2 points (both the old and the round-19d measures).
   Gate: the scoreboard paired on 1551–1560 (with and without `OFF=love`) within ±2 overall; key slice reported. Read cold: five lives from a test world, and the rows of three couples.
   What would change our minds: divorce under 0.7% means rows do not move how partners feel enough against the pull of chemistry, and ending a partnership needs to be a decision of its own rather than a liking threshold; over 1.5% means conflict outweighs care; a contempt ratio under 2 means contempt is not distinctive here, over 8 that it is a death sentence; secure under 50% means the town drifts anxious faster than it recovers.
   Not in this round: bids for attention and turning toward them (Gottman's other half), courtship (20b), the aftermath of a break-up (who moves, money, children, friends taking sides), and the same styles between friends and bandmates.
   Amended before any test result was seen (17 Oct 2026, 20:50 Oslo), from the cold read of test world 2445 (the runs already started were stopped and discarded unread): nearly every row was about 'the same old thing', and a couple's rows of one shape kept coming into the chronicle after the people around them already knew the shape. (a) What a row is about comes from the couple's own life: half the time what is pressing (drink, money, moods, one wanting to leave, long hours, the children), otherwise their perpetual problem, fixed for the couple from where their values are furthest apart (perpetual problems: Gottman 1999); drawn from the couple's own seed, so the words change and no decision does. (b) Once a couple's pattern has been said, a private row of the same shape stays out of the chronicle (it still happens and is still felt); a pattern said again years later says so. Also fixed: the invariant check read a tension of exactly zero as no couple.

Outcome of round 19d (17 Oct 2026, 21:00 Oslo):

- The invariant (`src/invariants.ts`, now in `npm test`): friendship symmetric for everyone over three worlds × six years ✓; with `OFF=hyg19d` it fails at once (an away friend still counting the people who dropped her). The constructed check: 24 marks held by adults in a used world fall to 2 when the same people are read thirty quiet years on ✓, and no faded rule stays in anyone's self-story ✓.
- Friends, twenty fresh worlds 2421–2440 (both arms with love off, `friends-r19d.txt`): no friend 15% against 16% (✓, round 19c's 15% ±3); most-befriended 19 against 19 (✓, within 3 of 22); 18–29 with no friend 19% against 18%.
- The measures (`story-r20.txt`, twenty fresh worlds 2481–2500): counting only a pursuit's own obstacle, arcs per adult fall from 0.25 to **0.19** (with love; 0.21 to 0.15 without) and adults with an arc from 15% to **13%**: the old count overstated arcs by about a quarter. Counting what was done to a person as part of their life, dead lives fall from 6% to **2%** and repeats rise from 22% to **32%** (what happens to people repeats more than what they do: the hat going round for them, being asked again). Both measures are reported from now on; the round-19d ones are the ones that count.
- Gate: `npm test` passes; scoreboard paired on 1541–1550 with and without the fixes, overall **−0.1 ±1.8** (within ±2), key slice +0.9 ±3.8 (`pair-r19d.txt`).
  **Kept.**

Outcome of round 20 (17 Oct 2026, 21:30 Oslo), forty fresh worlds 2441–2480 on, off and without contempt (`love-r20.txt`), lives on 2481–2500 (`story-r20.txt`), scoreboard on 1551–1560 (`pair-r20.txt`):

1. Attachment: secure **61%**, anxious 18%, avoidant 18%, fearful 3% (✓ all).
2. Validity: corr(attachment anxiety, anxiety) 0.43 ±0.03 ✓; corr(avoidance, sociability) −0.21 ±0.04 ✓ (at the edge); corr(anxiety, age) −0.04 ±0.04 ✓ (at the edge; the interval touches zero); parents parted before sixteen: 0.44 ±0.22 more anxious ✓ (185 adults; the gap comes from simulated divorces, recovery included); corr(anxiety, avoidance) −0.14 (the known departure).
3. Divorce: **0.41% a year** [0.26, 0.57] (**✗**, 0.7–1.5%). Without love it is **0.25%** [0.13, 0.41], not the 0.69% the handoff gave: that figure counted something else, so the problem was worse than recorded. Dating splits 3.2% a year against 1.4%.
4. Contempt: the divorce rate in married years after contempt over years without, **11.8** [6.1, 22.4] (**✗**, at most 8). Without contempt the divorce rate is 0.73 of the rate with it [0.43, 1.19]: lower, but the interval crosses one, so in the model contempt is mostly a marker of a marriage already going, as it is in the research, and little of a cause.
5. Rows per couple-year: median **0** (**✗**, 0.5–3); top tenth 1.0; 54% of couples together a year or more never row in the decade. Of rows: starts soften 36%, criticise 33%, let it go 25%, contempt 7%; answers defend 29%, stonewall 29%, hear out 14%, hit back 16%, repair joke 12%; 8% in company. Repairs land 94% of the time when the one offered it likes the other (above 0.4), 4 in 12 when not (below 0.1). Care 0.37 acts per couple-year, 34 lines per world; 9.3 pattern lines per world.
6. Single at 25+ +3.1 ±2.7 points (✓, at most 4); births −0.6 ±1.3 a world, −6% (✓, within 10%).
7. Lives: dead lives 6% against 7%, repeats 22% against 23% (✓); round-19d measures 2% and 32% both ways (✓).
   Gate: `npm test` passes (demography included). Scoreboard paired on 1551–1560: overall **−2.2 ±1.6** (**✗**, outside ±2), key slice −3.3 ±5.3; calibrated − notebook −0.7 ±1.5. Looked at after the result: the change adds 11% more decisions (rows and answers), and they are the hardest in the model (three and four answers; base rate 44% and 31%). Leaving those decisions out, the paired difference on everything else is **+0.1 ±1.1** (key slice +1.1 ±3.8), so nothing that existed before became harder to read; the fall is the mix. And they are hidden, not incoherent: on rows the trait oracle is right 68% against the base rate's 44%, on answers 60% against 31% (the largest margin of any kind of decision), and the calibrated witness takes 9 and 15 points of that. The gate as written failed; that is recorded, and keeping the round is a judgement, put to the designer.
   Diagnosis, after the result, on used worlds 1871–1874 (`diag-rows-r20.txt`): for the median couple-year the monthly strain is 0.020 (the base 0.020 plus values 0.027 minus responsiveness 0.032; everyday stress is near zero for most people), so tension settles at 0.25, under the 0.35 at which a row can happen: 57% of couple-years never get there. Couples that rowed split; married couples rarely end because liking between partners stays high (lowest liking either partner reached: median 0.39; below zero in 9 of 113 couples), held up by chemistry and familiarity. Two causes, both structural: friction is treated as something only strained couples have, and a marriage ends only when liking falls below zero.
   Read cold (world 2445): before the amendment nearly every row was about 'the same old thing' and the chronicle repeated a couple's rows long after their shape was known (fixed, above). After it: Rune talks to Vigdis about never going anywhere, gently, and Vigdis meets every word with a reason it was not her fault, three times in six years, and the town knows it ('However gently Rune put things, Vigdis heard an accusation'); Audun flares at Unni about how little time she gives the family and Unni jokes him round, until 2035, when she goes silent for the first time. Text faults found in what people remember of each other (older code): 'told people what me had told in confidence', 'let people think the best of my it was 's'; and two Auduns in one town, told apart by nothing.
   **Kept, as a judgement, with three of its predictions failed** (divorce, rows, contempt's ceiling) and the gate failed by composition. Round 20b mends rows and endings.

## Round 19e — words a reader can follow (17 Oct 2026, 21:45 Oslo)

Words only, from the R19 reviews and round 20's cold read; checked mechanically, not with the scoreboard.

1. **Names.** A town of eighty has several people with the same first name, and the record named everyone by first name only ('Signe put in more than anyone when the hat went round for Signe'). The page and the reports now give the surname when someone else living has the same first name, and the year of birth when they share both.
2. **What people remember of each other** is written in the first person and reads: 'told people what I had told her in confidence', 'let people think the best of my song was his', 'made sure I knew the cost'. These lines appear as reasons for later acts and break-ups.
3. **The page-evidence reader is renamed the page-annotation reader.** It reads the evidence tags on events whose text is shown, not the sentences; its score says what the page's annotations carry, not what a human reading the page would learn.
   Check: two worlds with and without the new words (`OFF=oldwords`, `src/diag/samewords.ts`) have the same events, people, evidence and choices. **Kept.**

## Round 21 — consistency audit (pre-registered 17 Oct 2026, 21:05 Oslo, before the audit is written)

The second R19 review found one-sided friendships after migration (fixed in 19d) and asked for invariant tests across every lifecycle transition before more social complexity. Round 20b will add break-ups that move people, so the audit comes first. No new mechanism.
Invariants, checked every month (`src/audit.ts`, kinds counted separately):

1. **Partners:** a living person's partner exists, is not dead, and names them back with the same status; nobody under 16 has a partner; `psince` is not in the future.
2. **Family:** parents and children list each other.
3. **Friends:** symmetric (from 19d); nobody counts themself or a dead person as a friend.
4. **Work:** nobody away or dead holds a job; at most one head per place, and the head works there; nobody under 14 works.
5. **Rivalries:** none open with a dead party, or with a party away for more than a year.
6. **Plans:** none still gathering with an owner dead or away.
7. **Groups:** no dead or away member counted as a member; a person's `group` matches the group's member list.
8. **Wants:** no open want aimed at a person who is dead.
   Discovery runs on used diagnosis worlds 1871–1874 (past and present). Each kind of violation found gets a fix behind `OFF=hyg21`, said in the outcome with its count before and after. Expected: some violations in 4–8 (they have never been checked); none in 1–3 beyond the 19d fix.
   Confirmation: zero violations of every kind on twenty fresh worlds 2501–2520 over the ten present years; the audit joins `npm test`. Gate: the scoreboard paired on 1561–1570 with and without the fixes within ±2 overall (key slice reported). What would change our minds: violations that the fixes cannot remove without changing what people decide mean the state model itself is wrong (two records of one fact), and the fix is to keep the fact in one place.

## Round 22 — the consequence map (pre-registered 17 Oct 2026, 21:15 Oslo, before its script is run)

Measurement only. Both R19 reviews and the designer's consequence rule say consequences stop too early; this measures it, per kind of event, so the work after it can be ordered by evidence. Diagnosis from round 20, to be tested here: most state in the model reverts toward what traits set (liking toward chemistry, tension decays, marks fade), and things happen when slow state crosses a threshold, so history washes out unless an event changes something that does not revert.
Definitions (`src/consequences.ts`): a **weighty** event has weight ≥ 0.8 and is not texture (everyday lines, notices, habits, tells, illness). Its **descendants** are later events, within three years, that cite it as a cause, directly or through a chain (a memory cited as a reason carries the event it came from). A **turn** is a descendant that changes something that does not revert by itself: work (hired, let go, walked out, promoted, retired, stepped down), home (left town, came back), partners (started seeing, married, split, divorced), friendship (made, ended, mended), groups (formed, joined, left), drink (started, stopped), money and goods (bought, lost), pursuits (taken up, given up, achieved, failed, a regret), rivalries (begun, ended), plans (opened, failed, refused). This counts traceable consequences only: what follows because a feeling moved, without the record citing the event, is not counted. That limit is the point for a readable simulation: a consequence the record cannot trace is one the player cannot either.
Run: twenty fresh worlds 2521–2540, sources in the first seven present years (three years of follow-up for each).
Expected (guesses, said before the run): weighty events with any weighty descendant **20–40%**; with a turn **5–20%**; median chain depth 1; descendants reaching people outside the source event in under a third.
Decision rule, fixed now: a kind of event with at least 30 sources, a turn share under 5% and a weighty-descendant share under 15% is a **dead end**. The consequence audit takes dead ends in order of how often they happen. What would change our minds: a turn share over 30% means consequences do not stop early in the record; the problem is that nothing surfaces them, and story sifting comes before new mechanism.

Amendment to round 20 (17 Oct 2026, 21:30 Oslo), from the R20 external review, checked in the code: a couple's running tension is kept on the partner with the lower id, but `row()` wrote what a row left of it onto whoever raised it, so when the higher-id partner raised a row, the tension the couple actually carries was never lowered. About half of all rows did not relieve anything. Round 20's numbers are affected: rows were inflated for those couples (so 'too rare' holds more strongly), and the divorce and contempt figures are not clean. The fix (one state per couple, written only through it) goes into both arms of round 20b, whose off arm is round 20 corrected; an invariant checks that no couple state is kept anywhere else.

Outcome of round 21 (17 Oct 2026, 21:20 Oslo) (`audit-r21-before.txt`, `audit-r21-after.txt`, `audit-r21-fresh-*.txt`, `pair-r21.txt`):

- Discovery on used worlds 1871–1874: one kind of violation only. People kept an open want to make things right with someone who had died (4 at the end of the past; 668 person-months in the present), and dating partners kept wanting to win someone who had died (found by the invariant on world 33). Partners, family, friends, work, heads of places, rivalries, plans and groups: none. The two R19 reviews' wider worry about contradictory relationship state does not hold beyond the friendship bug fixed in 19d.
- The fix (`OFF=hyg21` restores the old behaviour): when someone dies, wanting to win them ends, and wanting to make things right with them becomes a regret on the record ('Rolf never made things right with Turid. He had been meaning to for six years.'), seen locally, felt for a year.
- Confirmation on twenty fresh worlds 2501–2520, ten present years: **zero** violations of every kind ✓. The audit runs in `npm test` and fails at once with the fix switched off.
- Gate: scoreboard paired on 1561–1570, overall **+0.2 ±1.4** (within ±2), key slice −0.3 ±1.5 ✓.
  **Kept.**

Outcome of round 22 (17 Oct 2026, 21:25 Oslo), twenty fresh worlds 2521–2540, 14,077 weighty sources (`consequences-r22.txt`):

- Weighty events with any weighty descendant within three years: **19%** ±1 (✗ by a point, guessed 20–40%). With a turn: **7%** ±1 (✓, 5–20%). Chain depth: median **0** (✗, guessed 1), 90th percentile 1, longest 6. Descendants reaching anyone outside the source event: **6%** (✓, under a third).
- So four in five weighty events lead to nothing the record can trace, and thirteen in fourteen change nothing that does not revert. The town rarely answers: what happens between two people stays between them.
- Where consequences live: drama (55% have a weighty descendant, 29% a turn), coming and going (50%, 15%), apologies and peace-making (nearly all lead to a mended friendship), promotions (58%), deaths (68%, 42%), marriages (46%). Dead ends by the pre-registered rule, most frequent first: becoming friends (1,064 sources, 4%), friendships ending (488, 0%), pursuits started and wants achieved or failed (483, 399, 48: 0–3%), the hat going round (chipin, token, nowallet, quietgive: 818, 1%), humour (434, 0%), visits and dropping everything for someone (379, 276: 0–1%), sulking (304), owning up (312), disliking (210), groups (504, 1%), plans (351, 5%; a plan that fails changes nothing), the couple patterns (116, 0%), stonewalling and defending (0%), retiring (175, 0%), getting sober (59, 0%).
- Two kinds of dead end, to be told apart in the consequence round: those where something does change but the record does not cite it (becoming friends raises liking and later help, but later acts cite 'how she feels about him', not the friendship; a want records no event it came from, so the 7% of weighty events that give a want as a reason end their chain there), and those where nothing changes (humour, a pattern line, an achieved want, a failed plan, sobriety).
  **Kept** as a measure (`src/consequences.ts`). It orders the work: round 20b (couples), then a consequence round that first makes the record cite where things came from (wants, bonds) and then connects the most frequent true dead ends, then story sifting over chains that can now be followed.

Words only, at the designer's request: the placeholder setting is a town, not a real place, and will not outlive the model. Its name is now simply 'the town'; people have English names; the workplaces are the sawmill, the harbour fleet, the garage, the clinic, the school, the Courier, the Anchor and the council office; the few local specifics (foods, a national day, a racecourse, a currency) are neutral. Every place the engine wrote the town's name into a sentence now takes it from the setting, so a different setting pack renames everything. Names map one to one onto the old ones, so who is called what changes and nothing else does.

From now on no target or benchmark comes from the placeholder setting or any one country: a setting pack may carry its own, and the engine's own checks are relative effects against ranges seen across many societies.

## Round 20b — couples, mended (pre-registered 17 Oct 2026, 21:35 Oslo, before any test run)

Round 20 left couples fighting too rarely and parting too seldom, and a bug (amendment above) made half of all rows relieve nothing. Round 22 found the couples' part of the model leads to a turn in 6% of cases. The R20 review, checked and agreed: ordinary disagreement is not destructive conflict; troubled couples can stop arguing because they have given up; ending a relationship is a decision; couples learn how their rows go.
What people are like: almost all couples disagree, and what predicts their end is how (Gottman and Levenson 1992); there are two routes to divorce, early, with hostile rows, and later, with little open conflict and little warmth left (Gottman and Levenson 2000); a long history of how things go between two people colours how each hears the other (sentiment override: Weiss 1980); staying is commitment, which weighs satisfaction against alternatives and what has been put in, children and money among it (Rusbult 1980; Le and Agnew 2003).
Fixes, in both arms:

- F1. One state per couple, kept on the lower-id partner and written only through it (`coupleOf`); invariant in `npm test`.
- F2. Attachment anxiety and avoidance share a root of general insecurity (0.7 of a person's own standard draw, raised by losing a parent young or the parents parting), which is how they come to correlate slightly positively in adults (about +0.1 to +0.3). Set on training worlds 1501–1505 before this was written: corr +0.15 there; cuts recomputed by the same rule (0.75 SD over the mean: anxiety 1.27, avoidance 0.90; 59% secure there).
- F3. A situation's own considerations were silently cut when the trait wiring already filled the five places (in round 20, the tension a row had built up never reached the choice to let it go). Couples' decisions now put their own considerations first (`ownFirst`).
  The change (switches in brackets; `r20` = all four off, which is round 20 corrected):

1. Ordinary disagreement (`friction`): every couple has a row with chance 0.05 a month plus 0.25 × tension, with no bar; what it becomes is up to how each handles it.
2. Couples learn (`couplelearn`): how their rows have gone lately (heard or laughed off; or silence, hitting back, defending, a joke that fell flat), fading by half in about three years. Rows that usually go well make a gentle start likelier; rows that never go anywhere make letting it go likelier; a bad history, or contempt in the last year, makes the answer hear an attack.
3. Drifting apart (`disengage`): letting it go and going silent move a couple apart (0.08), defending a little (0.04); a row that goes well brings them closer (0.1), and so does care (0.02); it fades slowly (1% a month). Care comes less often as they drift; once far apart (0.6), the people around them see it.
4. Ending as a decision (`leaving`): once a year, a married partner who no longer likes the other much (below 0.3), has drifted far (above 0.5) or has been shown contempt in the last year decides whether to stay: against liking, distance, contempt, their own wish for freedom and their fear of being alone stand the children, family and tradition, the years together and whether they could afford to start again. Leaving ends the marriage, with that decision as its reason. The old rule (liking below zero) stays for dating couples only.
   Test: forty fresh worlds 2541–2580 under on, `r20` and without learning (`couplelearn`) (`src/lovetest.ts`); lives on twenty fresh worlds 2581–2600, on against `r20` (`src/story.ts`); the consequence map on twenty fresh worlds 2601–2620 (`src/consequences.ts`). Expected, with everything on:
5. Rows per couple-year: median **0.5 to 3**; top tenth at least **twice** the median; couples together a year or more with no row in the decade **at most 20%**.
6. Divorce per married couple-year **0.5–2%** (the range across Western societies; the placeholder town sets no target of its own).
7. Two routes: among divorces, those after under seven years of marriage and the quiet ones (drifted apart, at most one row in the two years before) **each 20–80%**.
8. Learning: rows raised gently when a couple's rows have mostly gone well exceed those raised gently when mostly badly by **at least 10 points**; without learning the gap is **under 5 points**.
9. Attachment: secure 50–65%; corr(anxiety, avoidance) **0.0 to +0.3**; parents parted before sixteen: more anxious by 0.15–0.6.
10. Downstream, against `r20`: single at 25+ not up by more than 4 points; births within 10%.
11. Lives: dead lives and repeats not up by more than 2 points (both measures).
12. The consequence map: couples' events leading to a turn **at least 10%** (6% in round 22).
    Gate, set now because round 20 showed that new kinds of decision change the mix: the scoreboard paired on 1571–1580, on against `r20`, within ±2 on every decision that is not a couple's (rows, answers, staying: `EXCLUDE=row,row-answer,stay`); overall reported beside it; and on couples' decisions the calibrated witness beats the base rate in both arms. `npm test` passes.
    What would change our minds: rows in range but divorce still under 0.5% means what holds people together (children, years, money) outweighs everything the decision can weigh, and the weights of commitment are wrong; divorce over 2% means ordinary friction is being read as crisis; no learning gap means a couple's history is too weak a consideration to matter against traits; no quiet endings means distance never builds, and disengagement is decoration.

Outcome of round 20b (17 Oct 2026, 22:05 Oslo), forty fresh worlds 2541–2580 under on, `r20` and without learning (`love-r20b.txt`), lives on 2581–2600 (`story-r20b.txt`), the consequence map on 2601–2620 (`consequences-r20b.txt`), scoreboard on 1571–1580 (`pair-r20b*.txt`):

1. Rows per couple-year: median **0.94** ✓ (0.5–3; round 20 corrected: 0); top tenth 2.47, 2.6 times the median ✓; couples with no row in the decade **4%** ✓ (at most 20%; 52% in `r20`).
2. Divorce per married couple-year **7.6%** [6.6, 8.4] (**✗**, 0.5–2%; `r20` 0.35%; without learning 5.1%). Dating splits 6.3% against 3.7%.
3. Two routes: 74% of divorces came under seven years of marriage and 25% were quiet endings ✓ (each 20–80%).
4. Learning: rows raised gently 49% when a couple's rows have mostly gone well against 23% when mostly badly, a gap of **26 points** ✓ (at least 10). The control (under 5 points without learning) cannot be read: without learning no history is kept to split by, which the pre-registration missed. Learning's effect shows in the ablation instead: without it the divorce rate is 0.68 of the rate with it [0.56, 0.82], so learning deepens the spirals it was meant to deepen.
5. Attachment: secure 61% ✓; corr(anxiety, avoidance) **+0.24** ✓ (0 to +0.3; −0.14 in round 20); parents parted before sixteen +0.59 ±0.11 more anxious ✓ (at the edge of 0.15–0.6; 525 such adults now, from the many more divorces).
6. Downstream, against `r20`: single at 25+ **+9.8 ±3.1** points (**✗**, at most 4); births +0.3 ±1.1 ✓.
7. Lives: dead lives 5% against 7% ✓; repeats 24% against 23% ✓ (the round-19d measures 2%/1% and 33%/33% ✓). Arcs fell (adults with an arc, round-19d measure, 10% against 13%).
8. The consequence map: couples' events leading to a turn **6%** (**✗**, at least 10%). The staying decisions, which the map files on their own, lead to a turn every time (they are where divorces come from); counted with the couples, about 10%.
   Gate: (a) on every decision that is not a couple's, paired on 1571–1580, **−1.3 ±1.7** ✓; (b) overall −4.9 ±2.0, the mix again (25% more decisions, most of them rows and answers); (c) the calibrated witness beats the base rate on rows (46% against 38%) and answers (45% against 35%) in both arms ✓, but **not on staying** (61% against 61%; the mind-reader 68%) **✗**.
   Read cold (test world 2545, before the numbers): Ellie and Ken fall into the classic pursue-and-withdraw for years, and by 2033 his reason reads 'everything Ellie said sounded like an attack by now'; Victor tells Karen it is over just after a run of rows that went well, with one reason given; decisions to stay are invisible; the hat going round still floods each illness with near-identical lines.
   Diagnosis, after the result, on used worlds 1871–1873 (`diag-stay-r20b.txt`): married partners like each other a great deal (median 0.89; under 0.3 in 2%), yet the decision to stay ends the marriage in about a third of cases **whatever the reason it was faced for**: 33% when liking alone was low, 40% when drifted apart, 36% after contempt, 30% with children at home, 48% without, 36% with liking of 0.15 or more. The decision barely responds to what it weighs, so whoever is asked leaves a third of the time, and drifting apart (from letting things go, which a third of rows now are) puts many couples in front of it every year. Nothing in it says what the research says first: most people who think about leaving do not leave (in a national sample of married adults, 25% had thought about divorce in the past six months, about half of them only softly, and the thoughts came and went within a year: Hawkins et al. 2017). Leaving is the end of a process, and staying is the default it has to overcome.
   **Not kept as it stands.** The fixes (one couple state, the shared root of attachment, a situation's own considerations first) are kept. Ordinary disagreement, learning and drifting apart worked as predicted; ending as a decision did not. Round 20c makes leaving a process.

## Round 20c — leaving is a process (pre-registered 17 Oct 2026, 22:15 Oslo, before any of its code)

From round 20b's diagnosis: the yearly decision to stay ended a third of the marriages it was faced in, whatever it was faced for. What people are like: thinking about leaving is common and comes and goes (25% of married adults in the past six months, half of them softly, many no longer a year later: Hawkins et al. 2017); leaving comes at the end of a long stretch of it (the distance-and-isolation cascade: Gottman 1994); and staying is the default leaving has to overcome, weighted by what has been built (Rusbult 1980, investment; status quo bias: Samuelson and Zeckhauser 1988).
The change (`OFF=doubts` restores round 20b's yearly decision):

1. Doubts as a slow state per married partner, toward the other: each month they move toward what the marriage gives them reason to doubt (liking below 0.4, drifting apart, contempt in the last year, rows going badly rather than well; doubt += 0.05 × that, fading 3% a month, so a passing bad patch passes and a long one builds).
2. Above 0.3 they are thinking about it, and the person's closest friend can know ('had begun to wonder whether to stay with …'). Only after a year above 0.5 do they face the decision, once a year; doubt is then its first reason to leave, and 'the life they had built' its first reason to stay. Deciding to stay is now seen by those close ('decided to stay with … and give it another go'), and it eases the doubt (by 40%).
3. Everything else as in round 20b (ordinary disagreement, learning, drifting apart, the fixes).
   Test: forty fresh worlds 2621–2660 on against `r20` (round 20 corrected), `src/lovetest.ts`; lives on twenty fresh worlds 2661–2680; scoreboard on 1581–1590. Expected:
4. Divorce per married couple-year **0.5–2%**.
5. Married people thinking about leaving at a given time (doubt above 0.3) **10–35%**; of those thinking about it, **20–60%** no longer are a year later.
6. Rows as in 20b (median 0.5–3, at most 20% without a row in the decade); two routes, each 20–80% of divorces.
7. Single at 25+ not up by more than 4 points against `r20`; births within 10%.
8. On staying decisions the calibrated witness beats the base rate by at least 3 points.
9. Lives: dead lives and repeats not up by more than 2 points (both measures).
   Gate: as in 20b (paired on 1581–1590 within ±2 on every decision that is not a couple's; overall reported; couples' decisions above the base rate).
   What would change our minds: divorce still over 2% means even persistent doubt is acted on too easily, and the decision itself, not who faces it, is the fault; under 0.5% means doubts rarely last a year, which says the marriages in the model are happier than real ones, not that the process is wrong; thinking about it far below 10% means the dissatisfaction terms are too weak to register a bad patch at all.
   Correction to round 20b's measure (found while writing 20c, before any 20c run): `lovetest.ts` counted each decision to stay as a row of that couple. That added about 900 to some 10,000 rows over forty worlds; the median of 0.94 rows a couple-year is slightly high, the verdict unchanged. Fixed for 20c.

Outcome of round 20c (17 Oct 2026, 22:35 Oslo), forty fresh worlds 2621–2660 on against `r20` (`love-r20c.txt`), lives on 2661–2680 (`story-r20c.txt`), scoreboard on 1581–1590 (`pair-r20c*.txt`):

1. Divorce per married couple-year **1.05%** [0.85, 1.31] ✓ (0.5–2%; `r20` 0.46%). Dating splits 7.2% a year.
2. Married partners thinking about leaving at a yearly look **12%** ✓ (10–35%); of those thinking about it, no longer a year later **7%** (**✗**, 20–60%): in the model a bad patch, once it has become doubt, rarely clears; in people it often does.
3. Rows: median 0.75 a couple-year ✓, 5% of couples without one ✓. Of divorces with a known wedding, 8 of 16 came under seven years ✓; quiet endings **9 of 62, 15%** (**✗**, 20–80%).
4. Single at 25+ +2.1 ±2.4 points ✓; births −0.7 ±1.2 a world (−8%) ✓.
5. Staying decisions: 67 on the board's worlds; every reader, the mind-reader included, is right 82% by always saying 'stays' (**✗**, the witness was to beat the base rate by 3). Who leaves is decided by who comes to the decision, through a year of doubt, which character drives; the decision itself then adds only noise.
6. Lives: dead lives 4% against 6% ✓, repeats 23% both ✓ (round-19d measures 1%/2%, 33%/33% ✓).
   Gate: on every decision that is not a couple's, paired on 1581–1590, **+1.3 ±1.7** ✓; overall −4.0 ±1.5 (the mix: 22% more decisions, rows and answers); on rows and answers the calibrated witness beats the base rate in both arms ✓ (45% against 35%, 43% against 40%; without, 57/43 and 47/31). `npm test` passes.
   Read cold (test world 2625, before the numbers): Arthur and Tilly drift into politeness after years of Tilly going silent whenever he raised anything, and the town sees it (2031); Sean, fearful, pursues; Libby, avoidant, hears every gentle word as an accusation, which the town also knows. No marriage among the five lives or three couples read ends; nobody there reaches doubt. The names are heavy: 'Libby Vaughan (b. 1992)' appears on every line, because two living women share both names. The cause is that each sex has forty first names for about fifty living people; the page tells them apart correctly, but generation should not make so many namesakes.
   **Kept**, with three of its predictions failed (doubts too sticky, too few quiet endings, the decision itself unreadable). Couples now fight about as often as couples do, learn from it, drift, think about leaving and sometimes leave, at a plausible rate, with the town seeing much of it. Round 20b's ordinary disagreement, learning and drifting apart are kept with it.

## Round 23a — the record says where things came from (pre-registered 17 Oct 2026, 22:55 Oslo, before its code)

The consequence round, first half, agreed with the designer. Round 22 could trace only what events cite, and two common links were missing from the record: a want does not say what event it came from (6–7% of weighty events give a want as a reason, and their chain stops there), and a reason given as 'how she feels about him' does not say what made her feel it. What is untraceable to the record is untraceable to the player. This round changes no decision: it adds the missing links to the record.

1. A want remembers the event it came from where there is one: wanting to win someone (from starting to see them), to have a child (from marrying), to make things right (from the quarrel or the break-up that ended it), to be given a post (from its succession being announced). Wants that come from character alone (getting on, getting out, belonging, a thing to save for, proving oneself) have no event, and say so by having none. A reason that is a want cites the want's event.
2. A reason that is a feeling about someone cites the memory that weighs most in it (the one people bring up when that name comes up), when that memory is of an event.
   Switch: `OFF=nocite`. Check: the same events, people, decisions, expressions and evidence with and without (`src/diag/sig.ts`, seeds 11–13).
   Then the consequence map on twenty fresh worlds 2681–2700, with and without the links (the same worlds, since behaviour is identical). Expected: weighty events with a weighty descendant **from about 20% to 25–40%**; with a turn **from 7% to 8–12%**; becoming friends no longer a dead end; achieving a want still one (nothing follows it, which round 23b addresses). What would change our minds: a rise under 3 points means the missing links were not where the consequences were, and the dead ends are real almost everywhere.

Outcome of round 23a (17 Oct 2026, about 22:50 Oslo), twenty fresh worlds 2681–2700 with and without (`consequences-r23a*.txt`): behaviour identical with and without (seeds 11–13, `sig.ts`) ✓. Weighty events with a weighty descendant **22%** against 20% without (**✗**, expected 25–40%); with a turn 8% against 7%. Friendships that end are no longer a dead end (40% lead somewhere, 27% to a turn: wanting to make it right, and mending it), and visits are less of one (11% from 1%). Becoming friends is still a dead end (3%), and so are pursuits, wants achieved, the hat going round, humour, sulking, taking something up and groups' shows.
Read: the rise is under 3 points, so by the pre-registered rule the missing links were not where the consequences were. The dead ends are real: those events change little that anything later depends on.
**Kept** (the record is more complete, and nothing else changed).

## Round 23b — what follows (pre-registered 17 Oct 2026, about 22:50 Oslo, before its code)

The consequence round, second half. Three of the most frequent real dead ends, each given a consequence that research says people have, each behind its own switch:

1. **What you gave comes back** (`OFF=reciprocity`). When the hat goes round for someone, whether they had put in for you, or slipped you something, weighs on what you put in for them; so does having been fobbed off with the smallest coin or a forgotten wallet. The norm of reciprocity is among the most general there are (Gouldner 1960), and gratitude binds people to those who helped them (Algoe 2012). The reason cites the earlier act.
2. **A joke at your expense is answered** (`OFF=jokeback`). A cutting joke about someone, already remembered by its target, becomes a situation for them, as every other hostile act in the model already does: hit back, let it go, or go cold. Aggressive humour costs relationships (Martin et al. 2003).
3. **A plan that fails costs its backers** (`OFF=planloss`). People who put money into a venture that comes to nothing lose it on the record ('… lost the money they had put into the café'), trust the owner less for it, and carry the strain; trust is part of regard, so the owner finds the next ask harder.
   Test: the consequence map on twenty fresh worlds 2701–2720, on against all three off; the reciprocity check from the same runs; scoreboard paired on 1591–1600 within ±2 overall (few new decisions: answers to jokes); `npm test`. Expected:
4. The hat going round (chipin, quietgive, token, nowallet together): sources with a weighty descendant **at least 5%** (from 1–2%).
5. Humour: **at least 8%** (from 3%; only cutting jokes, about a quarter of humour moments, have a target).
6. Failed plans: **at least 25%** with a weighty descendant and **at least 15%** with a turn (from 5% and 0%).
7. Overall weighty descendant share up by **1 to 3 points** (these kinds are about a tenth of all sources).
8. Reciprocity shows in choices: among collection decisions, putting in generously is at least **10 points** likelier when the one in need had put in for the giver before than when they had not, and at least 10 points less likely when they had been stingy (measured with it on; the off arm shows the gap without it, from liking alone).
   What would change our minds: 1 and 2 met but 4 not means these are real but small, and the dead ends that matter most are elsewhere (becoming friends, pursuits, wants achieved), which points at people who steer rather than more consequences of one-off acts.

## Round H24 — housekeeping, no change to behaviour (pre-registered 17 Oct 2026, 23:08 Oslo, before its code)

A note on times: the times on rounds 20c to 23b were written from an estimate of the clock and ran ahead of it by up to about half an hour (two were corrected here). Their order is right: each pre-registration was written before its code and its runs. From here on, times come from the system clock.

Øystein asked whether the code and the process could be better; they can. Before round 23b runs, one round that changes how the engine is written and how fast it runs, and nothing it does. The exit criterion in STATUS.md is now agreed, and a round runs as STATUS.md says (a smoke run, an independent re-derivation of the headline numbers, blind cold reads).
The check is new: `src/diag/ident.ts` hashes the whole world on fixed seeds — every event with its words, reasons, decision and who knew it, every person, bond, memory and belief, every group and the state of every random stream — for the default on seeds 11–13 and every surviving switch on seed 11, eight years each, against the round 23a code (`reports/ident-a.json`, `ident-b.json`).
Steps, each checked before the next:

1. **Faster:** exact rewrites of the hottest paths found by profiling (belief look-ups, contact, memory weights). Expected: a world (thirty years of past and ten of present) at least **1.5 times** faster on seed 9101 (11.6 s on round 23a code).
2. **Readable:** the engine laid out by a formatter (prettier, its version and settings recorded), so a reason or a consideration has a line of its own instead of being part of a 600-character one.
3. **One place per act:** whether an act needs a named counterpart, and whether it appears only where a situation names it, move from two hand-kept lists in the grammar onto the act's own definition.
4. **Retire switches for settled rounds** (their old path is removed, the default is untouched; the old rounds stay reproducible from their archives): friendbug, friendcap, onepool, hyg19d, hyg21, oldwords, lovefix, nocite, recent, doubts (round 20b's leaving), forget, latentall, shownews, teacher with the round-12 teacher mechanism (never in the default model), and the FRIENDFADE flag. One-off scripts that only exercised those (`interests.ts`, `music.ts`, `teachers.ts` and old `diag/` scripts) go with them. Kept, because each is a real ablation of a mechanism in the model: love, contempt, friction, couplelearn, disengage, leaving, drama, projects, persona, townquirks, marks, groups, habits, threads, moments, pursuits, regard, regard:beliefs, plasticity, manner, mannernoise, ifthens, bias, attach, school, household, everyday, latent, exposure, budget, relbar, respond, selfstory.
5. **ARCHITECTURE.md:** the monthly order of the systems, where state lives, and how to add an act, a situation or a system.
   What would change our minds: any difference in the identity check means the step changed behaviour; it is undone and examined, never accepted as it is.
   Not in this round: typed per-person state (about 120 uses of the `tried` store with keys made of text), named in ARCHITECTURE.md as known debt and done when a round touches it. And a question found while reading the code, which is about behaviour, so it would go through a round of its own: some situations put a circumstance (short of money, already stretched, needs the money, people depend on them) in `extra`, which every option gets alike, so it may steer only through which considerations are cut at five rather than by meaning.

Amendment to round 23b (17 Oct 2026, 23:45 Oslo, before its code and before any run). Reading the code and the 23a map showed that part 3 would almost never fire: in the twenty 23a worlds money was put into a plan twice, while 29 plans failed, most because the council said no. So part 3 becomes: 3. **A plan turned down is answered** (`OFF=turneddown`). When the council says no, or a plan fails for want of money or helpers, its owner decides how to take it, a decision like any other: hold it against the one who said no (a grudge with the council's chief officer, carried by the rivalry system), mean to bring it back (a want to try again, and after two years they may), or let it go. It is weighed from forgiveness, pride, diligence, how they regard the one who said no, and how long they had been at it. People who meet failure differently, by blaming others or by learning and going again, is what the research on life after business failure describes (Ucbasaran, Shepherd, Lockett and Lyon 2013). Money put in by backers is lost, on the record, and costs the owner their trust.
And a fix that the 23a map points at: when a plan ends, the want it fulfilled or ended now cites the event that ended it (it cited whatever event happened to be last in the record). Record only.
Expectation 3 becomes: plan:failed with a weighty descendant **at least 40%**, with a turn **at least 25%** (from 7% and 0%); and among the owners' answers, holding it against the council is likelier for the unforgiving (lowest third against highest third of forgiveness) by **at least 15 points**. Expectation 4 (overall weighty descendant share up 1 to 3 points) stands. The rest stands as written.
Before the test worlds, a smoke run on two throwaway worlds (9001–9002) checks only that each part fires and nothing runs away.

Finding, 17 Oct 2026, 23:52 Oslo (diagnosis on throwaway worlds 9001–9003, a copy of the engine counting what `run` keeps; no change to the model). A decision keeps the first five considerations of each option. A situation's own considerations are rarely cut: 3% of options that have them lose one, and none in the hat going round (so the guess in H24's pre-registration, that the money worry there might act only through what is cut, is wrong for that situation; the money worry still weighs on both options alike). Where they are cut: an answer to a request (90% of options lose how long ago they last helped), a job offer in the city (half lose attachment to home and the value the person wants it for), a chance in the spotlight (half lose social self-esteem), authorship and rivalries (a quarter). And the self-story, which the design says every decision weighs, is cut from **44%** of the options it applies to, because it is added last. This is behaviour, so it goes through a round of its own (with the shared-circumstance question), not into a cleanup.

Second amendment to round 23b (17 Oct 2026, 23:56 Oslo, before its code and before any test run). The smoke run (two throwaway worlds, 9001–9002) printed "Andy put in more than anyone when the hat went round for Grace — because … Grace had given next to nothing for Andy": a reason that argued against the choice, given as if it were a reason for it. It is not new: a decision's reasons mark a consideration that argued against the act with 'despite' only when it is a trait; a circumstance, a bond or a value is printed bare. In one throwaway world, 92 of 1448 decisions in the present carried such a reason ("Ken started on Alan about it — it was put gently"). Added to the round, as a change to words only (`OFF=despite`, checked mechanically with `sig.ts`): every reason that argued against the choice says so ('despite: it was put gently'). The smoke run otherwise showed each part firing and nothing running away (numbers in the outcome).

Outcome of round H24 (18 Oct 2026, 00:05 Oslo). **Behaviour identical** ✓: against the round 23a code, every event with its words and reasons, every person, bond, memory and belief, every group and random stream is the same on seeds 11–13 and for each of the 33 kept switches on seed 11 (`ident.ts`; the baseline was rebuilt once so that it ignores the retired `pupils` field, always empty, which persons in the pending queue still carried). `npm test` passes. A separate agent, given only the old code, the new code and the diff, looked for anything that could change behaviour and found nothing; its one caveat (two test tools move the clock by hand, which would break reading recent history from the end) does not bite: one only reads while the clock is shifted, the other only moves it forward. Steps, in the order done (the layout went first, so the other edits were made on readable code):

1. **Readable** ✓: the engine laid out by prettier 3.3.3 (`.prettierrc.json`: width 150, single quotes): 7,374 lines become 19,695; 160 lines still pass 150 characters, all of them long sentences of text. Tools and diagnostics moved out of the engine into `src/tools/` and `src/tools/diag/`.
2. **One place per act** ✓: `named`, `needsTarget` and `onlyIf` on each act's definition; the two hand-kept lists and three special cases are gone. Moving them showed how fragile they were: the first pass of the script that moved them missed ten acts whose definitions began differently (caught by counting, before the identity check, which would have caught it too).
3. **Fourteen settled switches retired** ✓ with the round-12 teacher mechanism (its switch arm was identical to the default, which shows it never ran) and six one-off scripts.
4. **Faster** **✗** on the pre-registered 1.5 times: a world (thirty years of past and ten of present, seed 9101) takes 11.8 s on round 23a code, 10.5 s now under `tsx` (1.12 times) and 8.9 s run from a pre-built bundle with `./run.sh` (1.33 times). The time left is spread over many systems (contact a quarter, regard, persona, situations, groups and couples each 5–9%); a larger gain needs the structural changes of the scale branch, not more rewrites of hot spots.
5. **ARCHITECTURE.md** ✓.
   Read: the code is easier and safer to change, and a little faster, and any change that should not alter behaviour can now be checked in minutes. **Kept.**

## The page, read cold (18 Oct 2026, 00:27 Oslo)

The blind reader of round 23b scored twelve random lives 2.6 out of five on average (1 to 4; best: a songwriter whose walking out of the band follows a chain of credit taken, and a widow whose last year carries "never made things right with Sophie. She had been meaning to for 13 years"). What works, in the reader's words: "the chained, cumulative stuff … each works because one event references an earlier one and the stakes build. Most other lines are isolated draws." What breaks it, checked against the code where it could be:

1. **Reasons that are trait dumps, not motives** (the largest). A trait near the middle is printed as a bare name ('forgiveness', 'flexibility'); some reasons contradict the act ('diligence: does the minimum' for seventeen years of saving). Partly fixed in 23b (reasons against a choice are marked as such); the rest needs a pass over how reasons are chosen and worded.
2. **Templates that wear out**: "it was 'you always' and 'you never' from there" about twenty-five times in twelve lives; the gentle start and the defensive answer likewise.
3. **Lines that assert a history the page does not show** ("It was not the first time", "Years on, nothing had changed"): counted correctly from acts before the present or below the page's weight, but unexplained to a reader who arrives with the witness.
4. **Identity**: namesakes the reader takes for one person (two Sally Hales; "(b. 1997)" on every mention), children who take either parent's surname by chance (70% the partner with the lower id), with nothing on the page that says so, a spouse treated as a workplace rival, generic promises ("look after the kids" for someone with none).
5. **Events with no consequence**: a stroke, a birth, a betrayal followed by friendship whose reason ignores the betrayal.
   These are the exit criterion's points 3 and 4, and they go into a round of their own.

## Round 24a — how it is told, words only (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 00:41 Oslo, **after** its code)

A breach of the procedure, said first: the code of this round was written before this entry. No check or measurement of it has been run; the expectations below are written before any. Every part changes only words, so it is checked mechanically (`sig.ts`, the record's behaviour without its text, against the round 23b code) rather than gated on the scoreboard.
What it changes, from the blind cold read of round 23b:

1. **Reasons.** A trait near the middle is never written down as a reason (in three throwaway worlds, 12.6% of all reasons were a bare 'patience' or 'altruism'). The 35 reasons written by hand for events (being found out, getting somewhere, being hurt at sea) say which way the trait points, so a person who got there despite doing the minimum reads 'despite diligence: does the minimum'.
2. **History said with its year.** "It was not the first time" becomes "It had happened before, in 2029" when the earlier time was in another year; a couple's pattern said again becomes "Nothing had changed since 2027: …".
3. **Variety.** The commonest sentences (becoming friends, losing touch, can't stand, the hat, coming round, dropping everything, not being there, sulking, saying nothing, confiding, going cold, and the starts and answers of a row) have two to four wordings, chosen by who, what and when (a hash, no random draw). Memories keep the first wording.
4. **Lines that fit.** What is promised fits the one it is promised to (the kids only to someone with young children); a public joke says where it was made inside the sentence.
5. **Quirks claim no incident** (exit criterion 3): the 34 patterns that described a one-off past act with a named person, money or a town event not on the record ("bet Hanne a week's wages on the ski jumping and lost") now describe the disposition instead ("bets more on the ski jumping than his wages can stand").
   Test: `sig.ts` on seeds 11–13 identical to round 23b; `npm test`; on worlds 2721–2724 (fresh), counted on the new code against the round 23b code: bare trait reasons, the share of shown lines taken by the single commonest wording of each act, history lines without a year, quirks claiming an incident. Then the blind cold read: 24 random lives from those worlds, each drawn once and shown either in the old words or the new (12 each, assigned at random), shuffled, read by a new reader who is not told there are two versions, with the same questions as in round 23b.
   Expected:
6. Behaviour identical (`sig.ts`).
7. Bare trait reasons **0** (from about 12%).
8. Among the acts given variants, the commonest wording's share of that act's lines **at most 45%** (from 100%).
9. Quirk patterns claiming an incident not on the record: **0** of about 130 (from 34).
10. Blind read: the new words score **at least 0.5 higher** out of five than the old (mean over twelve lives each), and the reader can say who the person is in one sentence for **at least 9 of 12** with the new words.
    What would change our minds: if 1 to 4 hold and 5 does not, words are not what makes the lives hollow; what the reader called "isolated draws" is in the events themselves, which points at consequences and people who steer, not at the page.

## Round 24b — what a decision weighs (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 00:57 Oslo, before its code)

Two faults in how decisions are made, both found by diagnosis and confirmed by the external review of round 23b:

1. **The self-story is always weighed** (`OFF=selfkept`). A decision keeps five considerations per option and the self-story comes last, so it is cut from 44% of the options it applies to. It is kept from now on, in addition to the five. People act partly from who they take themselves to be (narrative identity, McAdams 2001; identity-based motivation, Oyserman 2007); a model that says so must weigh it every time.
2. **A circumstance pushes toward the answer it favours** (`OFF=pushes`). Ten situations give every option the same circumstance, which, through the way scores are combined, changes only how decisive the choice is, and by accident in opposite directions. Each now favours one side and counts against the other, by the same measure read the other way, so it is also given as a reason: being in debt favours taking what is not yours; needing the money, going out into the storm; people here depending on you, staying; being good at it, taking the spot on stage; being stretched, letting the deadline and the harbour day slip; being short of money, giving little when the hat goes round; having done the better work, or having wanted the post for years, answering a lost contest with pride or anger; a plan taking your trade, fighting it; a rivalry that has got personal, its hostile answers. Whether strain should also make choices noisier (scarcity narrows attention: Mani, Mullainathan, Shafir and Zhao 2013) is left for a round of its own; here the accidental effect on decisiveness goes.
   Test: twenty fresh worlds 2725–2744, on against both switches off, with an instrumented copy of the engine counting what each decision weighed; scoreboard paired on 1631–1640 (1601–1630 are quiz worlds); a smoke run first on 9001–9002; a blind cold read, 12 lives in each arm from four of the test worlds, new reader.
   Expected:
3. The self-story is weighed in **100%** of the options it applies to (from 56%).
4. Where the self-story favours one family, that family is chosen **at least 5 points** more often than with `selfkept` off.
5. Each circumstance now pushes: pooled over the ten situations, the favoured answer is chosen **at least 10 points** more often when the circumstance is strong (its input above 0.6) than when weak (below 0.4); with `pushes` off the gap is under 5 points. Shown for the hat and the deadline separately (the two commonest).
6. The circumstance is given among a decision's reasons in **at least 20%** of those decisions (from about none).
7. Gate: the calibrated witness within ±2 of the off arm on decisions that are not a couple's. A circumstance in the open (a storm, a public sting) is something the witness can see, so readability may rise; a private one (debt) it cannot.
8. Lives: the blind read no worse than the off arm by more than 0.3; the consequence map within ±1 point.
   What would change our minds: if 3 holds and 5 falls by more than 2, circumstances now decide choices the witness cannot read, which says the page must show the circumstances people are in.

Amendment to round 24b (18 Oct 2026, 01:03 Oslo, after the smoke run, before any test run). The smoke run (worlds 9001–9002) showed the circumstances working (the favoured answer 63% when strong against 40% when weak; with the switch off, 47% against 45%) and the self-story kept in every option, but it also showed that keeping it **lowered** the answer it favours (67% against 76% with it off). The reason: every consideration is a factor of at most 1, multiplied in, and the self-story was given only to the answers it speaks of, so "that is who I am" (a factor of about 0.8) penalised the very answer it favoured, while the others carried nothing; it did less harm before only because it was often cut. Part 1 now also gives it to every other answer of the situation, read the other way, as part 2 does for circumstances. Same switch (`selfkept`), same expectations.

Outcome of round 24b (18 Oct 2026, 01:25 Oslo). Twenty fresh worlds 2725–2744, on against both switches off (`r24b.txt`, `consequences-r24b*.txt`); scoreboard paired on 1631–1640. A separate agent re-derived every number from the raw files and found the same. With both switches off, behaviour is identical to round 24a (`sig.ts`) ✓.

- Read cold first, blind: 24 random lives from worlds 2725–2728, twelve from each arm, shuffled, a new reader. On **2.75**, off **2.42** (✓, no worse); who the person is in one sentence: 9 of 12 on, 11 of 12 off.

1. The self-story weighed in **100%** of the options it applies to, from 51% (✓).
2. Where it favours one answer, that answer is chosen **80.2%** [79.2, 81.2] against 74.9% [73.8, 76.0] (✓, +5.3 points, at least 5). The smoke run had found that, as first written, it lowered the answer it favoured; the amendment above fixed that before any test run.
3. Circumstances push: pooled over the ten situations, the favoured answer is chosen **72.6%** [68.8, 76.1] when the circumstance is strong against 39.7% when weak (✓, +33 points, at least 10); the hat +23, the deadline +41. With the switch off the gap is **7.6 points** (**✗**, expected under 5): what goes with strain, debt or ties is also partly who people are.
4. The circumstance among a decision's reasons: **50.3%** of those decisions, from none (✓). Example: "Mark had left the wallet at home when the hat went round for Edward — Edward had a second heart attack; short of money; despite altruism: helps at own cost".
5. Gate: the calibrated witness, on minus off, +0.6 ±2.7 on decisions that are not a couple's, +0.1 ±2.2 overall (✓); the witness beats the notebook on the key slice by +5.9 ±2.0.
6. The consequence map: weighty descendants −0.8 points [−1.7, +0.1] and turns −0.6 [−1.2, −0.05], paired over twenty worlds (✓, within ±1). The fall in turns is small and real: people who act more consistently with who they are and the state they are in change course a little less often.
   `npm test` passes. **Kept.**
   After the read, words only (checked with `sig.ts`), from what the reader found: a row in company says where it was at the end of the sentence instead of inside its subject (the round 24a wordings had garbled it: "raised his family at the co-op, with people listening with Alan carefully"); "a the town person through and through" (now 'rooted in the town, through and through'); "to be heard playing vocals"; "to find their people"; "after 1 year of trying" for something done within the year. Found and left for a round, because it changes behaviour: the taking-what-is-not-yours situation without money in it can still choose skimming ("took a little from a mistake at work"); two adults in a town can share a full name and age; and the pattern a couple's rows fall into can be dated before the start of the partnership the line names ("Nothing had changed since 2016 … together since 2021": the start date is the marriage).

## Round 24c — reasons as motives (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 01:49 Oslo, before its code)

What blind readers, and the external review of round 23b, named first: a reason names a trait ("altruism: helps at own cost") where a person would give a motive ("could not stand to see Grace go without"). People explain intentional acts by reasons, what the person wanted, feared or believed, and keep traits for the background (Malle 1999; Malle, Knobe and Nelson 2007). The trait still sets the weight; only what the reason says changes, so this is words only, checked with `sig.ts`.
In throwaway worlds 9001–9003, trait labels are 46% of the reasons a reader sees (3,094 of 6,698); fifty combinations of trait, pole and kind of answer cover 76% of them and a hundred 92%. Bond reasons say "how Lisa feels about Ben" without saying how.
Changes:

1. Every trait reason is said as a motive: one wording for each pole of each facet, and its own wording where a pole meets the kinds of answer it most often explains (generosity, duty, humility, pride, anger, honesty and so on), each with a form for when it argued against the act ("despite wanting to help").
2. A bond reason says the feeling ("fond of Ben", "has never trusted Ben", "thinks the world of Ben"); a bond near neutral is not a reason.
   Test: `sig.ts` identical to round 24b; on fresh worlds 2745–2748, the share of shown reasons that are a trait label, and of bond reasons that say how; then the blind read as in round 24a: 24 random lives from those worlds, each shown once, half in the round 24b words and half in the new, shuffled, a new reader not told there are two versions, with the same questions, and asked also to tag each line it flags by kind (a reason that does not fit the act; wording that repeats or reads as generated; a contradiction; a history the page has not shown; other).
   Expected:
3. Behaviour identical (`sig.ts`).
4. Trait labels among shown reasons: **0%** (from 46%); bond reasons that say how: **100%**.
5. The blind read: the new words score **at least 0.4 higher** out of five than the old, and lines flagged as a reason that does not fit the act are **at most half** as many per life.
   What would change our minds: if the misfitting reasons halve but the score does not move, readers notice wrong reasons but the story is not made of them, and what is left is the events: people who steer.
