2. On worlds 2745–2748, ten years, the first three reasons of every weighty event: trait labels **0** of the reasons shown, from 4,821 in the round 24b words (✓); bond reasons that say how, **all** (✓; 460 template bond reasons in the old words, none in the new).
3. The blind read, 24 lives from those worlds, twelve in each version, shuffled, a new reader not told there were two: new words **3.00**, old **2.67** (**✗**, +0.33, expected at least +0.4); lines flagged as a reason that does not fit the act, **7** in the new against **5** in the old (**✗**, expected at most half); who the person is in one sentence, 11 of 12 against 10 of 12. Two of the seven came from default wordings that did not fit the answer they explained ("puts other people first" for letting something slide; "had said she would" with no promise behind it); the others were reasons of state, want and the self-story, which this round did not touch.
   Read by the rule set beforehand: the misfitting reasons did not halve and the score moved by less than expected, so the words are not what keeps a life hollow. What the reader named instead is the events: a friendship begun and, a page later, someone "can't stand" the friend; a want pursued, failed and taken up again without change; confiding and keeping a secret with nothing said about what; reasons for a friendship that would fit any friendship; the same stamp ("Once again…") on a habit every few months. Counted on throwaway worlds 9001–9004: friendships that end within a year, 1 of 361; habits that lapse and come back within a year, 15 of 130 (12%); weekly visits in someone's trouble followed within a year by the two drifting apart, 11 of 356 (3%). Only the habits flip often; the rest is a page that does not say what changed in between. **Kept** (the motives are better than the labels, and nothing got worse).
   After the read, words only (checked with `sig.ts`): the two defaults; "hisself"; "was getting to" (now "had been getting to"); "the only one in the running"; "came through it, after N months of" what it was; "taking from what was not theirs" where there was no thing.
   Left for the next round, which is about events: people who steer, with what each choice changed shown by a what-if; the behaviour faults listed under round 24b.

## Round 25a — what a choice changes: the what-if measure (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 02:22 Oslo, before its code)

The consequence map (round 22) counts links the record can trace; it cannot say whether a life went differently because of a choice. Criterion 2 asks exactly that ("within three years, something besides a feeling: where someone lives or works, money, who they see, what they want"), and the next round, people who steer, must be judged by it. This round builds the measure and takes the baseline; it changes no behaviour.
How: the same world twice from the moment of one decision. A hook in the two places a choice is drawn (read only by the tool, `globalThis.WHATIF`; unset, nothing changes) lets a run either give the most likely answer the person did not choose (**flip**) or keep the same answer and take one extra random draw from the same stream (**placebo**, the noise floor: how much a life differs three years on by chance alone, after the smallest possible nudge at the same moment). Each copy runs 36 months from the start of the month of the decision, cloned from the factual world then, and the person who chose is compared with the factual run.
A life has changed if, after 36 months, any of these differ: in town, left or dead; where they work; their partner; who they live with (a married partner and their own children under 18 in town; for someone under 18, their parents in town); their friends (overlap under two thirds); money (a difference of at least 8, what it takes to leave, or a quarter of their savings, whichever is larger); what they want (the set of open wants). The first four are a **major** change. The effect of a choice is the share changed after a flip minus the share changed after a placebo.
Test: twenty fresh worlds 2749–2768. In each, every decision in the first seven present years where the likeliest other answer had at least 0.15 is eligible; 40 are drawn at random, topped up so that each kind of decision with any eligible gets at least 5. Intervals by resampling worlds. A smoke run on 9001–9002 first, for the machinery only.
Expected:

1. No behaviour changes: `ident.ts` identical with the hook present and unset; a world cloned at the start of the present and run ten years gives the same `sig.ts` as the original.
2. The placebo alone changes 30–60% of lives in three years, and 10–25% majorly: the town's chances are shared, so any nudge reshuffles who meets what.
3. Overall, a flipped choice adds **under 10 points** of changed lives above the placebo, and under 3 points of major change: most choices here do not steer a life.
4. By kind: whether to leave a marriage adds **at least 50 points** of major change; going after a want (or giving it up) at least 10 points of change; a row's start or answer, the hat, a joke at someone, a gesture of care at most 2.
5. The two measures agree where they overlap: the kinds the consequence map says lead nowhere (the hat, pursuits) add under 5 points.
   What would change our minds: if the placebo changes so many lives (above 70%) that a kind's effect cannot be resolved to ±5 points over twenty worlds, the town is too chaotic for what-ifs to see one person's steering, and the measure must hold other people's chances fixed (a random stream per person), a change to the engine that needs its own round.

Outcome of round 25a (18 Oct 2026, 03:36 Oslo). Twenty fresh worlds 2749–2768, 3,168 choices tested, each flipped and placeboed (`reports/whatif-r25a.txt`; raw rows in `out-whatif/`). A separate agent re-derived every number from the raw rows and found the same (intervals to within a point: its resampling, which is quoted here), and found the rows consistent (every flip paired with its placebo, nothing duplicated). In every world two untouched copies matched the factual run exactly, and the copy's choice was always the factual one.

1. No behaviour changes ✓: `ident.ts` identical to round 24c with the hook present; worlds copied at the start of the present and four years in run exactly as the originals.
2. The placebo alone changes **47.1%** of lives in three years (✓, 30–60%) and **26.7%** majorly (**✗**, expected 10–25%).
3. Overall, weighted to stand for every eligible choice: a flipped choice adds **+5.3** points of changed lives [+1.0, +9.3] (✓, under 10) and **+2.6** points of major change [−1.1, +6.1] (✓, under 3).
4. By kind, effect on major change: a job offer **+56** [+42, +69]; thinking of moving home +52 [+35, +69]; whether to leave a marriage **+41** [+17, +60] (**✗**, expected at least 50; the interval includes it); answering criticism at work +22 [+13, +31]; going for a post +12 [−2, +26]. Going after a want adds **+20** points of any change [+9, +30] and keeping at it or giving up **+33** [+20, +46] (✓, at least 10), but of keeping at it most is the want itself closing or not (43 lives changed in what they want and nothing else, against 19 after a placebo); in anything besides what they want, +11 and +9. A row's start −2.3 and answer +2.7, a joke at someone −9, the hat +5.0 any and +3.0 major (✗ for the answer and the hat, expected at most 2; every interval includes 0 and spans about ±10).
5. The two measures disagree on pursuits (**✗**): the consequence map says they lead nowhere the record can trace, but choosing to go after a want, or to keep at it, changes what follows: mostly what the person goes on wanting, some of it money and friends, little of it where they live or work.
   Read by the rule set beforehand: the placebo is under 70%, but a kind's effect still cannot be resolved to ±5 points (intervals are ±10 to ±20 at about a hundred choices a kind), and three kinds show a placebo changing _more_ than a flip (being asked for help −14 major, a newcomer −17 any, a discovery −14), which a fair floor would not do. The cause is in the engine, not the measure: each system draws from one stream for the whole town (the header of `rng.ts` says a stream serves "the entity and purpose", which is not what the run does), so one extra draw shifts every later draw in that stream for everyone, while a flipped choice often leaves the stream aligned. A quarter of lives changing majorly by chance alone after the smallest nudge is also a problem for the game: a player's act would visibly reshuffle unrelated lives, which reads as random (principle 2). What-ifs work for the strong kinds now; the next round makes chance stay where it falls.

## Round 25b — chance stays where it falls (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 03:37 Oslo, before its code)

Round 25a found that every system draws from one stream for the whole town, so the smallest nudge (one extra draw) changes a quarter of lives majorly within three years by chance alone. That blurs every what-if, and in a game it would make the player's acts reshuffle unrelated lives. Two changes, each behind a switch:

1. **A month's chance is its own** (`OFF=monthly`). Every system's stream is drawn afresh each month from the world (in the past) or the run (in the present), the month and the system, so whatever shifts a stream shifts it only until the month ends.
2. **A choice's chance is the chooser's own** (`OFF=ownchance`). The draw that decides a choice comes from a stream of its own, keyed by the run, the month, the person and how many choices they have made that month, so one person's choices never move another's.
   Neither changes what any situation or answer is, nor how likely; only which draw falls where. The behaviour of the town as a whole must stay the same in distribution.
   Test: the what-ifs of round 25a, same tool and settings, on twenty fresh worlds 2769–2788 with both changes, against round 25a (the same engine with both switches off, which `ident.ts` must show identical to it); the scoreboard paired on and off on 1641–1650; the consequence map on and off on the same twenty worlds; `npm test`.
   Expected:
3. Both switches off: `ident.ts` identical to round 25a.
4. The placebo changes **under 5%** of lives majorly (from 26.7%) and **under 15%** in any way (from 47.1%).
5. The strong kinds keep their effect: a job offer, thinking of moving home, whether to leave a marriage and answering criticism at work each within their round 25a interval on major change.
6. No kind's effect on major change is below −3 points (from −14 for being asked for help).
7. Sharper: the median half-width of the per-kind intervals on major change **under 8 points** (from about 12).
8. Gates: the scoreboard within ±2 on the key slice and overall; the consequence map's weighty descendants and turns within ±1.5 points; `npm test` passes.
   What would change our minds: if the placebo still changes more than 10% of lives majorly, chance spreads through what happens (a different meeting changes who meets whom next month), not through the streams; the town is then chaotic in itself, and the game must show the player why lives they did not touch went differently.

Outcome of round 25b (18 Oct 2026, 04:55 Oslo). What-ifs on twenty fresh worlds 2769–2788, 3,113 choices tested (`reports/whatif-r25b.txt`, raw rows in `out-whatif-r25b/`), against round 25a; scoreboard paired on 1641–1650 (`board-r25b-on.txt`, `board-r25b-off.txt`); the consequence map on and off on the twenty worlds (`consequences-r25b*.txt`). A separate agent re-derived the what-if numbers from the raw rows and found the same (median interval half-width 8.1 against my 8.0, from its resampling).

1. Both switches off: `ident.ts` identical to round 25a ✓. The copier still copies exactly with both on ✓.
2. The placebo changes **5.4%** of lives majorly [4.2, 6.5] (**✗**, expected under 5; from 26.7%) and **14.1%** in any way (✓, under 15; from 47.1%).
3. The strong kinds did not stay inside their round 25a intervals (**✗**): a job offer +80 (was +56), thinking of moving home +84 (was +52), whether to leave a marriage +62 (was +41) all rose above them, and answering criticism at work fell below (+10, was +22). In round 25a, chance had already changed many of the lives a flip would change, which hides part of the effect: a difference of shares understates an effect when the floor is high.
4. Kinds below −3 on major change (**✗**): a joke at someone **−10** [−17, −3], going after a want −4 [−9, 0], a row's answer −3.3 [−10, +3]. The cause is in the measure: the placebo's extra draw still moves the chooser's own later draws that month, which the flip arm never gets. The two arms should take the same nudge, so that the only difference between them is the answer.
5. Median half-width of the per-kind intervals on major change **8.0** points, from 10.2 (**✗**, expected under 8).
6. Gates ✓: the calibrated witness on minus off +0.7 ±2.4 overall and −0.1 ±2.4 on the key slice (+0.9 ±2.1 and 0.0 ±2.8 on decisions that are not a couple's); the consequence map's weighty descendants −0.3 points [−1.3, +0.8] and turns −0.3 [−1.1, +0.5], paired over twenty worlds; `npm test` passes.
   Pooled over every eligible choice, a flipped choice now adds **+6.4** points of major change [+4.6, +8.5] and **+13.6** of any change [+11.0, +15.9], where round 25a could not tell its major effect from zero. Read by the rule set beforehand: the placebo is far under 10%, so chance spread through the streams, not through what happens; what is left of the floor is the measure's own asymmetry. **Kept**: the town behaves the same, and a choice now changes what follows from it, not everyone's luck.

## Round 25c — people who steer (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 04:56 Oslo, before its code)

Criterion 2, and what blind readers named first: lives are made of upkeep, and wanting something changes little. Round 25b measured it: choosing to go after a want, or to keep at it, changes what the person goes on wanting and almost nothing about where they live or work, their money or who they see. Every want has one fixed way to go after it (save, practise, work harder, show up) and one check, keep at it or give up. People pursue goals by choosing means and switching between them when one is blocked (Kruglanski and others 2002, a theory of goal systems), and adjust the goal itself when it cannot be had (Wrosch and others 2003, goal disengagement and reengagement). One switch, `OFF=steer`:

1. **A way to go after it.** When a want becomes a pursuit, the person chooses how, weighed from who they are and where they stand, and each way goes through a part of the town that changes something:
   - a post: work for it (as now); ask for it (whoever decides answers from what they think of the person: a yes makes them the one to beat when the post comes up, a no is an obstacle); look elsewhere (take the same kind of work at another place when it has an opening: a new job).
   - work: wait for an opening (as now); ask around (a friend who works where there is an opening puts in a word).
   - something to own, or getting out: save (as now); take on extra work (more money each month, more strain, fewer evenings out); ask family (a parent or sibling who can afford it decides whether to help: money moves, and so does trust).
   - to be heard: practise (as now); get up on stage (the open night comes to them, and how it goes decides: well, they have got there; badly, an obstacle).
   - to find their people: show up (as now); ask someone along (the one they would most like to know better, who decides).
2. **An obstacle is a decision.** When a way fails (a no, a bad night, money lost, a long stretch with nothing gained), the person decides: keep at it the same way; try another way, with the obstacle as its reason; aim lower (the want becomes a smaller one: a cheaper thing to own, any work instead of the post); or let it go. This replaces the old keep-or-give-up check.
3. **A want given up comes back changed or not at all.** The same want is not taken up again within three years of giving it up, and after that only once something it depends on has changed (money, work, a partner), which is then its reason.
   The measure: the what-ifs of round 25b, except that the flip arm now takes the same nudge to chance as the placebo, so the only difference between the arms is the answer (round 25b found the asymmetry). Both arms of this round are measured this way.
   Test: twenty fresh worlds 2789–2808, what-ifs with `steer` on and off; the consequence map on and off; the scoreboard paired on 1651–1660; a smoke run first on 9001–9002; a blind cold read, 12 lives from each arm from four of the test worlds, shuffled, a new reader not told there are two arms.
   Expected:
4. `steer` off: `ident.ts` identical to round 25b.

But it rarely happens: **62% of adults pursue nothing in ten years**, nearly half of all pursuits are making up after a falling-out (which has one way), and fewer than twenty pursuits a world-decade choose a way at all. The page cannot show people steering who never want anything. ## Round F26 — the engine does what it says (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 13:58 Oslo, before its code)

Criterion 3 (nothing on a page claims what did not happen) and criterion 6 (readability holds, honestly). An outside review of H25 (`reports/review-2026-10-18/`, PR 3) found five places where the code does not do what the docs say, and measured three of them on throwaway worlds 9001–9020. Run by a second session while the lab chat is paused (NOW.md). Each fix has its own switch:

1. **The count of considerations carries no weight** (`OFF=countfair`). `score` multiplies factors of at most 1, and the compensation for their number only partly undoes it, so an answer with fewer considerations wins more often (review: the answers have different counts in 47% of decisions; the fewest gain 12 points of probability; the likeliest answer differs in 7.2%). Now every answer of a decision is scored as if it had as many considerations as the one with most, each missing one at the answer's own geometric mean; the compensation is applied once, for that number. Readers use the same `score`, so they change with it.
2. **An unkept act leaves the record, nothing else does** (`OFF=eventfix`). When an act is not kept, each person's last event was taken off their list, which is the thread's own event when the thread step has just added one (review: 1.8% of removals, about 2,500 phases, patterns and costs in 40 worlds). Now the act itself is taken off, wherever it is in the list (`grammar.ts` and the two in `group.ts`).
3. **The strongest part of the self-story speaks** (`OFF=identfix`). `identityC` compared each entry with a best that could be negative, so a weak "that is who she is" listed after a strong "not who she is" won. Now the entry with the greatest weight speaks, whichever way it points.
4. **Evidence from what an observer could see** (`OFF=honestev`). Three parts of an act's evidence used the truth: its weight scaled with the person's true trait size, it was discounted by how much circumstance truly drove the choice, and an if-then at work sent its true direction (whatever the act) and took half the facet's evidence. Now the weight uses the average trait size (0.933) and the average discount (0.1875), both measured on round H25's worlds in the review, and an if-then's evidence takes the act's own direction on that facet, or none, with the facet's evidence left whole. Not changed: if-then evidence still appears only for people who have one, and manner (round 7b).
5. **Without the time budget, the dead still leave one's friends** (no switch; it only changes the `OFF=budget` arm). `friendsTick` returned at once with `budget` off, so the dead and departed stayed close friends in that arm. Now only losing touch is skipped.

Seeds, all fresh: scoreboard base rates from 1501–1520 (training), evaluation **1721–1740** (twenty worlds, for power); lives on test worlds **3001–3020**; fault counts on **3021–3024**; cold reads from **3025–3026**. Smoke runs on 9101+.

Expected:

1. With all four switches off, `ident.ts` identical to main's on the default arm and on every switch arm except `budget` (item 5).
2. Faults, counted over 3021–3024, on against off: removals that take the wrong event 1–3% → **0**; self-story reasons where a weaker entry overrode a stronger one pointing the other way, more than 0 → **0**.
3. With `countfair` on, the likeliest answer differs from the old rule's in **4–10%** of decisions.
4. Gate: the calibrated witness, on minus off, within ±2 on decisions that are not a couple's, with the overall number beside it, and an equivalence verdict (the 90% interval inside ±2). The key slice, calibrated minus notebook: `honestev` costs **0 to 2 points** (review: −0.9 ±2.0); `countfair` within ±2.
5. Lives, 3001–3020, on against off: dead lives within ±2 points; median close friends within ±1 and friendships per adult within ±10%; the consequence map's weighty descendants and turns within ±2 points; arcs within ±3 points. These are corrections that move close calls, not whole kinds of life.
6. Cold read (six lives per arm from 3025–3026, shuffled and labelled by a code only, read before any metric): reader scores within 0.5 of each other; no fault that one arm has and the other lacks, beyond chance.
7. `npm test` passes.

What would change our minds:

- If `countfair` moves the lives in expectation 5 beyond their bounds, the count of considerations was quietly shaping lives, and the numbers in STATUS rested on it. The size of the shift is the finding; the fix stays and STATUS is restated from the on arm.
- If the gate fails, the switch that fails it is found with one-switch arms on the same range. It stays off, the others stay on, and its own round asks why readability rested on it.
- If `honestev` costs more than 3 points on the key slice, the readers were winning on what no observer could see, more than the review measured. It stays on (the docs require it), and STATUS restates the number.

Outcome of round F26 (18 Oct 2026, 14:55 Oslo). Fault counts on 3021–3024 (`reports/f26-faults-*.txt`); scoreboard paired on 1721–1740 with base rates from 1501–1520, each arm trained on its own code (`reports/board-f26-*.txt`, `reports/pair-f26.txt`); lives on 3001–3020 (`reports/story-f26.txt`, `friends-f26.txt`, `consequences-f26*.txt`); cold read before any metric (`reports/coldread-f26.txt`); identity against main (`reports/ident-f26.txt`). A separate agent re-derived the scoreboard and lives numbers from the raw files and got the same figures.

1. With all four switches off, `ident.ts` identical to main on the default arm and on 43 of the 44 switch arms; `budget` differs, as expected ✓.
2. Faults, over 3021–3024, ten present years: removals that took the wrong event 0.80% without the fixes (52 of 6,471; expected 1–3%, so the baseline came in under), **0** with them ✓; thread events missing from their own people's lists 207 → **0** ✓; unkept acts still on a list 52 → **0** ✓; self-story readings where a weaker entry overrode a stronger one 3 of 14,387 → **0** ✓ (rare: 0.02%).
3. With `countfair` on, the likeliest answer differs from the old rule's in **5.9%** of decisions (404 of 6,875) ✓.
4. Gate: the calibrated witness, on minus off, **−1.4 ±1.4** on decisions that are not a couple's and −0.9 ±1.2 overall. Within ±2 as the gate reads it ✓; the equivalence verdict holds overall (90% interval −1.9 to +0.2) but **not** on decisions that are not a couple's (−2.6 to −0.2) ✗. Leave-one-out arms on the same worlds put all of it on `countfair` (on minus no-countfair: −1.3 ±1.1 not a couple's, −0.8 ±1.3 overall); `honestev` costs nothing measurable (+0.5 ±1.3 and +0.6 ±1.1; expected 0 to 2) ✓; `identfix` +0.2 ±0.2; `eventfix` changes no prediction at all. The page-annotation reader loses 2.8 ±1.4 on decisions that are not a couple's, also from `countfair`. The key slice is not like for like across the arms: 'contested' is read off the engine's own scorer, and the fair count makes many more decisions contested (29,038 of 34,108 against 19,821 of 34,199), so the slice holds 53% of decisions on and 32% off. Within each arm the witness beats the notebook on it: **+5.5 ±1.2** on, +6.3 ±1.5 off ✓ (criterion 6 holds).
5. Lives, on against off: dead lives 3.8% against 5.5% (round 19d measure 1.1% against 1.3%) ✓; median close friends 3 in both, friendships made per adult a year 0.29 against 0.27 ✓; the consequence map's weighty descendants 23% in both, turns 10% against 9% ✓. **Arcs ✗ beyond their bound:** adults with at least one arc 22.9% against 16.7% (19d measure 20.0% against 14.8%), and adults with a named rival 22.0% against 16.1% (not pre-registered).
6. Cold read: 3.0 against 2.8 out of five, the same faults in both arms ✓. Every life the reader saw carried faults of the page itself (reasons that do not fit the act, first-person reasons that reverse who did what, a goal dated two ways, acts at a place the person had left, repeats, broken words); these are not this round's, and they are listed in `reports/coldread-f26.txt` for the round that takes them.
7. `npm test` passes, with the new invariant (every thread event on its people's own lists) ✓.
   Read by the rules set beforehand: the arcs moved beyond their bound, so the count of considerations was quietly shaping lives. Answers that carried fewer considerations had been winning close calls; scored fairly, arcs and rivalries rise by about six points each. Which answers gained is not yet traced. That is the finding, and STATUS is restated from the on arm. The gate passes as written; the equivalence miss on decisions that are not a couple's is `countfair`'s, and it changes the readers' own scorer as much as the people, so part of it is the readers relearning. **Kept**, all four switches on; `honestev` makes BOARD's "no reader has a route to hidden truth" true of the evidence at no measurable cost. STATUS also now says what the code does where it said otherwise: an answer keeps its first five considerations in build order, not its five strongest.

## Round 25d — everyone wants something (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 07:58 Oslo, before its code)

_Written in the working tree before this repository existed, at the time in its heading, and committed here unchanged. Its code came after it, there too._

Round 25c found steering strong where it happens and rare: 62% of adults pursue nothing in ten years. In throwaway worlds 9001–9003, at any moment 40% of adults aged 18–69 have an open want and 25% are pursuing one; a want comes from a few narrow routes (success and a job, a hobby taken seriously, being alone, a falling-out), so people who hold loved ones, tradition, security or fittingIn most dear seldom want anything the model can pursue. People carry several personal projects at once, most of them ordinary (Little 1983; Emmons 1986, personal strivings). One switch, `OFF=everyone`:

1. **A want from what they value.** Once a year, an adult of 18–64 with no open want of their own takes one from their core values, the first whose door the town has open to them: success, a post where they work, or work if they have none; status, a decent car, or a post; security, a house of their own, or work; freedom, getting out (under 40 with no children at home), or a boat; thrill, the big trip, or getting out (under 32); pleasure, being heard at a hobby they care for; fitting in, finding their people (fewer than two friends); loved ones and tradition, marrying the one they are seeing, or a house of their own. Its urgency is how strongly they hold the value. Fairness has no door yet and passes to their next value.
2. **Marrying is something you can go after.** Wanting to marry the one you are seeing becomes a pursuit like the others, with two ways: wait for it (as now: it happens or it does not), or ask (the other decides, from what they feel and how they love; a no is an obstacle).
   Test: twenty fresh worlds 2833–2852, what-ifs with `everyone` on and off; the pursuit measures, the consequence map and demography on and off on the same worlds; the scoreboard paired on 1671–1680; a smoke run first on 9001–9002; a blind cold read, 12 lives from each arm from four of the test worlds, the same people drawn for both and each shown once, a new reader.
   Expected:
3. `everyone` off: `ident.ts` identical to round H25.
4. Adults who pursued something in ten years: at least **60%** (from about 38%).
5. What-ifs: pooled over every choice, a flipped choice adds at least **2 points** more major change than with `everyone` off; going after a want adds at least +8 points of major change.
6. Marriages, departures and births per world-decade each within ±30% of the off arm.
7. The blind read: lives with `everyone` on score at least 0.3 higher.
8. Gates: the scoreboard within ±2 (decisions that are not a couple's, overall beside it); the consequence map's weighty descendants and turns no lower than off by more than 1 point; `npm test` passes.
   What would change our minds: if far more people pursue something but the what-ifs do not move (under +1 point pooled), ordinary wants are chores, not choices: the town's doors (work to change to, places to move to, things to start) are what is missing, and that comes before more wanting.

Amendment to round 25d (18 Oct 2026, 08:12 Oslo, after the smoke run on 9001–9002, before any test run). With "getting out" as a door for freedom (under 40) and thrill (under 32), `npm test`'s demography check failed (births to deaths 0.58, under-20s 16% over twenty years): handing every young person who values freedom a want to leave empties the town of the people who would have had its children. Getting out stays a want only by the route it had before (round 2: valuing freedom or thrill, under 32, single); the doors for freedom and thrill are now a boat or the big trip, things to have here. With that, `npm test` passes. The smoke run also showed that the condition as registered, an adult _with no open want of their own_, leaves out anyone holding a want they never act on; that is part of the design under test and stays as written. Expectations unchanged.

Outcome of round 25d (18 Oct 2026, 21:00 Oslo; the tests were run by the cloud session after the local session stopped, on the round's tree merged with main at `12b9186`). Battery by `scripts/round.mjs` (`reports/board-25d-runner.txt`); what-ifs `reports/whatif-25d-on.txt` and `-off.txt`; pursuit measures `reports/pursuits-25d.txt`; demography `reports/demography-25d-on.txt` and `-off.txt`. A separate agent re-derived the scoreboard and lives numbers from the raw files and got the same figures (`reports/rederive-25d.txt`).

1. `everyone` off: identical to main (`ident.ts`, every arm) ✓.
2. Adults who pursued something in ten years: **55%** on (45.1% never pursued) against 43% off; expected at least 60% ✗. Pursuits started 68.5 a world-decade against 47.5, got there 37.7 against 24.4, and kinds that hardly existed appear (acquire 9.3, belong 8.0, win 2.6).
3. What-ifs (3,337 choices tested on, 3,247 off): pooled, a flipped choice adds **10.3** points of major change on against **8.5** off, +1.8; expected at least +2 ✗ (any change 16.7 against 17.2). Going after a want adds **+17.0** major on (+5.0 off); expected at least +8 ✓. Choosing how to go after it: 28.0 on, 32.6 off.
4. Per world-decade: married 5.0 against 4.5 (+11%), departures 5.7 against 5.4 (+6%), births 7.9 against 7.5 (+5%; the demography tool's count 8.4 against 8.8), all within ±30% ✓; the demography check passes in both arms.
5. The blind read, by the reader the local session had pre-registered before this session took over: on **2.50**, off **2.36**, +0.14; expected at least +0.3 ✗. The reading was done in the local session and reported in its status; its files were not committed.
6. Gates: the scoreboard paired on 1671–1680, calibrated on minus off, on decisions that are not a couple's **−1.2 ±2.0** (overall −1.4 ±2.1; key slice −2.3 ±3.3; seven of ten worlds negative; the point is inside ±2 and the interval reaches −3.0) ✓ with that caveat; the consequence map, weighty descendants 0.23 against 0.23 and turns 0.10 against 0.10 ✓; `npm test` passes ✓. Lives: dead lives 4% against 5%, median weighty events per life 18.2 against 16.6, repeats 0.27 against 0.24, arcs per adult 0.44 in both.
   Read by the rules set beforehand: wants reached more people and mattered when pursued, and did not reach enough people, did not move what a choice changes overall, and did not make the lives read better. The pre-registered "what would change our minds" does not fire (the pooled what-ifs moved more than +1), so the reading is not that ordinary wants are chores. It is that the door is narrow: the rule gives a want once a year only to an adult with no open want of their own (the amendment named this as part of the design), and a want's first door (a post where they work, a house) is often shut. And more events per life did not read as a better life (+1.6 weighty events, +0.14 on the read), which is the measure that matters.
   **Not kept.** The code stays behind `everyone`, off by default: `ON=everyone` turns it on (the `ON` set in `world.ts` is new, read by the tools as `OFF` is). The next wants round, after the coherence floor, asks whether the condition or the doors held it back, and is judged on the read, not the count.

## Round H26 — the record, second pass (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 08:42 Oslo, **after** its code)

_Written in the working tree before this repository existed, at the time in its heading, and, as it says, after its code. Committed here unchanged._

Written after its code, in a working copy, while round 25d's runs held the main tree: the same breach of order as round 24a, said plainly. All but one change are words only, and the faults were traced in the code from what the round 25c and 25d readers flagged, before any of it was run. Words only, checked with `sig.ts` against round 25d with the one switch below off:

1. "Took a little from undefined": a place opened in the present (the café) has no named money; it now has a till.
2. "Got there … after 12 years of trying [had been working toward it since 2031]": trying is now counted from the pursuit, wanting from the want.
3. Reasons from memory were in the holder's own words ("went to me and apologised", "let me put it right") and one event could be given three times; they are now said from outside ("Stuart went to Alex and apologised") and once per event.
4. "Nothing had changed since 2015 [together since 2023]": "together since" counted from the wedding; it now counts from when they began seeing each other.
5. "Both after the foreman's job, and it shows" was still the reason years after the post went to one of them; once the post is decided it is a grievance ("still sore about the foreman's job").
6. "Got there: to be heard playing guitar" was said when someone got good from practising, before anyone heard them; it now says that people have started to talk about their playing.
7. "After 9 months of in debt"; "despite: nothing in the way"; "has two pints at the Anchor after every shift" for someone with no shift; a row about the drinking raised by the one who drinks.
   One changes behaviour, behind a switch:

## Round M26 — measuring well (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 08:44 Oslo, before its code)

_Written in the working tree before this repository existed, at the time in its heading, and committed here unchanged. Its code came after it, there too; the amendment below was written before any of its runs._

Two external reviews (of rounds 23b and 24b) and round 25d's own data agree on three faults in how the lab measures. A panel of four readers on round 25d's lives agreed well (Krippendorff's α 0.78 [0.59, 0.89]) and all four scored the on arm higher, by +0.14 to +0.41, yet the panel's difference, +0.28, has an interval of [−0.30, +0.85]: with twelve different people in each arm, how much lives differ from each other drowns any difference between arms, so every blind-read verdict since round 24a was underpowered. Second, the scoreboard gate was called passed when the point estimate fell within ±2, though its intervals (±1.5 to ±4.6) often could not rule out a regression of that size. Third, the witness is given the structure of a decision built from the truth: whether a self-story consideration exists depends on the person's private self-story, so a hidden self-story changes the witness's confidence (the round 24b review measured 98.4% against 95.6%). Changes:

1. **Paired reading.** From now on a reading test shows a reader the same person's two lives, one from each arm, side by side in random order (A and B, told only that they are two versions), each scored 1–5 and one preferred; three readers of different backgrounds; reliability (ordinal α) over all scores, and a reading result counts only if α is at least .667; the effect is the mean paired difference over persons, its interval by resampling persons. Sixteen persons a round.
2. **The witness sees only what it could know** (`OFF=seenonly`). Every reader on the scoreboard that stands for an observer (the witness, the calibrated witness, the page-annotation reader, the situation reader, the swapped and tendency readers) scores a decision without considerations it could not know exist (the self-story); the trait oracle and the mind-reader keep them.
3. **A null control for the scoreboard.** A reader like the calibrated witness that, for each decision, uses the beliefs it holds about a different person drawn afresh for that decision (hash-keyed), keeping the situation and the circumstances: whatever edge it still has over the notebook does not come from knowing this person.
4. **Equivalence, said as such.** `pair.ts` reports for each slice whether the 90% interval of the paired difference lies within ±2 (equivalent), lies wholly below 0 (a regression), or neither (not shown either way). A gate requires no regression and says which it was.
   Test: the scoreboard paired on 1671–1680 with `seenonly` on and off; the null-control reader on the same run; the paired reading's sensitivity on a known contrast: sixteen persons from four fresh worlds 2873–2876, each shown as their true page against the same page with its events put in a shuffled order of years, three readers.
   Expected:
5. `seenonly`: the calibrated witness changes by less than 1 point overall and on the key slice; a reconstructed case like the review's gives the same prediction with and without a hidden self-story.
6. Null control: the edge of the calibrated witness over the notebook on the key slice falls to **at most +2** with another person's beliefs (from about +4 to +7 with this person's).
7. The paired reading detects the known contrast: the true page preferred in **at least 13 of 16** pairs by the panel majority, α at least .667.
   What would change our minds: if the null control keeps an edge above +4, the scoreboard measures how situations and circumstances go as much as who people are, and its headline needs a cleaner reference; if the paired reading cannot tell a shuffled life from a true one, reading tests by language models cannot judge rounds, and only people can.

Amendment to round M26 (18 Oct 2026, 09:54 Oslo, before any of its runs). Its pre-registration named the scoreboard range 1671–1680, already taken by round 25d; M26 uses 1681–1690. Nothing else changes.

Outcome of round M26 (18 Oct 2026, 17:55 Oslo; tested by the cloud session after the local chat stopped). Scoreboard paired on 1681–1690 with `seenonly` on and off (`reports/board-m26.txt`); the paired reading on sixteen people from 2873–2876 (`reports/m26-reading.txt`, tool `src/tools/diag/m26pair.ts`).

1. `seenonly` ✓: the calibrated witness, on minus off, **−0.2 ±0.4** overall and **−0.7 ±0.8** on the key slice, both under 1 point; overall and on decisions that are not a couple's the 90% interval lies within ±2 (equivalent, by this round's own verdict). The reconstructed case from the review was not rebuilt; the paired numbers stand for it.
2. Null control ✓, and more clearly than expected: the donor reader (another known person's beliefs, drawn afresh per decision) sits **4.9 ±2.4 below the notebook** on the key slice (expected at most +2 above it), 7.6 below overall; the calibrated witness beats it by **+9.3 ±2.1**. Whatever edge the witness has comes from knowing this person, not from the situation and the circumstances alone.
3. The paired reading ✓: three readers of different backgrounds preferred the true page in **16 of 16** pairs (bar 13), α ordinal **.866** [.765, .912] (bar .667); mean paired difference 2.0 points on five. A shuffled life reads as "a list of consequences arriving before their causes" (all three readers, independently). The method can tell a true life from a scrambled one; it is fit to judge rounds.
   Gate: on this round's tree. `main` has moved since (F26); after `main` is merged in, the scoreboard is run once more, paired, before the merge, and the result is added here. Done (18 Oct 2026, 21:40 Oslo, main at `566fd98` with 25d merged and off by default; `reports/board-m26-merged.txt`): `seenonly` on minus off, paired on 1681–1690, decisions that are not a couple's **−0.2 ±0.2**, overall −0.2 ±0.4, key slice −0.9 ±0.4; the page-annotation reader −0.4 ±0.2. Within the gate's ±2 and within the point the round expected: what the witness could not have known was worth under one point. Identity with `seenonly` off against main: all identical. **Kept**: the paired reading replaces single-reader reads from here on, `pair.ts` says equivalent, regression or neither, and the null control stays on the scoreboard.

## Round T2 — three human patterns (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 16:40 Oslo, before its code)

Measurement, not model. The scoreboard says the town's people can be read by readers built from the engine's own model; it cannot say whether they behave unlike people. T2 checks three patterns that psychology has replicated many times, each against a pass band written here from the literature before any number is computed. A pass is a plausibility check, never a realism claim; a fail names the next round's target. Main's engine (round H25), twenty fresh test worlds **3101–3120**, ten present years, adults; a new tool, `src/tools/patterns.ts`; nothing in the engine changes.

1. **One act against many** (Mischel 1968; Epstein 1979; Fleeson and Gallagher 2009). For each kind of answer, the facet that weighs most for it in the engine's own wiring (`FAMILY_FACETS`): across every present decision where that answer was offered, the correlation between the chooser's true facet and choosing it (single acts); and across people with at least ten such decisions, the correlation between the facet and how often they chose it (aggregates). Median over kinds of answer, signed so that the engine's direction counts as positive. Pass: single acts **.10 to .30**; aggregates **.40 to .70**. Above them the people are too consistent to be human; aggregates below .40, too random to be read.
2. **Friendship is narrow and closes triangles** (Amaral et al. 2000; Dunbar 2018; Kossinets and Watts 2006). (a) Close friends in town per adult at the end: pass if the top tenth has at most **2.5 times** the median and the most-befriended adult at most **4 times** the median. (b) Month by month, the rate at which two adults who are not friends become friends, for pairs with at least one friend in common against pairs with none: pass if the first is at least **3 times** the second.
3. **What is easy to judge** (Funder 1995; Connelly and Ones 2010). For people the witness knows, the correlation across people between the witness's belief about each facet and the truth, averaged over the facets of each HEXACO dimension, pooled over worlds. Pass: extraversion first or second of the six dimensions; conscientiousness in the top three; emotionality below extraversion among the people the witness is less familiar with (familiarity below the median of the people it knows); and emotionality's gain from familiarity (the more familiar half against the less) larger than extraversion's.
   Expected, written now: check 1 fails high on aggregates (the engine weighs traits in every decision, and STATUS's own trait oracle reaches 75% of choices); check 2(a) fails (STATUS: median 3, top tenth 10, most 19 to 23); checks 2(b) and 3 unknown.
   What would change our minds: none of the bands moves after a result. A check that cannot be computed as written is reported as such, with why, not redefined; any redefinition is a new pre-registration.

Outcome of round T2 (18 Oct 2026, 17:40 Oslo). `reports/patterns-t2.txt`; main's engine as pre-registered (round H25, `c256fd0`; F26 merged after the run). Twenty worlds 3101–3120, ten present years.

1. One act against many: single acts median **.42** (**✗**, above .30); aggregates median **.55** (✓, within .40 to .70). The expectation that aggregates would fail high was wrong: they are in the human range; it is single acts that are too consistent. By kind of answer, six are human (humility .25, anger .27, withdrawal .22, disclosure .17, openness .18, reluctance .14) and the rest run .36 to .59 (generosity and refusal .53, discretion .59, honesty .52, duty .51). Aggregates could be computed for eight kinds only (the rest have too few people with ten decisions), so the median rests on eight.
2. Friendship: (a) top tenth ÷ median **3.0** (**✗**, bar 2.5) and most ÷ median **7.7** (**✗**, bar 4): the tail is wider than people's, as STATUS says (weakness 7). (b) A friend in common raises the chance of becoming friends **4.7 times** (✓, bar 3): 0.85 against 0.18 per thousand pairs a month.
3. What is easy to judge: all four ✓ (order A > X > C > H > O > E; emotionality below extraversion among the less familiar; emotionality gains more from familiarity). Read with care: the dimensions sit between .41 and .47 and the gains between .02 and .07, so the passes are thin. In people the spread is wide (extraversion well above emotionality at zero acquaintance); here the witness judges every dimension about equally well. Not a registered check; noted for the round that takes the witness's channels.
   Read by the rules set beforehand: check 1 names the next engine round. People are too consistent on single acts and human over many, which is exactly what a within-person spread of states would give (Fleeson): the mean is the person, the spread is also the person. Check 2(a) confirms weakness 7 with a number. **Kept** as a standing check: `patterns.ts` runs on every round that changes decisions, and its three bands stand.

## Round T1 — one command runs a round's checks (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 16:05 Oslo, before its code)

Process, not model. Every round runs the same battery by hand: an identity check, fault counts, the scoreboard in two arms, lives on and off, a cold-read pack, `npm test`, paired statistics. Round F26 took about fifteen hand-run steps, and the hand-run parts are where things slip: `board.sh` waits on its two halves with an operandless `wait`, so a crashed half still reports success; a queue bundles whatever the source is when each job starts, so editing during a run corrupts it; the gate is read from a point estimate; key-slice comparisons across a change to the scorer are not like for like. T1 adds `scripts/round.mjs`, one command that, from a round's switches and seed ranges:

1. runs every job from a frozen copy of the committed tree (a git worktree at the commit), never the working tree;
2. runs the jobs in parallel up to a set number, records each job's exit status and log, and exits nonzero, naming the jobs, if any failed or any expected output is missing;
3. runs the scoreboard with and without the round's switches and, when there is more than one switch, once without each (leave-one-out); the lives measures (`story.ts`, `friends.ts`, `consequences.ts`) on and off; `ident.ts` with the switches off against a baseline it saves on the base branch; a round's own fault tool if one is given; `npm test`;
4. writes the cold-read pack first, before any metric: the most eventful lives from the cold-read worlds in each arm, shuffled and labelled by a code, the key in a separate file;
5. writes one summary: paired differences per world with 95% and 90% intervals and an equivalence verdict (90% interval inside ±2) on all decisions, decisions that are not a couple's, and the key slice; a warning when the key slice differs in size by more than a tenth between the arms; within each arm the calibrated witness against the notebook and the tendency learner, and its lift as a share of the distance from the base rate to the mind-reader; the leave-one-out table; the lives reports; the identity result.
   It changes nothing in `src/` and nothing an existing tool does. A separate agent still re-derives the headline numbers; the runner does not replace that.
   Test: run it on round F26's tree (`round/F26`) with F26's switches and seed ranges (re-running F26's measurement to reproduce it; it decides nothing, so no range is claimed), and once with a job made to fail.
   Expected:
6. Every number F26 recorded is reproduced exactly: on minus off −1.4 ±1.4 on decisions that are not a couple's and −0.9 ±1.2 overall; leave-one-out `countfair` −1.3 ±1.1; key slice within each arm +5.5 ±1.2 and +6.3 ±1.5; 17,979 against 11,001 decisions on the key slice; the fault counts in `reports/f26-faults-*.txt`; `ident.ts` differing only on the `budget` arm; the lives figures in `reports/story-f26.txt`, `friends-f26.txt` and `consequences-f26*.txt`; `npm test` passing.
7. With one job made to fail, the runner exits nonzero and names it, and the summary marks every number that rests on it as missing.
8. Wall time on four cores within 10% of the sum of F26's longest parallel stages, about 70 minutes.
   What would change our minds: any F26 number not reproduced means either the runner or F26's hand-run steps differ, and the difference is traced before the runner is used for any round.

Outcome of round T1 (18 Oct 2026, 19:40 Oslo). The runner on round F26's tree (`0bd9a6b`) with F26's switches and ranges, four jobs at a time; its summary in `reports/round-t1-f26-summary.md`.

1. Every number F26 recorded is reproduced ✓, with one exception found and fixed: the scoreboard (on minus off −1.4 ±1.4 not a couple's, −0.9 ±1.2 overall; key slice +5.5 ±1.2 on and +6.3 ±1.5 off; 17,979 against 11,001 decisions), the leave-one-out table (`countfair` −1.3 ±1.1, `honestev` +0.5 ±1.4, `identfix` +0.2 ±0.2, `eventfix` 0.0), the fault counts (71 of 6,344 and 52 of 6,471; 207 → 0; 404 of 6,875), the consequence map (23% and 23%, 10% and 9%), the identity check (only `budget/11` differs) and `npm test` all match to the figure. The friends table did not: the runner called `friends.ts report` without naming the two arms and got "0 worlds". Fixed (the arms are named), and a lives-only re-run reproduces F26's friends table exactly (median 3, top tenth 10, most 24, none 15%; made 0.29, ended 0.26). Two more things that run exposed: the raw lives outputs lived inside the frozen tree and went with it, so they are now copied beside the summary before the tree is removed (they go on the release); and an evaluation chunk may be one file or one per world (`board.ts` from round 25d writes world by world).
2. A job made to fail (a fault tool that does not exist): exit code 1, both jobs named, every number resting on them marked missing ✓. On a single world the verdict column printed ±0.0; the runner now says "too few worlds for a verdict" under three.
3. Wall time **47 minutes** on four jobs, against the expected "within 10% of about 70": the expectation was mis-stated (it summed stages that run in parallel), and the runner is faster than the hand-run battery by about a third (**✗** as written, in the good direction).
   Running it on round 25d's tree, after this outcome was written, found two more: the lives tools there also write one file per world, so a lives chunk may be either; and a registered worktree whose folder is gone is pruned before a new one is added. Both fixed.
   **Kept.** `scripts/round.mjs` is how a round's battery runs from here on; the cold-read pack it writes first is the pack a reader gets; `--skip board` gives a lives-only run.

## Round T3 — properties over many seeds (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 18:20 Oslo, before its code)

Process, not model. `npm test` checks the invariants on three fixed worlds. A deterministic engine whose only input is a seed is the ideal case for property-based testing: the same invariants, run over a range of seeds by `fast-check`, which also shrinks a failure to its smallest case. Round F26's lost thread events (1.8% of removals) and the self-story reading that let a weaker entry win were both invariant breaks that three worlds never showed. T3 adds `src/tools/props.ts`, a property suite over throwaway seeds (9201 and up, never a test range), with these properties, each checked at the end of the past and every present month:

1. the record is in time order, every event's people exist, and every event's `who` is on the own list of each person in it, once;
2. a decision's chosen answer is among its options, and every reason's `who` exists;
3. friendship is mutual among the living, and partnership is mutual;
4. the consistency audit (`audit.ts`) finds nothing;
5. nobody dead or departed decides, is hired, befriended or married after they died or left;
6. a bond's two ends exist, and no bond key exceeds the contract's bound;
7. the same seed twice gives the same signature (`sig.ts`'s), on every seed, not only one.
   Two runs: `npm test` gains a short one (5 seeds, 2 present years each, under two minutes); `npm run props` runs 50 seeds (9201–9250) with 3 present years, for a round that touches the engine.
   Expected:
8. The 50-seed run passes every property on main at `12b9186`, or every failure it finds is a real fault, recorded here with its seed and the shrunk case; no property is weakened to pass.
9. `npm test` passes and takes no more than two minutes longer.
   What would change our minds: a property that fails on main is kept as a known fault and listed in STATUS, not removed; if more than three properties fail on main, the suite is still kept and the faults become a round of their own before any engine round.

Outcome of round T3 (18 Oct 2026, 20:30 Oslo). `reports/props-t3.txt`.

1. The 50-seed run (9201–9250, three present years each, 1,200 s under load): every property holds except two real faults, both recorded and kept as named known faults in the suite and in STATUS (weakness 11): a group's event shares the group's live member list (`who: g.members` in `group.ts`), so who "started" it changes as members join and leave and later members never get it on their own list, 80 distinct events; and a dead member stays in a group's show for a month or two (seed 9211, Alex Holmes, "night:good" two months after dying), though the group code clears the dead monthly, so the cause is in the order of the month and is for the coherence round to trace. No property was weakened ✓. Two things the suite's own first run taught: an act the record does not keep is meant to be off every own list, so the property is "on everyone's list or on nobody's, and a kept event on everyone's", which still catches round F26's bug class; and a known fault is counted by distinct events, not by monthly re-checks.
2. `npm test` gains the 5-seed run: **127 s** on a machine running the 25d battery beside it (load 10 on 4 cores), so under two minutes unloaded is likely but not measured ✓ (provisionally; `reports/props-t3-5.txt`).
   **Kept.** `npm run props` is part of any round that touches the engine; a round that fixes a known fault removes its line, and the property then guards it.

## Round F27 — the coherence floor (pre-registered 18 Oct 2026, 22:15 Oslo, before its code)

Criterion 3 (nothing on a page claims what did not happen) and criterion 4. Every blind read since round 22 quotes the same kinds of fault, in both arms alike, and they cap the read: lives score 2.8–3.0 of 5 whatever the engine does underneath (F26's cold read: on 3.0, off 2.8). H26 took the wording faults to zero but could not reach two; T3 found two more in the engine. This round is not a mechanism. It is a floor: a check that counts every known fault type on every rendered life, the fixes, and the check kept in `npm test` so the floor cannot be crossed again.

The faults, each with where it comes from:

1. A remembered event given twice (H26: 17 → 14). Traced: for a non-solo act with a target, `run` in `src/grammar.ts` (about line 722) has the target remember the actor under the event's id, and the situation's own effect then remembers the same event again with its own words (`afterRun` in grammar.ts for welcome, straight talk, flattery, rescue and froze; `src/time.ts` for apologise; `src/life.ts` for going cool; `src/threads.ts` for confiding; `src/love.ts` for the repair joke). `remember` in `src/world.ts` pushes unconditionally, so the bond holds the event twice and its deltas apply twice. On one world after ten years, 188 bonds hold a doubled event. The fix: `remember` replaces an entry that already has the same event id (keeping the later, situation-specific words) and applies the deltas once. That changes bond numbers, so it is behind the switch and goes through the gate; this is the one fix in the round that can move the scoreboard.
2. "Nothing had changed since" dated earlier than "together since" (H26: 11 → 1).
3. A group's event holds the group's live member list (T3, 80 distinct events in 50 worlds). Traced: `src/group.ts` creates `group:formed` (about line 165), `group:hole` (684) and `group:split` (747) with `who: g.members`; `addEvent` spreads shallowly, so the event's `who` is the same array, and `recruit` (712) pushes into it. The fix: `who: [...g.members]` at the three sites (and, belt and braces, a copy in `addEvent`).
4. A dead person named at a project's night (T3, seed 9211: Alex Holmes, dead two months, in `night:good`). Traced: not a group fault. `src/projects.ts` (about lines 506–554) builds the night's `who`, its quality and its "N people pitched in" reason from `pr.helpers`, a list that is only appended to and never cleared of the dead or departed; the acts loop beside it already filters by `status === 'in'`. Deaths happen at step 1 of `tick`, the night later the same month or in a later one. The fix: filter the helpers once at the top of `night` and use that list throughout.
5. Reasons that do not fit the act: a stock tag ("would not take what was not his") put on an act it does not describe. Each tag is tied to the facets and situation families it may describe; a reason outside them is not written.
6. Reasons citing an event that never appears on the page. A reason may only cite an event the page shows or that the witness saw.
7. One goal dated two ways ("since 2020", "since 2021"). One date per want, taken from its first event.
8. Acts in a place or role the person has left (the sawmill after leaving it). H26 took the job case to zero; the place case is checked the same way.
9. Broken phrases: "head of the clinic at the clinic", "playing vocals", a secret said "out loud" without what.
10. Repeats a writer would cut: the same festival, loan, row or song said more than twice in a life with nothing new. The page keeps the first and the last, and says "again" once.
11. Near-identical names in one town (Susie Vale and Susie Hale; two Tom Howells). Generation rejects a name within one letter of a living person's, and a full name already in use (STATUS 4 and 8).
12. Friendships that end and restart without a cause on the page. The restart cites what changed, or does not happen that month.

The change, behind `OFF=floor` for everything that touches the engine (1, 3, 4, 11, 12); the page and reason fixes (2, 5–10) touch only words and are checked with `sig.ts`. Fix 1 is the only one that can move the scoreboard (bond deltas no longer stack); 3 and 4 change who is named, 11 and 12 change rare events. `src/tools/coherence.ts` counts each fault type over every rendered life of a world and prints a table; `npm test` runs it on the three fixed worlds and fails on any count above zero for a type this round took to zero. The T3 property suite drops its two known-fault lines when 3 and 4 are fixed.

Test: four fresh worlds, ten present years, before → after, for every fault type (the H26 method); `sig.ts` for the word-only fixes; the scoreboard paired on a fresh eval range with `floor` off against on; lives on a fresh range (dead lives, twists, the consequence map); a blind cold read of twelve fresh lives, on against off, by readers not told the arm; `npm run props`; `npm test`.
Expected:

1. Every fault type 1–12 at **0** after, on four worlds and ten years; before counts are recorded beside them.
2. `sig.ts` identical for the word-only fixes; the scoreboard with `floor` on within **±2** of off on the key slice (fixes 3, 4, 11 and 12 change who is in a few events, nothing more).
3. Dead lives within ±2 points; the consequence map within ±2.
4. The blind read: on arm at least **3.3** against off, and the readers quote none of the twelve fault types in the on arm.
5. `npm run props`: both known faults gone, every property holds on 50 worlds.
   What would change our minds: a fault type that cannot reach zero without a mechanism (say, 12 needs a reason to re-befriend) is left at its count, named, and given to the round that owns the mechanism; the floor is still kept for the others. If the blind read does not rise above 3.0 with all twelve at zero, the cap on reading is not coherence, and STATUS 3 ("the page reads as a list") becomes the next round's subject rather than more fixes.
