## Round Q3 — the human study page and its pilot (pre-registered 19 Oct 2026, 00:05 Oslo, before its code)

Criterion 5 (a blind outside reading where the page helps a reader rather than misleads). It waits for an interface people can use. This round builds it and runs a pilot; the pilot proves the page and the analysis, not the result. The protocol (`docs/study.md`, written with this round) is the human quiz's third sitting, redesigned after Quiz II made a skilled reader worse: a person meets the town, then answers items drawn from the scoreboard's key slice, half with the person's page and half without, in random order, each item answered once, the town's usual answer shown in both conditions, evidence counts shown with every impression, the reveal with the engine's reasons after every item, and at the end the tester's score beside the machine readers' on the same items. Nothing in the engine changes.

The change:

1. **Items.** `src/tools/quiz.ts` on twenty fresh worlds 2901–2920 with the engine as merged (round F27), base rates from 1501–1520: about 96 items on the key slice, no kind of situation more than a quarter of any tester's set.
2. **The page** (`study/`, one HTML file with the items inside, no server): 24 items per tester, drawn at random from the pool with the conditions balanced within the tester and, over testers, each item seen in both conditions; a confidence (guessing, fairly sure, sure) with every answer; time per item recorded; the end questions ("did the people feel like people", 1–5; one line; a second set offered). The tester's answers leave the page as one block of text they copy and send back, or a file they save; nothing is sent anywhere by the page. Phone and desktop.
3. **The analysis script** (`scripts/study.py`), committed before the first tester: the primary estimate (accuracy with the page minus without, a mixed model with testers and items as random effects, 95% interval), the exclusion rules of the protocol (median time per item under 5 s; one answer position on more than 80% of items), calibration, and the machine readers' accuracy on the same items. It runs on the pilot's files and on any later sitting's.
4. **The pilot:** three readers who have not seen the model, one sitting each. It tests the page (do they finish, where do they stall, what do they ask) and the script (does it run end to end on real files). Its estimate is reported as a pilot's and claims nothing.
   Expected:
5. The pool holds at least **80** items from at least 16 worlds, no kind over a quarter; the page runs the full sitting on a phone and a desktop with no script error, in the browser check (`scripts/browser-check.mjs` gains a pass over it).
6. The three pilot readers finish a sitting in **under 30 minutes** each, and their files parse and run through the script end to end.
7. The script reproduces, on a synthetic file where the answer is known, the planted effect to within one point.
8. The machine readers' accuracy on the pool: calibrated witness above the notebook, as on the scoreboard.
   What would change our minds: if a pilot reader cannot finish 24 items in 30 minutes, the sitting becomes 16 items and the pool is read accordingly before any real sitting; if readers say the reveal gives away the next items' answers (the same person again), items from one person are never adjacent. The result of the pilot (helps, misleads, not shown) is recorded and not acted on: the real sitting comes after round 27, on that engine.
   The warning in `WHY.md` this round risks: optimising internal metrics that say little about believability to human observers. This round puts the observers in front of the page; the risk it carries instead is a page that makes the model look better or worse than it is through its design, which is why the design choices are written down with their reasons and the next design (the raw ledger, no impressions) is named now.

Outcome of round Q3, part one: the page and the script (19 Oct 2026, 00:40 Oslo). The pool: `study/items.json`, 174 items from twenty worlds 2901–2920 (`src/tools/quiz.ts` on the engine after F27, base rates from 1501–1520); the page: `study/template.html` and `study/build.mjs` → `dist/study.html` (built by `npm run build`); the script: `scripts/study.py`; the protocol: `docs/study.md`.

1. The pool holds **174** items from **20** worlds (eighteen kinds; loss, provocation and support 25 each, the cap in packing); a sitting takes 24 with no kind over six and one item per person; the page runs a whole sitting on a phone-sized screen in the browser check, which `npm test` now runs, and hands back a block that parses ✓.
2. Waits for the pilot.
3. The script on synthetic testers with a planted 6-point effect: 400 testers on the real pool, two seeds, **+3.7** and **+5.6**; on a made-up pool, three seeds, +5.9, +7.8, +6.7. Right on average (mean of five +5.9 against 6.0), but a single run lands within two points, not one, because a synthetic sitting of 400 × 24 answers has about a point of noise of its own; "within one point" was too tight for one run ✗ as written, ✓ as a bias check. The interval the bootstrap gives at 40 testers is about **±12 points** (one synthetic run: +11.0 [−1.6, +22.4] for a planted 7), far wider than the protocol's power estimate, which came from a mixed-model calculation. So forty testers would not detect seven points with this estimator; the pilot will show the real spread, and the protocol's "how many people" paragraph is corrected to say so.
4. The machine readers on the pool: calibrated witness **63.2%**, notebook 60.3%, base rate 60.3%, page-evidence reader 57.5%; the witness above the notebook ✓ (by 2.9 points, less than on the scoreboard's key slice, as the pool is one item per person and the kinds are capped).
   Part two, the pilot, is added when three readers' files are in. The page and the script merge now, so the script is committed before the first tester, as the protocol requires.

## Round 27 — people who vary within themselves (pre-registered 19 Oct 2026, 00:25 Oslo, before its code)

Criterion 6 (readability holds, honestly) and principle 5 (people stay able to surprise). Round T2 measured how much one act says about the trait behind it: single acts predict the trait behind them at r = .42 and aggregates over many at .55 (round T2, twenty worlds), where people run .10 to .30 on one act and above .60 only over many (Mischel 1968; Epstein 1979; Fleeson 2001). The town's people are too consistent to be human, and every number downstream rests on it: the witness reads them from a few acts, surprises are rare and unexplained, and the lives read as the same answer given again. Fleeson's finding is that a trait is a distribution of states with a stable mean and a spread of its own; the mean is the person, the spread is also the person.

The change, behind `OFF=states`:

1. **A spread of one's own.** At generation each person draws a spread, about 0.6 of a facet's standard deviation (0.3 to 1.0), a little wider for the anxious (Fleeson and Gallagher 2009). It is a fact about the person, learnable like a trait: the witness can come to know someone as steady or as changeable.
2. **A state each month.** In a decision, every facet a consideration reads is the person's level plus a deviation for that month: spread × a draw from a stream of the person's own (the world seed or run seed, 'state', the person, the month, the facet). The draw is keyed, not taken from any shared stream, so a person's states move nobody else's dice, and switching states off leaves everything else identical.
3. **Said as a reason when it decided.** When the state on the facet of a decision's top reason is more than 0.8 of a standard deviation from the person's level, the reason says so, in the direction it went ("sharper than usual that month", "softer than usual"), so a reader who later learns the person can see why the act did not fit them. A state never changes world state on its own; it only weighs in a decision.
4. **Nothing else reads states.** Evidence from an act is unchanged (it rests on what was done, and on the average weights of round F26); manner, persona, the self-story and the witness's beliefs read the level, so beliefs are of the mean and converge more slowly, as they should.

Not changed: if-then signatures (the situational part of variation, already in), emotions and phases (states with a cause), the choice draw.

Test: fresh worlds 2921–2940 for the lives and T2's three patterns (`src/tools/patterns.ts`) with and without; the scoreboard paired on 1761–1780 with base rates from 1501–1520; `ident.ts` with `states` off against main; a blind paired reading (M26's method, three readers, α) of twelve people from 2921–2924, each shown with and without; the coherence counter on 2921–2924 in both arms; `npm run props`; `npm test`.
Expected:

1. `states` off: identical to main.
2. T2 check 1 with states on: single acts **.10 to .30**, aggregates still **at least .40**. Check 2 and 3 within their bands as before (states change no network and no judgement).
3. Readability falls, and is meant to: the calibrated witness on minus off, **−2 to −6 points** on the key slice; calibrated minus notebook on the key slice stays **above 0, interval above 0** (criterion 6 holds); the mind-reader, which sees states, keeps its lead over the trait oracle, which does not: the gap between them grows by at least 2 points (the price of character that varies).
4. Lives: twists per world up by at least a fifth (`test.ts`), lottery (surprises without a reason) not up by more than a tenth, because a state that decided is given as a reason; dead lives within ±2; the consequence map within ±2.
5. Paired reading: the on arm preferred in at least 7 of 12, α at least .667 (a surprise with a reason reads better than none).
   What would change our minds: if single-act r falls into the band only when aggregates fall below .40, the spread is too wide and people become unreadable; the spread's centre is lowered once, to 0.4, and that is pre-registered as an amendment before any further run. If lottery rises by more than a tenth, states are not reaching the page as reasons, and item 3 is wrong before item 2 is.
   An outside review (18 Oct 2026, reports/external/) asks whether people are too consistent because traits are too stable or because situations and interpretations are too thin. States are the cheap test of the first. If they bring single-act r into the band but the blind paired reading does not prefer the on arm (expectation 5 fails), the number was fixed and the reads were not, and the next round tests the second: a reason to act out of character given by the situation (a trusted friend's backing, a recent success, a change in money), not a state.

The warning in `WHY.md` this round risks: characters with elaborate hidden states that communicate too little for anyone to understand them. A state is a hidden state by construction; item 3 (a state that decided is said as a reason, in its direction) is the answer, and expectation 4's lottery measure is the check that it reaches the page. The outside review's challenge (section 8 of `reports/external/2026-10-18-consequential-lives.md`) is carried in "what would change our minds" above: states are the cheap test of the first explanation; if they fix the number and not the reads, context is tested next.

Outcome of round 27 (19 Oct 2026, 01:40 Oslo; tree `fe8d77e`, main at `957536a`). The battery (`reports/board-27-runner.md`, scoreboard paired on 1761–1780, lives on 2921–2940), T2's patterns on 2921–2940 in both arms (`reports/patterns-27-on.txt`, `-off.txt`), the paired reading (`reports/27-reading.txt`, tool `src/tools/diag/pairarm.ts`), the coherence counter (`reports/coherence-27-on.txt`, `-off.txt`), `npm run props` (`reports/props-27-on.txt`), the lives test on the same worlds (`reports/test-27-on.txt`, `-off.txt`).
One deviation from the letter of item 1, decided before any run: the spread is not a second draw but is set from the person's variability, drawn at generation with anxiety in it (`spreadOf`, 0.3 to 1.0, centre 0.6: 0.32, 0.60, 1.00 on the smoke world). It is the same fact about the person, written once.

1. `states` off: **identical** to main, all arms (`ident.ts`, default and `states`). ✓
2. T2 check 1: single acts **.36** with states against **.39** without (band .10 to .30); aggregates .50 against .53. ✗. A state of 0.6 of a standard deviation moves the correlation by the factor it should (1/√1.36 ≈ 0.86) and no more; to reach the band the spread would have to be the trait's own size, which is not what people have (Fleeson 2001 puts within-person spread near the between-person spread for a single hour, not a month's decision). The amendment the pre-registration allowed (lower the centre to 0.4) runs the wrong way and was not used.
3. Readability fell as meant: the calibrated witness, on minus off, **−2.5 ±1.4** on decisions that are not a couple's, −2.5 ±2.1 on the key slice (expected −2 to −6) ✓; calibrated minus notebook on the key slice with states **3.3 ±1.4**, interval above 0 ✓; the mind-reader's lead over the trait oracle grew from 3.6 to 7.1 points on the key slice (3.7 to 7.4 overall; expected at least 2) ✓. The number the round was for is the one it did not move.
4. Lives: dead lives 0.04 in both arms ✓; the consequence map 0.24 against 0.23 ✓; twists per world **23.4 against 21.4** (+9%; expected at least +20%) ✗, lottery **15.4 against 17.0** (down 9%; the bar was not up by more than a tenth) ✓: the states that decided did reach the page as reasons, and they made few new twists.
5. The paired reading ✗: three blind readers preferred the on arm in **4 of 12** pairs by majority (bar 7), mean paired difference −0.22 points on five, α ordinal **.728** [.467, .881] (bar .667). The state notes were quoted as faults seven times ("patience: short fuse, more so than usual that month": an unfit reason; "modesty: feels entitled to more, less so than usual": wording) and never as a reason that made an act make sense. The readers' preferences went to the version with the clearer arc, whichever arm it was: states changed the lives, and the changes were not read as character.
   Also found: births fell (births ÷ deaths 0.90 against 1.03 on twenty worlds; `npm test`'s six-world check 0.55 against its bar of 0.6, so the suite fails with states on, as it did with H26's `jobact`); the coherence counter found 4 stock reasons that do not fit the act with states against 0 without (a refusal given "would not take what was not hers"), the state having moved a decision to an answer whose stock reason belongs to another; `props` held on 50 worlds. A first pack of pairs matched people across arms by id and paired three wrong people, because the past differs between arms and the ids of those born or arrived in it shift; the tool now matches by name and birth, and the reading above is of the second pack (the first is not counted).
   **Not kept.** The code stays behind `ON=states` (off by default; the default is identical to main). Re-derivation (`reports/rederive-27.md`, a separate agent, its own code from the raw files): every number above agrees to the decimal (the paired means, the oracle and mind-reader rows, 4 of 12, −0.22, α .728, the pattern medians, 4 against 0 faults); its one note is that the intervals are t(19) × the standard error, as the runner computes them, not 1.96 ×, which would make them a tenth narrower and changes no verdict.
   What it says, against the outside review's challenge (section 8 of `reports/external/2026-10-18-consequential-lives.md`): states are the cheap test of "traits too stable", and at a size people have they move single-act r by .03 and the reads by nothing; the number T2 found is not made by stable traits alone. The next test is the second explanation, the situational part: a reason to act out of character given by the situation (a trusted friend's backing, a recent success, a change in money), which a reader can see on the page, which a state cannot be.

## Round H28 — the engine can move: portability contract, part one (pre-registered 19 Oct 2026, 18:49 Oslo, before its code)

Housekeeping, no change to behaviour. Øystein asked that the engine stay free to move to another language later (Rust or C#), and to sit under any game engine, without a redesign (issue #17). This round writes the rules for that and does the parts that change nothing the engine does. It moves no exit-criterion point directly; like H24 it is housekeeping, and what it protects is point 7: one engine, measured and, if it ever comes to it, ported as one, with a port provable against this one. WHY.md's warning it risks is building for an application before the model is good enough; it guards against that by adding no mechanism and touching no decision.

Steps:

1. **The contract:** `docs/scale/portability.md`, seven rules beside the scale contract (the boundary, the record as reference, the engine's own arithmetic, explicit order, typed state, plain data, content as data), and a line in CLAUDE.md that new engine code follows it. Rule 4 as written in #17 ("ties broken by id") would change behaviour wherever a stable sort now keeps input order, so it is written as the order the engine actually relies on: insertion order for maps and sets, stable sorts, nothing from hash order or locale; a port keeps those (insertion-ordered maps, stable sorts). An audit of the 79 sorts, 31 maps and the one `localeCompare` checks that nothing relies on anything else.
2. **The boundary:** `src/boundary.ts`. A world is created and advanced through commands, and read through events and queries, all plain data (no Map, Set or function in anything it returns). Nothing in the engine changes; the lab page and the tools keep reading the engine directly, as measuring instruments do. A test in `npm test` checks that a world run through the boundary hashes identical to one run directly, and that everything the boundary returns survives a JSON round trip unchanged.
3. **The record as reference:** `ident.ts record` writes the full event record of the identity seeds (11–13, eight years) as canonical JSON lines in `reports/reference/`, through the boundary, so a port can be diffed event by event, not only by hash.
   Not in this round: rule 3 (the engine's own `exp`, `log`, `pow`; 35 calls) changes the last digits of numbers and is a round of its own, judged by the gate; rules 5 (typed state: 174 `as any`, 107 uses of the `tried` store) and 7 (content as data) are later housekeeping rounds.
   Expected: `ident.ts check` identical on seeds 11–13 and every switch arm; `scale/identity2.ts` identical to main on seeds 1–3; `npm test` passes with the new boundary test; a separate agent, given the diff, finds nothing that could change behaviour.
   What would change our minds: any difference in either identity check means a step changed behaviour; it is undone and examined, never accepted.

Outcome of round H28 (19 Oct 2026, 19:45 Oslo). **Behaviour identical** ✓. Against a baseline saved on main at `s12` (every surviving switch as its own arm, 53 signatures), `ident.ts check` is identical on seeds 11–13 and every arm; `scale/identity2.ts` gives the same whole-world fingerprint on main and the branch on seeds 1–3; `npm test` passes with the boundary test in it. No engine file changed: `src/boundary.ts` is imported only by two tools. The committed `reports/ident-baseline.json` turned out to be stale on main as well (from before round H24: it named retired switches and fails `check` on main by hundreds of events per seed); round checks have used a baseline saved on their base branch (`scripts/round.mjs`), so no earlier verdict rests on it. It is replaced by the baseline saved on main.

1. **The contract** ✓: `docs/scale/portability.md`, and CLAUDE.md now says new engine code follows it beside the scale contract. Rule 4 is written as the order the engine relies on (insertion order, stable sorts, JavaScript's key order, code-point string comparison). The audit: the 84 sorts in engine files (counted without the page, `app.ts` and `page.ts`) all use pure numeric-difference comparators, and the helpers they call (`regard`, `likeOf`, `facet`, `inclination`) read and write nothing; the one `localeCompare` sorts the page's list of people, not a decision. The pre-registration's counts were taken with a looser grep; counted in engine files only, there are 33 calls to `exp`, `log`, `pow`, `tanh` and `cos` (not 35, which counted `sqrt`), 84 sorts, 49 Maps made, 106 uses of the `tried` store and 232 lines with `as any` (ARCHITECTURE said about 140; corrected).
2. **The boundary** ✓: `createWorld` and `command` in, `events`, `now`, `townIds`, `person`, `bondOf` and `beliefsHeld` out, all plain data. Building it found one thing JSON cannot carry: a negative zero (a facet presented at −0), now given as zero.
3. **The record as reference** ✓, written to `out-reference/` rather than the pre-registered `reports/reference/`: the three files are 8.4–10.4 MB, too large to rewrite in git with every round that changes behaviour, and the engine reproduces them exactly from any tag (two runs, byte-identical).
   Review: a separate agent, given the diff, re-ran the identity check against its own baseline from main (identical) and found nothing that changes behaviour. It found five things, fixed before this outcome: the boundary test took its "reading changes nothing" snapshot after the reads (now compared with a world nobody read); `events(h, since)` read the record from the end, which a city may not keep in time order (it now reads the whole record; STATUS issue 14 for the engine's own readers); `townIds` went through the city's index, which places people in neighbourhoods as it is built (it now reads the people directly); rule 6 did not name the random streams' class; and the counts above.
   **Kept.** Rules 3, 5 and 7 are known debt in ARCHITECTURE.md; rule 3 is a round of its own, judged by the gate.

## Round H29 — CI spends fewer minutes (pre-registered 19 Oct 2026, 20:15 Oslo, before its code)

Process, not model; no exit-criterion point, like T1. GitHub stopped starting jobs on 19 Oct (the account's Actions allowance for the month was spent), which also stopped H28's release. Of the last 40 CI runs, about 216 minutes in all, 7 runs (42 minutes, a fifth) were pushes that changed only NOW.md, and every run downloads the npm packages and installs Chromium with its system libraries from scratch. This round changes only `.github/workflows/ci.yml`:

1. CI does not start for a push or a pull request that changes only Markdown files, except `docs/seeds.md`, whose seed check CI runs. A round's own PR always changes code or BOARD with code, so it still runs; a NOW.md update does not.
2. npm's download cache and Playwright's browser are kept between runs (`actions/setup-node` cache, `actions/cache` keyed on the lockfile); on a hit only the system libraries are installed.
   Nothing in `src/`, `scale/` or the tests changes, so `ident.ts` is identical by construction (checked anyway).
   Expected, measured on the first runs once Actions start again: a push that changes only NOW.md starts no CI run; a pull request run with a warm cache takes at least one minute less than the median of today's pull request runs (about 6.5 minutes); `npm test` passes in CI as before.
   What would change our minds: a CI run that fails where main passed, or that skips when code changed, means the filter or the cache is wrong; it is fixed before anything else merges.

Outcome of round H29 (19 Oct 2026, 20:40 Oslo). The pre-registration's time (20:15) was written ahead of the clock; the pushed commit is 20:08. Two CI runs on this branch, once Actions started again (`ci` run 39300211067, attempts 3 and 4):

1. **Markdown-only changes start no run**: kept. The filter is in `ci.yml` for pushes to main and for pull requests; Markdown changes other than `docs/seeds.md` no longer cost a run. Of the last forty runs, seven (a fifth of their minutes) would not have started. It covers `ci.yml` only: the `identity` workflow added by PR #19 runs on every pull request event, Markdown or not, and is left as it is (another change's file).
2. **Keeping the browser between runs** ✗, and taken out. The cold run installed Chromium with its system libraries in 18 s; the warm run took 60 s to install the system libraries alone, from the cache. The whole job took 6.1 minutes both times, of which `npm test` is 5.3: there was never a minute to save outside the tests. npm's own download cache stays (`setup-node` `cache: npm`; the install took 3 s in both runs).
   What it says: CI minutes are the test suite's minutes. The way to spend fewer is to run CI less often (the filter), not to install faster.
   **Kept in part.**

## Round 28 — the engine's own arithmetic (pre-registered 19 Oct 2026, 20:20 Oslo, before its code)

Rule 3 of `docs/scale/portability.md`, the second part of issue #17. The engine calls `Math.exp`, `Math.log`, `Math.pow`, `Math.tanh` and `Math.cos` 33 times (in `build`, `coarse`, `decide`, `group`, `life`, `persona`, `regard`, `rng` and `world`). JavaScript leaves their last digits to the engine that runs it, so the same seed may not give the same world in another browser, another Node, or a port in Rust or C#. This round replaces them with the engine's own functions in `src/mathx.ts`, built only from addition, subtraction, multiplication, division and `Math.sqrt`, which IEEE 754 rounds exactly everywhere: `exp` (reduction by ln 2 split in two parts, a Taylor polynomial, exact scaling by powers of two), `log` (reduction to [√½, √2), the atanh series), `pow` for a positive base (`exp(y log x)`), `tanh` (through an exact `expm1` series for small arguments) and `cos` (reduction by π/2 split in two parts, Taylor series). Behind `OFF=mathx`, which restores the `Math` functions.
It moves no exit-criterion point directly; like H28 it protects point 7 (one engine that can be ported and proven against itself). The WHY.md warning it risks is complexity without progress: a change that alters every world's last digits for no change a reader can see. What shows it did not is the gate: it must change nothing that can be measured.
Seeds: eval 1781–1800, test 2941–2960, cold 2961–2962; train 1501–1520 as always.
Expected:

1. Accuracy: on a million arguments drawn over the ranges the engine uses, each function is within 4×10⁻¹⁶ relative error of the `Math` function (a check in `npm test`; the `Math` functions are themselves only near-exact, so this checks the new ones are as good, not that either is the true value).
2. With `OFF=mathx`, `ident.ts` is identical to main on seeds 11–13 and every switch arm.
3. Gate: the calibrated witness, on minus off, within ±2 on decisions that are not a couple's, paired over the eval worlds, the overall number beside it.
4. Lives (`story`, `friends`, `consequences` on the test worlds): no measure moves by more than its own interval between the arms; the change is noise by design.
5. Cold reads: a blind reader given lives from both arms prefers neither (within 4–8 of 12 either way).
6. Cost: a world (seed 9101, thirty years of past and ten of present) at most 10% slower than with `OFF=mathx`.
   What would change our minds: a systematic shift in any measure means the model is sensitive to last digits in a way that matters, and it is examined before anything is kept; a function outside the accuracy bound is fixed before any run.

Amendment to round 28 (19 Oct 2026, 20:25 Oslo, before any test or eval world has run). The pre-registration assumed that changing the last digits of every exponential would make worlds part ways within months, so that the round would be judged as a change to behaviour. The smoke runs say otherwise. On seeds 11–13 and 9101, thirty years of past and eight of present, the two arms have the same events, in the same order, with the same people, decisions and words; only stored numbers differ, by at most 2.5×10⁻¹⁵ in an option's probability and 7×10⁻¹⁶ in a bond. No decision sat close enough to a draw for the last digit to tip it. So the round is first judged as near-identical:

- a new check compares the full event record of the two arms (type, month, who, decision chosen, text) on all forty fresh worlds, the eval range 1781–1800 and the test range 2941–2960, with the same years as the battery would use;
- if every world's record is the same in both arms, the scoreboard, the lives and the cold reads cannot differ beyond the fifteenth digit, so they are not run, and the outcome says so;
- if any world's record differs, the full battery runs as first pre-registered, on the same seeds.
  The accuracy check (expectation 1) measures cos by absolute error, since cos crosses zero, where a relative error means nothing; the others by relative error, as written.

Outcome of round 28 (19 Oct 2026, 20:47 Oslo). The pre-registration's time (20:20) was written ahead of the clock; the pushed commit is 20:11, and the amendment 20:25, both before any test or eval world ran. **Same story, own digits** ✓.

1. **Accuracy** ✓: on a million arguments (seed 9301), the worst gaps to JavaScript's functions are exp 2.2, log 2.2, pow 0.5^y 3.1, pow x^1.4 3.5, pow x^1.5 3.5, tanh 3.7 and cos 1.1, all ×10⁻¹⁶, inside the bound of 4. Two of the functions were first over it (log 4.4, tanh 4.8) and were rebuilt before any run, as the pre-registration allowed: log with fdlibm's arrangement around f = m − 1, tanh with Lambert's continued fraction below 1. The re-derivation found what the bound hides: on five million draws tanh reaches 4.06×10⁻¹⁶ from Math.tanh, and measured against a fifty-digit reference the engine's functions are within 1.6 units in the last place everywhere (Math's within 1.9; the engine's tanh is the closer of the two). So the right claim is "within two units in the last place of the true value", and the test's bound holds for its fixed draws only.
2. **The switch restores the old engine** ✓: with `OFF=mathx`, `ident.ts` is identical to main on seeds 11–13 and all 53 switch arms.
   3–5. **The two arms tell the same story** on all forty fresh worlds (`src/tools/diag/samerecord.ts`, `out-round-28/`): every event, in the same order, with the same people, decisions and words. The largest gap in any option's probability is 1.8×10⁻¹⁵. By the amendment, the scoreboard, lives and cold reads cannot differ beyond that and were not run.
3. **Cost** ✓: seed 9101, thirty years and ten, 9.0 s against 8.8 s with `OFF=mathx` (three runs each, 3% slower; bar 10%).
   Re-derivation: a separate agent ran the accuracy check itself, compared eight of the fresh seeds with its own stricter script (every field of every event, every person's ties, job and home) and found every event the same, with gaps matching `out-round-28/` to the digit; it confirmed all 33 calls converted and the switch-off identity. It also found `pow`'s exactness falls off for large exponents and that a negative base, an infinite exponent or a NaN base give NaN (none reachable from the engine; now said in its comment), and two things a port must keep, now in `portability.md`: no fused multiply-add, and `Math.round` rounding halves up.
   What it says: the engine's decisions do not sit on the last digit of its arithmetic. Over forty worlds and four hundred years, no choice came within 10⁻¹⁵ of tipping. The worlds a seed makes are now the same wherever the engine runs.
   **Kept.** `mathx` joins the kept fixes; its switch and the `Math` path go in the next neutral change.

## Round 29 — accounts: what a bystander keeps (pre-registered 19 Oct 2026, 23:05 Oslo, before its code)

The first of the three accounts rounds (issue #20; renumbered 29–31, since 28 went to the arithmetic). Exit point 2 (events travel: at least 15% of weighty events reach someone outside the people involved; 6% today, 0% for friendships) and exit point 1 (friends, family and colleagues have only liking). Two human readers found the lives repetitive and lifeless while rating the people's logic 4 of 5; the consequence map says why: four in five weighty events lead to nothing the record can trace, and almost none reach a third person.

What the code has today: the person an act was done _to_ keeps a memory of it (`bond.mem`, up to twelve per pair), which a bond reason cites, friendship and dislike reasons quote, and an old wound can bring back. A bystander keeps nothing of the act: only trait evidence ("less reliable"), which shifts regard and so, untraceably, whom they later pick. Nobody can say "Grace never asked Ben again because she'd seen what he did to Tom", because Grace holds no such thing.

The change, behind `OFF=accounts`:

1. **A memory becomes an account.** The memory record gains `source`: whose version this is (`done`: it was done to me, every memory today; `saw`: I was there). The record keeps what happened; accounts keep what people hold about it. Nothing else about existing memories changes.
2. **Bystanders keep the act.** When a weighty event (`w ≥ 0.8`, the consequence map's own line; not texture) that others could see (`vis` public or local) is broadcast, each person it reached as `seen` who was not in it keeps an account of it in their bond toward the actor (`who[0]`), with `source: 'saw'`. Its effect on the bond is the event's evidence read as regard reads it (the admired facets: sincerity, fairness, altruism, diligence and the rest), scaled to 0.3 of the hit the person it was done to takes, so that seeing a thing changes how one feels about the doer, a little. The witness's trait evidence stays as it is: an account is a feeling about a person for an act, beside a belief about their traits.
3. **An account decides, and says so.** Everywhere memories are read today reads accounts: when a decision weighs the actor (to back, ask, hire, trust, invite, befriend), the bond reason cites the strongest account held, and a seen one is worded as seen ("had seen Ben back out of the festival"), never as if it were done to the holder. Whom a person picks as a counterpart changes through regard, which reads the bond. An old wound stays what it is: only an act done to oneself can come back as one.
4. **Forgetting, not a quota.** A seen account fades as memories do (`memWeight`: half-life 18 months for the negative scaled by forgiveness, 30 for the positive scaled by sentimentality). There is no cap on what can form; when a person holds more than 32 live seen accounts, the ones faded furthest are dropped first, so a dense month crowds out what was half-forgotten and never what mattered. A town never reaches the bound; a game month that does loses only what memory would.
5. **Nothing new is drawn.** Formation follows `knownBy`, which `broadcast` already fills from settings and reach (rule 4 of the scale contract: bounded per person, never a loop over the town); background people hold none. With the switch off nothing forms and nothing shifts, so the off arm is identical to main by construction.

Not changed: what the person an act was done to remembers, the trait evidence a bystander learns, gossip (round 30 makes accounts travel and bend), lessons (round 31). `version` and `blame` from the issue's table are **not** added: nothing in this round reads them, and state nothing reads is cut (the consequence rule). They come with round 30, where bending blame does work.

Test: fresh worlds **3027–3046** for the lives, the consequence map (`src/tools/consequences.ts`) and the what-ifs, with and without; the scoreboard paired on **2071–2090** with base rates from 1501–1520; `ident.ts` with `accounts` off against main; a blind paired reading (M26's method: three readers on another model, α) of twelve people from **3047–3050**, each shown with and without, matched by name and birth; the coherence counter on 3047–3050 in both arms; `npm run props`; `npm test` with the switch on and off.
Expected:

1. `accounts` off: identical to main.
2. **Reach:** weighty events reaching someone outside the people involved, **at least 12%** (6% on round 23b's worlds), and for friendships above 0; weighty descendants up by **at least 3 points** (22% before).
3. **It decides, not only cites:** of decisions that weigh a named other person, **at least 3%** have a seen account as the top reason (so the reason passes the counterfactual check: without it, the odds of the choice halve). A number near zero means accounts are held and never used.
4. **The gate:** the scoreboard paired, on minus off, within **±2** on decisions that are not a couple's, overall beside it. Calibrated minus notebook on the key slice stays above 0, interval above 0.
5. **Paired reading:** the on arm preferred in **at least 7 of 12**, α at least .667; readers quote a seen account as a reason that made an act make sense at least once, and never as an unfit reason.
6. Lives: dead lives within ±2; twists not down by more than a tenth; the coherence counter at zero in both arms; births ÷ deaths within ±0.05 of the off arm (round 27 found a change here by accident); `props` holds.
   What would change our minds: if reach rises (2) and the reading does not prefer the on arm (5), accounts are bookkeeping, not story, and the round is not kept however the numbers look. If the scoreboard falls by more than 2 (4), people are acting on others' deeds more than the witness can read; one amendment is allowed, pre-registered here: the bond effect in item 2 is halved once (0.3 → 0.15) and every number is rerun and recorded beside the first. If reach does not move (2), bystanders do not choose counterparts often enough for what they hold to matter, and the next round is about who chooses whom, not about memory.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: hidden states that communicate too little (an account is one; item 3 is the answer: every account that decides is said as a reason, as seen, with its event cited, and expectation 3 is the check that it reaches the page); forced drama (nothing forms except from an event that happened and was seen; no account is ever invented); complexity mistaken for progress (one field, one formation rule, one wording; expectation 5 decides whether it is progress, not expectation 2).

The game lens: a player should be able to say "Grace never asked Ben again, because she'd seen what he did to Tom". Promises remembered by those who were there, a reputation that is what people recall, and a murder with witnesses who saw different things all stand on bystanders who keep the act.

Amendment to round 29, decided on the smoke world (9002) before any test run (19 Oct 2026, 23:40 Oslo):

1. **Accounts form only from acts:** a weighty event that carries a decision, something someone chose to do. The smoke world formed accounts of states and outcomes ("has taken to the sea, like his father", "has got seriously good on the vocals", "has been making jokes about the break-up since the week it happened"), which are not something a person was there for, and no wording of "was there when" can be made true of them. This narrows the round and probably costs some reach; expectation 2 is judged as written.
2. **A memory the holder saw first becomes theirs when it turns out to have been done to them.** `broadcast` runs before an act's own effects, so a workmate the act was done to (the lead someone came through for) first got the bystander's share, and the act's own memory then found the event already held and kept that share. The direct memory now replaces the seen one and its effect on the bond (`remember`, world.ts). Without this the person an act was done to would feel it less than before the round, which the pre-registration never intended.
3. **The account's words are the record's first sentence,** without a note such as "It had happened before, in 2012", which belongs to the record.

Outcome of round 29 (20 Oct 2026, 00:55 Oslo; code tree `0e6c6fc`, battery tree `8d9b5d7`, main at `69aa573`, then main's `eb6fdae` merged for the suite). The battery (`out-round-29/summary.md`, attached to the release: scoreboard paired on 2071–2090, lives on 3027–3046), the round's own count (`src/tools/diag/accounts.ts` on 3027–3046), the paired reading (`reports/29-reading.txt`), the coherence counter on 3047–3050 in both arms, `test.ts` on 3027–3046 in both arms, `npm run props`, `npm test`.

1. `accounts` off: **identical** to main (`ident.ts`, three worlds, locally and in CI). ✓
2. Reach ✗: weighty events reaching someone outside the people involved **7%** with accounts against 5% without (bar 12%); weighty descendants **24% against 23%** (bar +3 points).
3. It decides ✗: a seen account was the top reason that passed the counterfactual check in **65 of 22,290** decisions with another person (**0.29%**; bar 3%); cited among the reasons at all in 535 (2.4%). People hold about 23 seen accounts each (19.7 to 25.1 an adult by world), and each is faint: a seen act moves the bystander's liking by a few hundredths.
4. The gate ✓: the calibrated witness, on minus off, **+0.1 ±1.4** on decisions that are not a couple's (+0.6 ±1.5 overall, +1.1 ±1.7 on the key slice); calibrated minus notebook on the key slice with accounts **+4.3 ±1.2**.
5. The paired reading ✗: three blind readers on Fable 5.1 preferred the on arm in **3 of 12** pairs by majority (bar 7); mean paired difference **−0.28** points on five (novelist −0.17, social worker −0.33, player −0.33); α ordinal **.710** [.466, .857] (bar .667). A seen account was quoted twice as a reason that made an act make sense ("though he thinks the world of Libby, having seen it happen") and never as a fault. Unfit reasons were quoted 31 times in the on arm against 13: on the pages, bond reasons that argued against the act rose from 2 to 12 ("Ivan took a little from the trip money. [though he thinks highly of Olive]"), and readers took them as reasons that do not fit.
6. Lives: dead lives 0.06 against 0.05 ✓; twists 24.9 against 23.2 a world-decade (+7%) ✓, lottery 15.4 against 15.8 ✓; the coherence counter at zero in both arms ✓; `props` holds on 50 worlds ✓; births ÷ deaths ✗ by the letter, and in opposite directions in the two tools that count it (test.ts 1.35 against 1.25; the friends tool 1.60 ±0.61 against 1.77 ±0.59), which is noise at this size, not a finding.
   Also found: (a) **`npm test` failed with accounts on** on a latent fault of main's: the default wording for high fairness was a money wording ("would not take what was not his"), and a world that took another path gave it to a refusal. Fixed on its own (#34, words only, behaviour identical) and merged into this branch; the suite passes. (b) **The paired reading is biased toward the arm it picks people from.** `pairarm.ts` takes the three most eventful adults of each world in the off arm and finds them again in the on arm; the on-arm versions had 15% fewer lines (648 against 767), while across all twenty worlds lives are not thinner with accounts (weighty events per life, median, 17.1 against 16.8). People picked for being eventful in one arm regress toward the mean in the other. Every paired reading so far (M26, 27, F27, 29) chose from one arm; how much this moved any verdict is not known. Round 29's verdict does not rest on it: the readers' own quotes say what failed.
   **Not kept.** The code stays behind `ON=accounts`, off by default; the default is identical to main (`ident.ts`, the default arm, in CI). Re-derivation (`reports/rederive-29.md`, a separate agent on Sonnet 5.5, its own code from the raw files): every number above agrees (reach 0.066 against 0.053; the gate +0.118 ±1.355, intervals t(19) as the runner computes them; 3 of 12, −0.278, α .710 by its own implementation too; 65 and 535 of 22,290 rerun exactly). Its notes: the 24 lives read are twelve pairs, not independent, so α's interval is probably a little narrow; the reading's worlds (3047–3050) are not the battery's (3027–3046), as pre-registered.
   What it says: holding the act is not enough. A bystander who keeps a faint memory of everything weighty they saw changes almost no decision on its own (0.29%), while the sum of many faint ones warms and cools feelings across the town, and that shows on the page as bond reasons nobody can follow. An account has to be held by fewer people, more strongly, about acts that bear on them, and it has to be said where it decides. Two things follow for round 30: (1) the reading's selection must be made from both arms (or from the shared past) before the next reading is run, a measurement fix that comes first; (2) telling an account (round 30's idea) gives it a reason to matter to the hearer: someone told something about a person they are about to deal with, by someone they trust, which is the situation in which a held account should decide.

## Round 30 — accounts travel: told, and read as the act (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 08:05 Oslo, before its code)

The second accounts round (issue #20), redesigned on what round 29 found. Exit point 2 (events travel: at least 15% of weighty events reach someone outside the people involved; 5–7% today) and point 1 (friends and colleagues have only liking). Round 29 gave every bystander a faint memory of every weighty act they saw, and it failed three ways that decide this design: the account never reached the page as an act (it spoke only through the bond's temperature, "thinks highly of Olive, having seen it happen"); whom a person picked by regard changed silently, cited nowhere; and a bystander already learns trait evidence from what they see, so the bond shift counted the act twice. Twenty-three faint accounts an adult decided 0.29% of choices and warmed and cooled feelings across the town.

Hypothesis: an account matters when it is **told** to someone who knows the person it is about, by someone they trust, and when the page gives **the act itself, with its source,** as the reason. Few accounts, each strong where it bears, every one of them readable.

The change, behind `OFF=telling`:

1. **Telling.** When a weighty act (`w ≥ 0.8`, an event with a decision, not texture, not private) has been seen or had done to oneself, the teller may tell it once, to **one hearer**: the friend or partner they like most who **knows the actor** (familiarity `bond.fam ≥ 0.2`, or a shared workplace, group or circle) and does not already hold an account of the act. Whether they tell is a draw on the social system's stream with probability `0.35 × z01(sociability)`, higher by half for an act done to the teller. The hearer may retell once more by the same rule, with the account's confidence multiplied by 0.6; a third telling does not happen. Background people neither tell nor hear.
2. **The account held.** The hearer's bond toward the actor gains a memory with `source: heard`, naming the teller (`from`), with `confidence` (0.7 at first hand, ×0.6 at second), `blame` (-1 to 1: how far the teller laid it on the actor rather than circumstance), and the record's words. It fades as memories do. A told account shifts **no bond**: it is read, never felt in the background.
3. **Telling bends.** The teller's `blame` starts at the act's valence in the town's eyes (as round 29 read it) and moves: liking the actor (bond like > 0.3) halves blame on them; disliking (like < −0.3) or an insincere teller (sincerity < −0.8) adds half again. **Wrongness**, rare: a teller with sincerity < −0.8 names the wrong actor with probability 0.15 (so at most a few per cent of all tellings): another person present at the event, else someone the hearer knows who shares a setting with the actor. The record keeps who did it; the page says whose version the hearer holds.
4. **Read as the act.** A told or done-to account is its own consideration (`accountC`) in every decision about the actor the engine already makes (backing a plan, answering a request, hiring, inviting into a group, putting in a word, befriending, lending, taking on a helper), weighed by confidence × fade × |blame|, and said as the act with its source: "had heard from Grace that Ben backed out of the festival" / "Ben had let her down over the festival (2031)". Where a counterpart is chosen by regard, regard gains an account part (the sum of live accounts' blame × confidence × fade, bounded), and the event that follows the choice cites the account that moved it. Round 29's seen accounts stay off (`ON=accounts`), and the bond reason's wording goes back to the bond alone.
5. **Nothing else changes.** Gossip's trait evidence stays as it is (the belief side); memories of acts done to oneself stay as they are, and gain the `blame` and `confidence` fields with their natural values (blame from the act's hit, confidence 1).

Test: fresh worlds **3051–3070** for the lives, the consequence map and the round's own count; the scoreboard paired on **2963–2982** with base rates from 1501–1520; `ident.ts` with `telling` off against main; a blind paired reading (three readers on another model, `pairarm.ts` ranking over both arms, PR #35) of twelve people from **3071–3074**; the coherence counter on 3071–3074 in both arms; `npm run props`; `npm test` on and off.
Expected:

1. `telling` off: identical to main.
2. **Reach:** weighty events reaching someone outside the people involved **at least 12%** (5–7%); for friendships above 0; weighty descendants up by at least 3 points.
3. **It decides:** of decisions about a named other person, **at least 3%** have an account (told or done-to) as the top reason that passed the counterfactual check, counted as its own kind of reason (`accountC`), not through the bond. Told accounts alone at least 1%.
4. **Held, and how many:** told accounts per adult over ten years between **1 and 8** (median over worlds); a number near 23 means telling is not bounded as item 1 says; near 0 means nobody who knows the actor is close to anyone who saw it, and the mechanism has no channel.
5. **Wrongness in a band:** held accounts whose actor differs from the record's between **0.5% and 4%** of told accounts; blame differing from the act's own valence by more than 0.3 in **10% to 40%**. Below the bands, telling does not bend and the town has one truth; above, nobody can be trusted and the page becomes noise.
6. **The gate:** the scoreboard paired, on minus off, within ±2 on decisions that are not a couple's; calibrated minus notebook on the key slice above 0, interval above 0.
7. **Paired reading:** the on arm preferred in **at least 7 of 12**, α at least .667; an account quoted as a reason that made an act make sense at least twice, and quoted as an unfit reason at most twice across all readers.
8. Lives: dead lives within ±2; twists not down by more than a tenth; the coherence counter at zero in both arms; `props` holds; births ÷ deaths within ±0.1 in `test.ts`.
   What would change our minds: if accounts are held (4) and decide (3) but the reading does not prefer the on arm (7), travelling accounts are noise, not story, and the arc stops here to be rethought. If reach rises and accounts rarely decide, reach came from something else and the round is not kept. If 4 lands below 1 the channel is too narrow; one amendment is pre-registered: the familiarity bar in item 1 drops once from 0.2 to 0.1, and every number is rerun and recorded beside the first. If wrongness (5) lands above 4%, the misattribution probability is not lowered after the fact: the round records it and the next pre-registration sets it.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: hidden states that communicate too little (an account decides only as its own reason, said as the act with its source; expectation 3 is the check, counted as that kind of reason); forced drama (nothing is told that did not happen; a wrong account is a person being wrong about a real act, and the page says so); complexity mistaken for progress (one kind of state, one channel, one reason; expectation 7 decides).

The game lens: "Grace never asked Ben again, because she'd heard from Tom what he did, and what she heard wasn't even true." A promise others remember, a reputation that is what people have been told, and witnesses whose versions differ all begin here.

2. Reach ✗: weighty events reaching someone outside the people involved **7.2% against 5.2%** (bar 12%; paired by world +2.0 ±0.5 points, clear of zero); weighty descendants **24.7% against 22.9%** (+1.8, bar +3).
3. It decides ✗: an account was the top reason that passed the counterfactual check in **94 of 22,990** decisions with another person (**0.41%**; bar 3%); a told account in 31 (**0.13%**; bar 1%). 6. The gate ✗: the calibrated witness, on minus off, **−1.3 ±1.4** on decisions that are not a couple's (not shown within ±2), −1.3 ±1.3 overall (a regression by the runner's verdict, p = .044, 11 of 20 worlds below zero), −1.8 ±2.2 on the key slice; calibrated minus notebook on the key slice with telling **+4.2 ±1.1** ✓. Choices moved by what a person was told are choices the witness cannot see coming.
7. The paired reading, mixed: three blind readers on Fable 5.1 preferred the on arm in **9 of 12** pairs by majority (bar 7) ✓, mean paired difference **+0.64** (novelist +0.50, social worker +0.83, player +0.58); α ordinal **.567** [.283, .764] (bar .667) ✗; a told account quoted **four** times as a reason that made an act make sense (bar 2) ✓ and **seven** times as an unfit reason (bar 2) ✗. The unfit ones are accounts weighed in decisions they do not bear on ("had heard from Larry that Eddie let people think the best of Olive's song was his", given for a decision about something else). No false line quoted in the on arm involves an account.
8. Lives: dead lives 0.04 against 0.05 ✓; twists 24.4 against 23.9 ✓, lottery 16.1 against 15.6; the coherence counter at zero in both arms ✓; `props` holds on 50 worlds ✓; births ÷ deaths ✗ by the letter, +0.15 in both tools that count it (test.ts 1.14 against 0.99; the friends tool 1.24 ±0.28 against 1.09 ±0.27), inside their own noise.
   **Not kept,** by the pre-registration's own rule: reach rose and accounts rarely decided. The code stays behind `ON=telling`, off by default; the default is identical to main. Re-derivation (`reports/rederive-30.md`, a separate agent on Sonnet 5.5, its own code from the raw files): every number agrees; the told-account count reran exactly. Its notes, carried here: the story gains are within noise (arcs per adult +0.12 ±0.13 paired by world), the reach gain is not; the median of told accounts per adult is 1.575 (the upper middle value is 1.59).
   What it says: a told account, said as the act with its source, reads as story; readers preferred the town that has them, all three, for the first time in the arc. What fails is the engine around it. An account is weighed beside every bond about its subject, so it is given as a reason where it does not bear, and it moves choices of a counterpart that no reason names; the first is what readers called unfit, the second is what the witness could not foresee. The next round keeps telling as it is and changes where an account is read: only in a decision it bears on (the kind of act it is about against the kind of choice: trust for a promise broken, money for a hand put in the hat, a post for a job done), and a choice of counterpart that an account moved is counted only when the choice, recomputed without it, goes to someone else, and then said as the reason.

## Round 31 — accounts bear, and change whom people deal with (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 10:40 Oslo, before its code)

The third accounts round (issue #20). Exit point 2 (events travel) and point 1 (friends and colleagues have more than liking). Round 30 built telling and it read as story: three blind readers preferred the town with told accounts in 9 of 12 pairs, the first such result in the arc. It failed on two mechanics of its own, both in where an account is read, not in telling: an account was weighed beside every bond about its subject, so it was given as a reason in decisions it has nothing to do with (seven unfit-reason quotes, every one of this kind); and an account moved the choice of a counterpart whenever it leaned on the ranking, counted 1,419 times without a test of whether the choice would have gone to anyone else, and said as a reason only as a lean. Telling itself held its bands (1.6 told accounts an adult, 2.2% misattributed, 26.5% bent) and is kept as built.

Hypothesis: an account should be read where it bears, and only there, and the choices it changes should be the ones it is said to have changed.

The change, behind `OFF=telling` (round 30's mechanism, read differently; the off arm is main):

1. **An act bears on an axis.** Every act already carries its effect on the three axes of a bond (`like`, `respect`, `trust` in `families.ts`). An account's bearing is the axis its act moved most, with any other axis it moved at least 0.3 of as much. An event with no act of its own (a want achieved, a night that went well) bears on the axis its evidence points at: sincerity and fairness on trust; diligence, organisation, prudence and courage on respect; altruism, gentleness, patience and forgiveness on liking.
2. **An account is weighed only where it bears.** Beside a bond consideration about its subject (`bond:<axis>:<id>`), an account is weighed only when its bearing includes that axis, with the strength of its effect on that axis, in that axis's direction. A song passed off as one's own (trust, respect) is weighed when someone decides whether to confide in or back its author, never when they decide whether to stay with them. Nothing else about `accountC` changes: the act with its source is the reason.
3. **A choice of counterpart counts only when it flips.** Wherever a counterpart is chosen by regard (a sort, a best, or a weighted draw), the choice is made twice on the same draw: with the account part of regard and without it. When the two agree, no account is cited. When they differ, the event that follows cites the account that made the difference, as the reason ("asked @12 rather than @7: had heard from Grace that @7 backed out of the festival"), and the flip is counted. The account part of regard is otherwise as in round 30 (0.25 × the bounded sum of live accounts).
4. **Nothing else changes.** Telling (who tells whom, bending, misattribution, fading, the bound) is round 30's, untouched. Round 29's seen accounts stay off.

Test: fresh worlds **3121–3140** for the lives, the consequence map and the round's own count; the scoreboard paired on **3075–3094** with base rates from 1501–1520; `ident.ts` with `telling` off against main; a blind paired reading (three readers on another model, people ranked over both arms) of twelve people from **3141–3144**; the coherence counter on 3141–3144 in both arms; `npm run props`; `npm test` on and off.
Expected:

1. `telling` off: identical to main.
2. **Where it bears:** across all readers, an account quoted as an unfit reason **at most twice** (seven in round 30), and as a reason that made an act make sense **at least twice**.
3. **It changes whom people deal with:** of counterpart choices made by regard among two or more candidates, **at least 1.5%** flip because of an account, each cited as the reason; and an account is the top reason that passed the counterfactual check in **at least 0.5%** of decisions with another person. This replaces the 3% top-reason bar of rounds 29 and 30, which both missed by an order of magnitude: a single decision's top reason is nearly always a trait or a circumstance, and the arc's claim was never that accounts outweigh character in one choice, but that they change whom people turn to. The flip count measures that claim; the bar is set here, before any run.
4. **Telling holds its bands:** told accounts per adult 1 to 8; misattributed 0.5% to 4%; bent 10% to 40% (a check that nothing upstream moved).
5. **The gate:** the scoreboard paired, on minus off, within ±2 on decisions that are not a couple's; calibrated minus notebook on the key slice above 0, interval above 0.
6. **Paired reading:** the on arm preferred in **at least 7 of 12**, α at least .667.
7. **Reach,** reported against the arc's target: weighty events reaching someone outside the people involved, with the 12% target beside it, and the off arm's figure. Reach is not in the keep rule below, and the reason is written here: items 2 and 3 are bound to cut the number of events that cite an account (round 30 cited a lean 1,419 times; a flip is rarer), and reach is the arc's goal, which lessons (round 32) and the consequences of acts serve, not this mechanism's own claim. A round that read well, decided choices and flipped nobody's reach would still have done what it set out to do; it is recorded as such, and reach stays the arc's open debt.
8. Lives: dead lives within ±2; twists not down by more than a tenth; the coherence counter at zero in both arms; `props` holds; births ÷ deaths within ±0.15 in `test.ts`.
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6 hold, with 4 and 8 as checks. What would change our minds: if 2 holds and 6 fails, accounts read well in isolation and still do not make a life worth more; the arc stops to be rethought. If 3's flips land below 0.5%, accounts do not change whom people deal with at the strength telling gives them, and strengthening them is a new round, not an amendment here (none is pre-registered). If 5 fails by more than 2, choices moved by what a person was told are choices the witness cannot see; the next round would have to let the witness hear what the town says, which is a design question, not a fix.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: hidden states that communicate too little (item 3 is the answer: a flip is said as the reason, and only a flip; item 2 keeps a said reason one that fits); complexity mistaken for progress (no new state; two rules about where an existing state is read; 6 decides); optimising a metric (the bar in 3 is replaced before the run, with the reason given, and the old bar is still reported).

The game lens: "Grace asked Tom, not Ben, because she'd heard what Ben did, and the page says so." A player watching Grace sees the account at the moment it changed something, and nowhere else.

Amendment to round 31, decided while building, before any test run (20 Oct 2026, 11:40 Oslo):

1. **Which choices are tested for flips.** "A counterpart chosen by regard" is taken to mean one person choosing among candidates by their own regard of each, and these six are wrapped: whom to ask for help or money (`request`), whom to confide in (`discovery`), whom an owner holds a failed plan against and whom an owner asks to help with one (`projects`), whom a group lead takes on (`group`), whom a lead hires (`work`). Not wrapped: selections of who comes forward by the candidates' own regard of a person (an ally standing up at a dressing-down, friends who come to support, a grudge's target, the hat's threshold), and the monthly draws of company and contact, which no event follows and which run thousands of times a month. Those are moved by an account as in round 30, untested and uncited; expectation 3 is measured on the six.
2. **Courage** has no facet of its own; an event's evidence on fearfulness or anxiety bears on respect.
3. **The account part of regard** sums the live accounts whatever their axis, as in round 30 (the pre-registration's item 3); only the decision-side reading (`accountC`) is limited to the axis it stands beside.

It still does not make a life worth more to a reader who does not know which version has it: round 30's readers, with the order balanced six and six, preferred the town with telling in 9 of 12; this round's, with the order unbalanced, in 7 of 12 that cannot be told from a preference for the second version, and neither panel agreed to α .667. By the pre-registration, 2 held and 6 failed: **the accounts arc stops here to be rethought.** Two things come before any next round on it: (1) the paired reading balances its order (six and six) by construction, not by the draw; (2) the question the arc did not ask: what a player sees. Telling, bearing and flips are hidden states that surface as one bracketed reason a year; the arc's next design has to start from the moment a player could notice that someone acted on what they were told, and work back to the mechanism.

## Round 32 — who carries the friendship (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 13:35 Oslo, before its code)

Exit point 1 (every close tie has an inner life that shows in what people do), and point 3's open debt (a page can show a friendship and, later, its end with nothing between). A friendship today is a flag on two people, four numbers on each one's bond and a list of memories; being a friend is one feature that raises the weight of helping. A couple has one shared state (tension, how rows have gone, drift, doubts), and that state is what made couples readable. This round gives a friendship one.

Diagnostic before the design (four throwaway worlds, 9301–9304, ten years; `src/tools/diag/carry-ledger.ts` and `carry-seek.ts`): favours between friends are rare (816 friendships, 642 acts of help, 191 let-downs; six in ten friendships had neither), so a ledger of favours would sit near zero almost everywhere. Effort is not rare and is lopsided: friends seek each other out about thirteen times a decade, and in a third of friendships one does three times the seeking of the other (a quarter with six or more contacts); where friends confide, it goes one way only in a third. The state worth building is who carries the friendship.

Hypothesis: a friendship has a balance of who puts in, the one who carries it notices when the gap is large and lasting, and whether they mind changes how they treat the other and whether the friendship survives; that shows on the page as a line the town knows and as the reason a friendship ends.

The change, behind `OFF=carry`:

1. **The balance.** Each friendship keeps what each has put in: seeking the other out (small, frequent), hearing them out when they confide (medium), turning up or helping when asked (large), and let-downs counted against the one who let the other down. Entries fade with a half-life of about three years, so the balance is about the friendship as it has been lately. The state is one record per friendship, read from either side.
2. **Minding.** The one who puts in more notices when the gap is large (the carrier has put in at least three times as much, over at least twelve entries' worth) and has been for a year. How much they mind is theirs: higher fairness minds more, higher altruism minds less. It is said in their words as a reason ("was always the one who came round"), with the years it rests on.
3. **Where it bears, and nowhere else** (round 31's lesson, fixed before the code): (a) asking a friend for something and being asked: the one who is owed finds it harder to refuse, the one who carries the friendship is readier to say no; (b) standing by a friend under strain; (c) the carrier's own seeking: one who minds seeks that friend out less, which is how a one-sided friendship ends, from the side that was keeping it alive; (d) the end: a friendship that fades this way says so, dated. The balance is not read in rows, hiring, plans, groups or any other decision.
4. **On the page.** A friendship one-sided for a year becomes a line the town knows, as a couple's pattern is: "Hal was always the one who went round to Ruth's." Its end: "Hal stopped going round. He had always been the one who did."
5. **Nothing else changes.** How friendships form, liking, familiarity, the contact budget and the memories are as on main.

Test: fresh worlds **3165–3184** for the lives, the consequence map, the friends tool and the round's own count (`src/tools/diag/carry.ts`: one-sided friendships, endings by cause, where the balance was a reason); the scoreboard paired on **3145–3164** with base rates from 1501–1520; `ident.ts` with `carry` off against main; a blind paired reading (three readers on another model, people ranked over both arms, order dealt half and half, #40) of twelve people from **3185–3188**; the coherence counter on 3185–3188 in both arms; `npm run props`; `npm test` on and off.
Expected:

1. `carry` off: identical to main.
2. **One-sided friendships exist and are not everywhere:** of friendships lasting three years or more, between **10% and 40%** are one-sided for a year at some point (today's proxy: 25–32% lean 3:1 in seeking).
3. **It changes what happens:** at least **15%** of friendship endings cite the balance as a reason (today none: "lost touch" says nothing); and the balance is the top reason that passed the counterfactual check in at least **1%** of request, obligation and strain decisions between friends.
4. **Nothing dissolves:** close friends per adult, median, within ±1 of main's; friendship endings per adult-year not up by more than half.
5. **The gate:** the scoreboard paired, on minus off, within ±2 on decisions that are not a couple's; calibrated minus notebook on the key slice above 0.
6. **Paired reading:** the on arm preferred in at least **7 of 12**, α at least .667; the balance quoted as an unfit reason **at most twice**, as a reason that made an act make sense **at least twice**.
7. **Point 3's debt, reported:** the share of friendship endings whose reasons name something dated that came before, in both arms.
8. Lives: dead lives within ±2; twists not down by more than a tenth; the coherence counter at zero in both arms; `props` holds; births ÷ deaths within ±0.15 in `test.ts`.
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 3, 5 and 6 hold, with 2, 4, 7 and 8 as checks. What would change our minds: if 2 holds and 3 fails, people notice and do not act at the strength given, and the next round is about minding, not a tune here (none is pre-registered); if 6 fails on unfit quotes, four places were still too many, and the next design reads the balance at the end alone; if 4 fails, the fade in 3(c) is too strong and the round is not kept however the rest reads; if 5 fails by more than 2, what one friend puts in is something the witness cannot see, and the pattern line is where it would have to show.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: complexity mistaken for progress (one state, read in four named places; 6 decides); hidden states that communicate too little (item 4 is the answer: the line the town knows, and the end that says why); forced drama (2 and 4 have ceilings as well as floors; a friendship that is carried and not minded goes on as before).

The game lens: "Hal was always the one who went round. Then he stopped, and the page says why." A player who follows Ruth sees a friendship she took for granted end, and can trace it back.

Amendment to round 32, decided while building, before any test run (20 Oct 2026, 13:55 Oslo; smoke worlds 9001–9004 only):

1. **The threshold was set on unfaded counts.** "Twelve entries' worth" came from the diagnostic's ten undecayed years (thirteen seeking contacts a decade); through a three-year half-life a friendship's live record is a median of 3 and a 90th percentile of 9, and at twelve the mechanism was inert (7 lines in four worlds; one-sided 1.2% of lasting friendships). Read through the half-life, twelve entries over ten years is about six live: the threshold is **six entries' worth, faded**. The ratio (three to one), the half-life (three years) and the year are unchanged.
2. **A friendship that goes quiet keeps its last judgement.** As first built, the record judged who carried a friendship afresh on every read, so once a carrier who minded stopped putting in, the record faded below the threshold and the friendship stopped counting as carried just before it ended: no ending could say so (0 in four worlds). Below the threshold there is nothing new to judge, and the last judgement stands until the record is large enough to judge again.
3. **Which decisions are "request, obligation and strain decisions between friends"** (expectation 3): the four where the record bears, by the situation's own name: `request-answer` (being asked for money or help), `obligation` (a promise coming due), `support` (a friend in trouble after a loss) and `notice` (a friend struggling, noticed). Item 3(a)'s "the one who is owed finds it harder to refuse" was a slip for **the one who owes**, which is what the rest of the sentence and the hypothesis say.
4. **What counts as putting in:** seeking the other out 1; hearing them out (being confided in) 2; asking what is wrong 2; visiting, turning up, giving, rescuing, speaking up for them 3; keeping a promise or giving grudgingly 2; chipping in or giving quietly 1. Let-downs take from the one who let the other down: kept away 3; refused, made excuses, let a promise slip, looked away or froze 2; had no wallet or kept quiet 1. The record never goes below zero.
5. **How much a carrier minds:** 0.5 + 0.25 × fairness − 0.25 × altruism (facets in standard units), between 0 and 1; a carrier minds when it is at least 0.5. In seeking, one who minds weighs that friend at 1 − ⅔ × that much.

Round 31 showed the same direction with a different mechanism (28% against 17%); the friends tool's young-adult count may be more sensitive to any change than its interval suggests, and is worth a look of its own before it is read as an effect.
