Neither result calls for a stronger dose here. The next step on close ties is about what a carrier who minds does, once, that a reader cannot miss (says it, asks for something back, or stops), not about how much the record weighs.

## Round 33 — the reckoning: what a friend who carries a friendship does about it (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 17:05 Oslo, before its code)

Exit point 1 (every close tie has an inner life that shows in what people do) and point 3's open debt (a friendship's end with nothing between). Round 32 gave a friendship a record of who puts in and found one lasting friendship in seven one-sided, but the record changed little that happens (4.4% of cooled endings said why) and three blind readers never quoted it: a line the town knows ("Hal was always the one who went round to Ruth's") is a statement of a pattern with nothing following it. The last four rounds (29–32) share that shape: a hidden state that reads well where it shows and is never noticed. Couples are readable because a couple's state comes out as acts nobody can miss (a row, a repair, leaving). This round gives the carrier an act.

Hypothesis: a friend who minds carrying a friendship does something about it, once, that the other has to answer, and that act, with what it rests on and what follows, is what a reader notices and what changes the friendship.

The change, behind `OFF=reckon`. The on arm is round 32's record (`carry.ts`, as built, with the two repairs below) plus the reckoning; the off arm is main, where the record is off. The arm that reads better is judged against main, as every round is, and round 32's own measurements say what the record alone did.

1. **The reckoning.** When a friendship has been one-sided for a year and the carrier minds (round 32's definition: fairness up, altruism down, 0.5 or more), the carrier faces it once: a situation `reckon` with three answers, chosen as every decision is chosen, from the carrier's traits, the bond and the situation.
   - **Says it** (honesty): "Hal told Ruth straight that he was always the one who came round." Ruth answers (a second decision): takes it to heart, or brushes it off. Taken to heart: for a year she seeks Hal out at twice her weight, and Hal remembers that she heard him out. Brushed off: Hal remembers that she did not want to hear it, and his liking falls.
   - **Asks for something back** (seeking): "Hal asked Ruth for something back, for once: help with the roof." Ruth answers as anyone asked by a friend answers (`request-answer`, with the pull of owing him). Given: the record is squared by what was given, and Hal remembers she came through. Refused: a let-down on the record, Hal remembers it, and the friendship is left to the record.
   - **Stops** (withdrawal): "Hal stopped going round to Ruth's." Hal seeks Ruth out no more for a year (round 32 cut it by two thirds for a carrier who minds; the reckoning's stop is whole), so a friendship kept alive from one side fades, and its end says so: "Hal had stopped going round. He had always been the one who did."
2. **What it rests on.** The record keeps the last six acts that put something in (their events: a visit, a promise kept, help given) beside its faded totals. Every reckoning, and every reason the record gives, cites up to three of them as the acts they are ("Hal looked in on Ruth most days (2029)"), with seeking, which has no event of its own, said as the line since its year. The review of `9a82092` (finding E) asked for this: a claim about a friendship a player can trace.
3. **Once, and not everywhere.** A friendship has at most one reckoning in three years; a carrier who does not mind has none. The answer to a reckoning is one decision; nothing else is added.
4. **Repairs to round 32's record,** from the same review, written here before the code: (B) the record is cleared at every place a friendship ends (death, leaving, a betrayal, a thread's cost), not only where it cools; (C) when the other friend becomes the carrier, the town's line is written again for them.
5. **Nothing else changes.** Round 32's record, where it bears, and the line the town knows are as merged.

Test: fresh worlds **3209–3228** for the lives, the consequence map, the friends tool and the round's own count (`src/tools/diag/reckon.ts`: reckonings and their answers, what followed within a year, endings by cause over every path); the scoreboard paired on **3189–3208** with base rates from 1501–1520; `ident.ts` with `reckon` off against main; a blind paired reading (three readers on another model, people ranked over both arms, order dealt six and six) of twelve people from **3229–3232**; the coherence counter on 3229–3232 in both arms; `npm run props`; `npm test` on and off.
Expected:

1. `reckon` off: identical to main.
2. **It happens, and not everywhere:** of friendships one-sided for a year whose carrier minds, at least **60%** have a reckoning within the next year; reckonings per world-decade between **3 and 30** (a town of about 70 adults).
3. **It changes what happens:** within a year of a reckoning, in at least **60%** of cases one of these follows: the other's share of the record rises so that it is no longer one-sided, the carrier got what they asked, or the friendship ends. And at least **15%** of cooled endings cite the record (round 32: 4.4%), with the share over every ending path reported beside it.
4. **Nothing dissolves:** close friends per adult, median, within ±1 of main's; friendship endings per adult-year (every path) not up by more than half.
5. **The gate:** the scoreboard paired, on minus off, within ±2 on decisions that are not a couple's; calibrated minus notebook on the key slice above 0.
6. **Paired reading:** the on arm preferred in at least **7 of 12**, α at least .667; the reckoning or what followed it quoted by readers at least **3** times in all (round 32's record: 0), as a reason that made an act make sense at least **twice**, and as an unfit reason **at most twice**.
7. Reach and point 3's debt, reported: weighty events reaching someone outside the people involved; the share of friendship endings whose reasons name something dated that came before.
8. Lives: dead lives within ±2; twists not down by more than a tenth; the coherence counter at zero in both arms; `props` holds; births ÷ deaths within ±0.15 in `test.ts`.
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 3, 5 and 6 hold, with 2, 4, 7 and 8 as checks. What would change our minds: if 2 holds and 6 fails on "quoted at all", an act between friends is not what readers read for, and close ties stop until the reading itself is rethought (what readers are asked to look for); if 6 holds on being quoted and fails on preference, the act is seen and does not make the life better, and the next round asks why (the answer's consequences are too small, or the wrong people reckon); if 3 fails on what follows, a reckoning is an event without a consequence, and the next design starts from the answer; if 5 fails by more than 2, a friendship's reckoning is a choice the witness cannot see, and it would have to be public.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: forced drama (one reckoning in three years, only for a carrier who minds; 2 and 4 have ceilings); complexity mistaken for progress (one situation and one answer, on a record that exists; 6 decides); hidden states that communicate too little (the act is the answer, and it cites what it rests on).

The game lens: "Hal told Ruth straight that he was always the one who came round. Ruth took it to heart, and that spring it was Ruth at Hal's door." A player following either of them sees the reckoning the month it happens, can open the acts it rests on, and sees what Ruth did about it.

Amendment to round 33, decided while building, before any test run (20 Oct 2026, 17:05 Oslo; smoke worlds 9001–9004 only):

1. **How often a reckoning comes up.** A carrier who minds and may face it (one-sided for a year, no reckoning in three) faces it in a given month with chance one in four, the friendship minded most first; it then competes with the month's other situations, as every situation does. A friendship can be reckoned with again three years later if nothing has changed, and the page then says it had happened before, as it does for any act repeated toward the same person.
2. **The answers.** Says it: the other takes it to heart (warmth, weighed with their liking and respect for the carrier) or tells the carrier it was nothing (withdrawal). Asks back: the other answers as anyone asked by a friend answers (`request-answer`, with round 32's pull of owing the carrier), for one of four plain favours (a hand with the roof, a day of help with the garden, a hand moving the furniture, the loan of the car for a day). Taken to heart: the other seeks the carrier out at twice their weight for a year. Stopped: the carrier seeks the other out not at all for a year.
3. **What follows**, for expectation 3, is counted only for reckonings with a full year left in the window: the record stops being one-sided (`balanced`), the carrier was given what they asked (`given`), or the friendship ends by any path (`ended`), whichever comes first.
4. **The acts cited** are the carrier's last different acts on the record, up to three, each said as the first sentence of its event.

   What it says: the reckoning was seen, the first act between friends in this arc that readers noticed, and it did not make lives better; it made them worse where it repeated and where the record's pull to give or withhold was read as a reason that does not fit. By the pre-registration ("6 holds on being quoted and fails on preference"), the next close-ties round would ask why: the answer changes too little (a friendship reckoned with goes on as before and is reckoned with again), and the claim behind it is the record's, an accumulation of small contacts that readers never see as acts. The arc's two rounds (32, 33) and the accounts arc's three (29–31) share one lesson: a state built from many small things, however it is said, adds reasons faster than it adds story. Close ties pause here. The next work is the reading itself (24 pairs, judged on the effect with the order taken out; the next entry), and then consequences that last (exit point 2), a change to what happens rather than to what is said.

## Round M34 — a reading that can tell (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 18:40 Oslo, **after** its code, which only reads answers already given)

Seven rounds that aimed at lives (25d, 27, 29–33) were not kept, and the blind paired reading decided or co-decided every one. Two things were wrong with it as an instrument. It was small: twelve pairs, so a real effect of +0.4 on five showed above zero about three times in four. And it was judged on the wrong things: a majority of pairs, which the order of the two versions moves (readers score the version they read second about half a point higher), and the readers' agreement (α), which describes the panel, not the change.

The change (tools and the method only; nothing the engine does changes):

1. **A committed scorer,** `scripts/pairscore.py`, in place of the scoring each round wrote for itself: the majority, the paired score difference with its interval, the effect with the order as a second term (difference = effect + lean × (+1 if the on arm was B, −1 if A)) with its 95% interval and one-sided 90% lower bound, α (reported, not a gate), faults by kind in each arm, and the quotes matching the round's own wording.
2. **Twenty-four pairs** (eight fresh worlds, three people each), written in two parts of twelve so a reader reads twelve at a sitting (`pairarm.ts`); three readers each read both parts.
3. **How a round uses it** (`docs/method/reading.md`): a pre-registration claims either "reads better" (the effect's 90% lower bound above 0) or "reads no worse" (above −0.3), and names its bars for its own wording quoted as good and as unfit.

Expected: (1) the scorer reproduces every published reading number of rounds 29–33 (majority, mean difference, each reader's difference, α); (2) with the order as a term the per-pair standard deviation falls from about 0.85 to about 0.7; (3) `npm test` passes and nothing the engine does changes (label `refactor`).

Outcome (the same evening, before any new reading):

1. Reproduced ✓: rounds 29 (3 of 12, −0.28, α .710), 30 (9, +0.64, .567), 31 (7, +0.14, .604), 32 (8, +0.14, .656) and 33 (3, −0.08, .612), every reader's difference included.
2. The order as a term ✓: per-pair standard deviation 0.85 raw, 0.73 with the order taken out (0.64, 0.62, 0.70, 0.69, 0.99 by round).
3. Rounds 29–33 scored this way:

| Round            | Pairs, on arm B | Majority on | Effect, order as a term (95%) | 90% lower bound | Lean to second | α ordinal |
| ---------------- | --------------- | ----------- | ----------------------------- | --------------- | -------------- | --------- |
| 29 seen accounts | 12, 6           | 3           | −0.28 ±0.41                   | −0.53           | +0.44          | .710      |
| 30 telling       | 12, 6           | 9           | **+0.64 ±0.40**               | **+0.39**       | −0.58          | .567      |
| 31 accounts bear | 12, 9           | 7           | −0.13 ±0.52                   | −0.45           | +0.54          | .604      |
| 32 who carries   | 12, 6           | 8           | +0.14 ±0.44                   | −0.13           | +0.53          | .656      |
| 33 the reckoning | 12, 6           | 3           | −0.08 ±0.64                   | −0.48           | +0.14          | .612      |

**Kept.** What it says: only round 30 read better by a margin the reading can stand behind; it failed its own rule on how often accounts decided and on reach, and on α, which is no longer a gate. Its verdict stands (nothing is re-judged after the fact), but telling is the one mechanism of the last five rounds that readers preferred, and the first to bring back. Round 31's 7 of 12 was the order: with it taken out the effect is −0.13. From the next round on, readings use 24 pairs and this scorer.

## Round 34 — aftermath: a blow changes something that lasts (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 19:20 Oslo, before its code)

Exit point 2 (most weighty events change something besides a feeling within three years) and STATUS weakness 3 ("big events that change nothing in the person they happen to"). Seven rounds in a row (25d, 27, 29–33) added a state that surfaces as reasons; none was kept, and readers' complaint about today's lives is that they reset: a death, a sacking, a friendship's end are recorded, mourned for a month, and then the person goes on exactly as before. This round changes what happens after a blow, not what is said about it. Øystein agreed the direction on 20 Oct.

Hypothesis: when a blow lands, the person decides once whether anything changes, and some do change course (work, a routine, the people they see); lives in which blows leave a lasting mark read better.

The change, behind `OFF=aftermath`:

1. **Blows.** An event is a blow to someone when it is, for them: the death of their partner, a child or parent of sixteen or over, or one of the two friends they liked most (`death`); their marriage or partnership ending when they were the one who still cared more (`divorce`, `split`); a friendship ending likewise (`cooled`); being let go from work (`dismiss`), passed over for a post (`passed`), found out (`caught`), dressed down or having the past dragged up in front of everyone (`dressdown`, `rake`); a want they pursued failing (`want:failed`).
2. **The aftermath.** The month after a blow, the person (sixteen or over, in town, no aftermath in the past year; the heaviest blow of the month if several) faces one decision with up to four answers, chosen as every decision is chosen, from their traits, the blow and the situation:
   - **start again at work** (courage): if they have a job and are under 62, they walk out of it to start again somewhere else ("Hal walked out of the boatyard to start again somewhere else"), and look for work as anyone without a job does;
   - **take something up** (openness): a new routine, the best fit they do not have ("Ruth now swims in the harbour every morning, whatever the weather"), which brings its own company;
   - **keep away** (withdrawal): if they have a social routine, they drop it, the one they shared with the person the blow came from if there is one ("Hal is no longer at the Anchor every Friday");
   - **carry on** (reluctance): nothing changes, and nothing is written.
     The act is written as its own event, with the blow as its reason, and the change it makes stays until life changes it again.
3. **What weighs:** the blow's weight favours a change; a blow at work favours starting again; disliking the person the blow came from favours keeping away; the family's own traits do the rest (boldness and fearlessness, inquisitiveness, low sociability, sentimentality).
4. **Nothing else changes.** Mourning, support and the coping situation that follow a death or a sacking are as they are.

Test: fresh worlds **3253–3272** for the lives, the consequence map, the friends tool and the round's own count (`src/tools/diag/aftermath.ts`: blows and their aftermath, and for every blow in both arms, whether the person's life changed within three years: their job, their partner, their routines, the music they live for, their group, or their being in town); the scoreboard paired on **3233–3252** with base rates from 1501–1520; `ident.ts` with `aftermath` off against main; the blind paired reading of round M34 (`docs/method/reading.md`: 24 pairs from **3273–3280**, order dealt half and half, three readers on another model, scored with `scripts/pairscore.py`); the coherence counter on 3273–3276 in both arms; `npm run props`; `npm test` on and off.
Expected:

1. `aftermath` off: identical to main.
2. **It happens, and not for everything:** between **20 and 150** aftermath decisions a world-decade; between **20% and 70%** of them change something (not carry on).
3. **Blows change lives:** of blows, the share after which the person's life changed within three years is at least **10 points** higher with the aftermath than without, paired by world, with its 95% interval above 0.
4. **Nothing dissolves:** births ÷ deaths within ±0.15 in `test.ts`; adults without work (not retired, not students) not up by more than 3 points; population within ±5%.
5. **The gate:** the scoreboard paired, on minus off, within ±2 on decisions that are not a couple's; calibrated minus notebook on the key slice above 0.
6. **Paired reading, "reads better":** the effect with the order as a term, one-sided 90% lower bound above **0**; the aftermath's own acts quoted as a reason that made an act make sense at least as often as as an unfit one.
7. Reported: the consequence map (weighty descendants, turns, reach) in both arms; α and the majority.
8. Lives: dead lives within ±2; twists not down by more than a tenth; the coherence counter at zero in both arms; `props` holds.
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 3, 5 and 6 hold, with 2, 4, 7 and 8 as checks. What would change our minds: if 3 holds and 6 fails, lives that change after a blow do not read better, and what readers want from a life is not what the exit criterion asks (a question for the criterion, not another mechanism); if 2 holds and 3 fails, people choose a change that does not last, and the doors (work, routines) are the problem; if 4 fails, starting again at work empties workplaces faster than the town fills them, and that door is closed to all but work blows.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: forced drama (a carry-on answer that writes nothing, and a ceiling on how often); complexity mistaken for progress (one decision on blows the engine already has, through doors it already has; 6 decides); consequences that occur without becoming visible (the act is its own line, with the blow as its reason).

The game lens: "After Tom died, Ruth started swimming in the harbour every morning, and that is where she met the people she spends her days with now." A player sees a blow, sees what it did to someone, and can follow where it led.

Amendment to round 34, decided while building, before any test run (20 Oct 2026, 19:50 Oslo; smoke worlds 9001–9002 only):

1. **Starting again at work answers a blow that came through work.** As registered, it was open to anyone with a job after any blow, and the smoke run showed people walking out of the council office because a friendship had drifted: the door did not fit the blow. It is now open after being passed over, being found out, a shaming at their own workplace, or a want they pursued failing. Taking something up and keeping away stay open after any blow.
2. **A blow is followed for each person once a month** (the heaviest), as registered; the round's count follows every such blow in both arms, including those that come within a year of an aftermath and so bring none.
3. **What weighs**, as built: the blow's weight (its event weight over 2.5, favouring the three changes), a blow at their own work (favouring starting again), and disliking the person the blow came from (for keeping away). The routine taken up is the best-fitting one they do not keep (a social one first if it fits nearly as well); the routine dropped is a social one, the one they share with the person the blow came from if there is one, else their most recent.

Outcome of round 34 (20 Oct 2026, 21:05 Oslo; code tree `a6e9afd`, battery tree `ed04f69`, main at `af1fc4c`). 2. It happens, and not for everything ✓: **1,458** aftermath decisions in twenty worlds, **72.9** a world-decade (band 20 to 150); **54%** changed something (band 20% to 70%): took up a routine 445, carried on 667, kept away 200, started again at work 146.
3. Blows change lives ✓: of blows followed for three years, the person's life changed in **67.3%** with the aftermath (811 of 1,206) against **50.0%** without (612 of 1,223); paired by world **+17.1 ±3.8** points (bar +10, interval above 0), higher in all twenty worlds. Most of it is routines (648 against 445) and work (252 against 163); partners too (150 against 92).
4. Nothing dissolves: births ÷ deaths in `test.ts` 1.07 against 1.01 ✓; population 82 against 84 (−2.4%) ✓; adults of 18–64 without work, not retired, at the end of the ten years **8.4% against 9.2%** (paired −0.7 ±2.5 points; bar not up by more than 3) ✓. Hires 20.9 against 12.5 a world-decade: those who walk out are taken on elsewhere.
5. The gate ✓: the calibrated witness, on minus off, **−0.5 ±1.2** on decisions that are not a couple's (90%: −1.5 to 0.5), −0.4 ±1.3 overall, −0.4 ±1.7 on the key slice (equivalent within ±2, the first time on that slice since round 31); calibrated minus notebook on the key slice with the aftermath **+4.4 ±1.1** (without, +5.2 ±1.1).
6. The paired reading ✗: **24 pairs**, the order dealt twelve and twelve; with the order as a term the effect is **−0.12 ±0.41**, one-sided 90% lower bound **−0.38** (bar above 0); the on arm preferred by majority in 11 of 24; α ordinal .626. Of the 36 aftermath acts in the pack, none was quoted as a reason that made an act make sense and **five** were quoted as unfit reasons, four of them a routine taken up with a trait as its reason: "Eddie Reed now plays cards at the Anchor on Tuesdays. [Eddie Reed and Stella lost touch.; that is who Eddie Reed is: not made for one place; wanted to know]".
7. Reported: the consequence map, weighty descendants 0.23 against 0.23, turns 0.10 against 0.09, reach 0.05 against 0.05: a change made in the aftermath is a change of state (a routine, a job), and the map counts only events that cite the blow.
8. Lives ✓: dead lives 0.05 against 0.05; twists 26.7 against 22.5 a world-decade (+19%); weighty events per life 17.9 against 16.9; the coherence counter at zero in both arms; `props` holds on 50 worlds.
   **Not kept,** by the pre-registration's own rule: 6 fails. The code stays behind `ON=aftermath`, off by default; the default is identical to main. Re-derivation (`reports/rederive-34.md`, a separate agent on Sonnet 5.5, its own code from the raw files): every number agrees; the round's own count reran exactly in both arms. Its notes, carried here: the +17 points counts the aftermath's own acts among the changes, so it shows the acts take hold, not that blows change lives by other paths as well; the witness is right on 49.8% of aftermath decisions against 62.3% of the rest, which is all of the scoreboard's dip (without them the gate's differences are +0.2, +0.6 and +0.2); arcs per adult fell, 0.40 against 0.51 (own-obstacle arcs 0.25 against 0.34, p = .04), and splits rose, 4.8 against 3.1 a world-decade, neither explained by the raw files; adults aged 18–29 with no friend in town fell from 25% to 16%.
   What it says: this is the first round in nine that changed what happens in a way the exit criterion asks for. Blows now change lives (+17 points, in every world), the scoreboard holds, nothing dissolves. And the reading, the strongest it has been, cannot say the lives read better: −0.12 with an interval of ±0.41 is no effect either way. The pre-registration named this case: if 3 holds and 6 fails, what readers want from a life is not what the exit criterion asks, and that is a question for the criterion, not another mechanism. The readers' own quotes add one thing the criterion does not decide: the change is right, the reason given for it is not. A routine taken up after a death, explained by "wanted to know" or "not made for one place", reads as a trait looking for an act. Whether a change after a blow should be explained by the blow and the person's state (grief, shame, needing people) rather than by a trait is a design question worth one more look before the criterion is changed; it is written here, not pre-registered.

## Round 35 — the page keeps what matters (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 21:35 Oslo, before its code)

The plan's first step (SUMMARY, Next 1a; exit points 3 and 5). Over the six blind readings of rounds 29–34, readers faulted today's lives (the off arms, 252 lives) for repeats more than anything else: 208 of 754 faults, led by the town's and a group's routine ("The summer festival that … put on was the best night the town had had in years", 21 times; "told … to count him in for the summer festival", 11; a band's songs and nights). A life's page today shows every event the person is named in: 39 lines a life over ten years, of which friendships made (2.5) and ended (1.3), another member's song brought to the band, and the hat going round for someone are a large share. The page already folds a line repeated word for word; it does not fold routine that differs only in who and when, and it shows a person events they only stood beside.

Hypothesis: a page that shows what the person did and what was done to them, with routine folded, reads better than one that shows everything they were named in. This changes the page only; the world is untouched.

The change, behind `OFF=upkeep` (`src/page.ts`, used alike by the app, the reading packs, `read.ts` and the coherence counter):

1. **What the person did and what was done to them.** A line in which the person is neither the one who acted nor the one it was done to (another member's song brought to their band, a helper's name on someone else's festival) is shown only if it weighs 1.5 or more (a death in the family, a wedding) or a line of their own cites it (shown under that line, as now). Events everyone named took part in (a group's show, its forming or splitting, a night someone put on with helpers) count as their own.
2. **Routine folds by kind.** For the town's and a group's routine and the small upkeep of a life (the yearly day and nights put on, pitching in or not, a group's work and shows, the hat, everyday moments, what was told and noticed), a line of the same kind and the same part (did it, had it done to them, took part) within three years of one shown is folded into it, said as "much the same N more times, to YEAR".
3. **Friendships made and lost with nothing between.** A friendship that begins and ends on the page with no other line between the two people is left out, its beginning and its end.
4. **Nothing else changes:** the words of every line, its reasons, the cited events shown under it, and the folding of lines repeated word for word.

Test: the blind paired reading of round M34 (`docs/method/reading.md`): 24 pairs from **3281–3288**, each person's page rendered with the change on and off from the same world (the worlds are identical; only the page differs), order dealt half and half, three readers on another model, scored with `scripts/pairscore.py`. Also on those worlds: lines a life in both arms; every event a shown reason cites is shown; the coherence counter in both arms; `ident.ts` and `npm test` (the world is unchanged).
Expected:

1. The world is identical with the change on and off (`ident.ts`; only the page reads the switch).
2. **A page that folds, not one that hides:** lines a life fall by between **20% and 50%**; no shown reason cites an event the page does not show.
3. The coherence counter at zero in both arms.
4. **Reads better:** the effect with the order as a term, one-sided 90% lower bound above **0**; repeats among the faults readers name fall; the "much the same" notes quoted as a fault (false line or wording) **at most twice**.
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 2, 3 and 4 hold. What would change our minds: if 2 holds and 4 fails, repetition is not what makes the page read as a list; the next round (reasons a reader can follow) goes ahead and this one's code waits behind its switch to be read again with it; if readers fault the folding notes, the routine should be left out rather than summed.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: a world internally consistent and still empty (the page may now show how little happens: 4 decides); hidden states that communicate too little (a page that shows less: 2 has a floor as well as a ceiling).

The game lens: a player opening someone's page sees their life, not the town's calendar: what they did, what was done to them, and the town's routine once, with how often it came round.

Round 35 withdrawn before its test (20 Oct 2026, 21:15 Oslo). No code was written and no test world was run: worlds 3281–3288 stay unused. (The pre-registration's header says 21:35; it was pushed at 20:52, `1e01827`, which is its timestamp.) Two findings, the first from smoke worlds and the second from the six readings already on file, each enough to sink the round's own test:

1. **The fold does not fold.** On throwaway worlds 9001–9008, for the three most eventful adults of each world (as the reading picks them), the page has 73.9 lines a life. Rules 1 and 3 (another's act, a friendship made and lost with nothing between) take off 2%; rule 2 as registered (routine of one kind and part within three years) 9% in all; routine of one kind over the whole ten years 13%; the same words over the ten years 7% (`reports/35-fold.ts`, `35-fold.txt`). Expectation 2 asks for 20–50%. The routine readers quote (the festival, "count me in") is real, but it is a small share of a page: what a reader calls a repeat is spread over every kind of act and across years, the same person doing the same kind of thing.
2. **Readers prefer the page where more happens.** Over rounds 29–34 (84 pairs, 504 single choices), when the two versions were at least five lines apart, readers chose the longer one 150 times and the shorter 63 (70%), every reader and every round alike; each ten lines more in the on version moved the score difference by **+0.16 ±0.07** (`reports/35-length.py`; the packs of rounds 29 and 30 are now committed beside the others). A page made shorter by folding is expected to read worse, not better, unless what it folds is what readers object to, and finding 1 says it mostly is not.

The second finding also says something about the reading as an instrument. The two arms of an engine change are different worlds, and the people read differ between them by 16–28 lines (sd) in every round: a good part of each verdict is which arm happened to give its people more to do. Each round's effect with the difference in lines as a third term: 29 −0.15, 30 +0.55, 31 −0.16, 32 +0.21, 33 −0.03, 34 −0.10 (against −0.28, +0.64, −0.13, +0.14, −0.08, −0.13 with the order only). No verdict changes. The reading will report the difference in lines and the effect with it as a term beside the effect it is judged on (round M35); the judgement stays on the effect, since a change that makes more happen is meant to read better for it.

What changes: the plan. Its first step rested on readers' repeats being what holds the page back. Repeats are the fault readers name most when asked for three, but what they choose is the life where more happens. The order in `SUMMARY.md` now puts substance first: who wants things (proposed work 1; round 25d's version read better to all four of its readers, +0.14 to +0.41, at a size the twelve-pair reading of the time could not confirm), then reasons a reader can follow (the page, judged on the same worlds in both arms, the cleanest test the reading has), then the rounds that changed lives brought back.

## Round M35 — the reading reports how much happened (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 21:40 Oslo, **after** its code, which only reads answers already given and changes what the pack shows to what the app shows)

Round 35's withdrawal found that readers choose the version of a life where more happens (70% of their choices over rounds 29–34; +0.16 a pair for every ten lines more), and that the same person's two pages differ by 16–28 lines (sd) between the arms of an engine change, which are different worlds. It also found, while checking the pack against the app, that the pack shows reasons the app does not. The change (tools and the method only; nothing the engine does changes):

1. **How much happened,** in `scripts/pairscore.py`: it reads the pack (`--pack`, or `pairs.txt` beside the key) and reports the lines in each arm (not counting the cited events shown under a line), how often the longer version was chosen where the two are five or more lines apart, and the effect with the order and the difference in lines as terms, with its interval and the change per ten lines. Reported beside the effect a round is judged on, which stays the effect with the order as a term (`docs/method/reading.md`): a change that makes more happen is meant to read better for it.
2. **The pack shows what the app shows.** `pairarm.ts` and `read.ts` printed a line's first three causes, so a "despite" note on the strongest consideration against the act ("though he thinks the world of Alan", "despite: nothing much had built up"), which the app and the coherence counter leave out, reached readers on about one line in eleven that has reasons (528 of 5,677 on throwaway worlds 9001–9002). Both now print `shownCauses`, as the app does.

Expected: (1) the scorer reproduces round 35's per-round figures from the committed files (rounds 29–34: the effect with the lines as a term −0.15, +0.55, −0.16, +0.21, −0.03, −0.10) and every number it reported before; (2) `npm test` passes and nothing the engine does changes (`ident.ts` identical; label `refactor`).

Outcome (the same evening, before any new reading):

1. Reproduced ✓: with the order and the lines as terms, rounds 29–34 give −0.15 ±0.48, +0.55 ±0.41, −0.16 ±0.50, +0.21 ±0.40, −0.03 ±0.57, −0.10 ±0.35, ten lines more +0.09 to +0.20 in every round; every earlier figure (majority, effect with the order as a term, each reader, α, faults) is unchanged.
2. `npm test` passes (coherence counter at zero) ✓; the engine is untouched (CI's identity check on the pull request).

**Kept.** From the next reading on, the pack carries no "despite" notes the app hides, so faults quoted from them stop; readings before this one are not comparable on that one point.

## Round 36 — wants that answer what happens (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 21:35 Oslo, before its code)

The plan's first step (SUMMARY, Next 1; exit points 2 and 5). Readers choose the life where more happens (round 35: 70% of their choices), and today 53% of adults pursue nothing in ten years (throwaway worlds 9001–9006): a want comes from a few narrow routes, half of them making up after a falling-out. Round 25d gave a yearly want from values to those with none open; 55% pursued something, a pursued want changed lives (+17 points of major change), and all four readers scored it higher at a size the twelve-pair reading could not confirm. Its reasons were dispositions ("had always wanted"), the kind readers fault. Two more things the design's smoke runs found (9001–9011): 94% of adults meet, in ten years, something that could give a want (someone close buys something, a round birthday, a baby, a wedding, retirement, being passed over, a split, a death close by, a band they see play), so occasions are not scarce; and the doors are shut where money is concerned: the median adult has savings of 106 against a house at 45, but a want for a thing is barred to anyone with more than half its cost put by ("the rich just buy it"), and nobody buys anything without wanting it. So 8% of adults own a house.

Hypothesis: when wants answer what happens to a person and around them, through doors that are open to them, more lives have an arc a reader can follow (why they wanted it, what they did, what got in the way, how it ended), and the lives read better.

The change, behind `OFF=occasions` (new `src/occasions.ts`; `time.ts`, `steer.ts`, `world.ts`):

1. **Occasions.** Each month, for everyone 16 or over: the events of the last month the person was in or knows of, and the stages of their life (turning 30, 40, 50 or 60; retiring; a baby; a wedding; the last child turning 18). A table maps each occasion to the want it can open, open only if the person holds the value it serves (a core value, or held strongly) and its door is open to them: someone close bought a thing they do not own → that thing; someone else made lead where they work → that post (not the lead, two years in, under 62, success or status); a band they saw play, with an interest of their own → to be heard (skill enough, in no group, under 55); passed over → a post at another place; a split, a death close to them, their band breaking up → to find their people (fewer than two friends), the big trip, or to be heard again; let go → work, for anyone; a wedding or a baby → a house (security, loved ones or tradition); a round birthday, retiring, the last child grown → a car, a post, a boat, to be heard, the trip, to find their people, by value; a post won → a house. Each row has its chance (0.15 to 0.6). One occasion of a type per person in three years; one want an occasion. The want records its occasion (`Want.origin`: the event or stage, and its words).
2. **Wanting, without flooding.** At most three open wants of one's own, never two of a kind; a want not gone after in three years lapses (said only if it had been told: "Hal has stopped talking about the boat"); a want achieved does not come back for five years.
3. **Going after it.** The start offers the most pressing open want, not a random one. The occasion is a consideration in the decision to start (public, so others can read it) and the first reason the line gives, then the want and its date, then the deciding reason. A wish is let slip once, soon after the want comes, in the occasion's words; a pursuit is talked about at most twice.
4. **One door opened.** A thing they can afford can be bought: buying it is a way of going after it (with prudence and generosity weighing against), one line, not "started saving" then "bought".

Unchanged: every other way, obstacle and outcome of a pursuit (asking, working for it, looking elsewhere, saving, extra work, family, practising, the open night, turning up to things, aiming lower, a want that comes back only changed). Leaving town is not a door (round 25d's demography failure); the trip is.

Test (scripts/round.mjs, as round 34): the scoreboard paired on fresh worlds **3289–3308**; lives, what-ifs ("a want:" and "how to go about it", both arms the same nudge), the consequence map, demography, `r25c.ts` and the round's own count (`src/tools/diag/occasions.ts`: wants by occasion, pursued, way, outcome, open wants per adult, adults pursuing, lines a life; both arms) on **3309–3328**; `npm test`, the coherence counter, props. The blind paired reading (`docs/method/reading.md`, scored by `scripts/pairscore.py` with the lines reported) on **3329–3336**: 24 pairs, three readers on another model, read before any other number of the round.
Expected:

1. `occasions` off: identical to main (`ident.ts`).
2. Adults who pursue something in ten years **62–72%** (47% on smoke worlds today; 25d 55%). Pursuits started 85–100 a world-decade (about 53), 60–75 not counting making up (about 30).
3. At least **60%** of wants started carry an occasion; things bought 10–18 a world-decade (about 2).
4. What-ifs: going after a want changes the chooser's life majorly at least **+10** points more often than chance.
5. Lines a life for the three most eventful adults of a world: **+4 to +9**.
6. Gates: the scoreboard within ±2 (decisions that are not a couple's, overall beside it); population within ±5%, departures within ±30% and births to deaths within ±0.15 of the off arm; the coherence counter at zero; `npm test`.
7. **Reads better:** the effect with the order as a term, one-sided 90% lower bound above **0** (the effect with the lines as a term reported beside it); lines that give an occasion as their reason quoted as a good reason at least twice as often as an unfit one; a want quoted as "out of nowhere" at most twice.
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 2, 6 and 7 hold. What would change our minds: if 2 and 4 hold and 7 fails while occasion reasons are quoted as fitting, more arcs do not make a life read better, and the exit criterion's assumption about consequences is in question for wants as it was for blows (round 34); if readers call the wants that follow someone else's purchase unbelievable, that row goes and the rest stay; if the scoreboard falls past −2, the decision to start is the cause, and a want should start without one; if 2 stays under 55%, open doors, not occasions, are the limit, and the next round adds doors (places, things to start), not wants.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: too much forced drama (a want on every birthday: the chance, the value and the open door gate it, and a want shows only when it is let slip or acted on); complexity mistaken for progress (one table over doors that exist; the reading decides); events that change nothing (a thing bought is kept and used; the what-ifs count it).

The game lens: a player sees why someone started wanting something (the day their friend got the job, the night they turned forty and watched the band), what they did about it, and how it came out; and can see it coming, because people let slip what they want before they act.

Round 36 withdrawn before its test (20 Oct 2026, 22:10 Oslo). Its code was built and run on throwaway worlds only; no test world was run, and 3289–3336 stay unused. The code is kept behind a switch that is off (`ON=occasions`).

Built as registered, with four repairs found on the smoke worlds and made before anything else was run: the cap on open wants had been applied to every way a want comes, which cut the ones the engine already gives (making up after a falling-out fell by a fifth), so it now applies to the wants occasions open; the most pressing want was offered again every time it was put off, so no other want ever came up, and now a want put off is not offered first for a year; couples bought two houses (both had the wedding and the baby as occasions), so a house is a couple's and its door is shut while the partner has one or wants one, and someone who has bought a house is no longer said to live in the one they grew up in; and the row for being let go is not built, because being let go already gives everyone a want for work (`work.ts`).

On throwaway worlds 9001–9008, with the switch on and off (`src/tools/diag/occasions.ts`, `reports/36-smoke-on.txt`, `-off.txt`):

|                                                          | on          | off         | registered     |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | ----------- | ----------- | -------------- |
| adults who went after something in ten years             | 56%         | 46%         | 62–72%         |
| pursuits started a world-decade (not counting making up) | 60.5 (39.9) | 51.9 (28.5) | 85–100 (60–75) |
| new wants with an occasion                               | 27%         | —           | at least 60%   |
| things bought a world-decade                             | 15.6        | 4.0         | 10–18          |
| adults owning a house                                    | 27%         | 8%          |                |
| page lines, the three most eventful adults of a world    | 74.6        | 73.6        | +4 to +9       |
| page lines, every adult                                  | 37.7        | 35.9        |                |

`npm test` passed with it on (demography: births 8.1, deaths 10.3 a world-decade, population 77 → 78), and the coherence counter stayed at zero.

Why it was withdrawn: three of its expectations, one of them in the keep rule (2), fall well short on eight worlds, so the test could not keep it whatever the reading said; and the reading could hardly see it, because it reads the three most eventful adults of each world, whose pages grew by one line. What it showed:

1. **It reaches people, but not enough of them.** Ten more adults in a hundred went after something; things bought four times as often, houses three times. What holds it back, among the 44% who still went after nothing (`reports/36-never.ts`, `36-never.txt`): 43% are over 65, and the doors the table opens (a post, being heard, a house) are mostly shut to them; 22% were busy with a pursuit started before the ten years, since a person goes after one thing at a time; and 27% had a want they never started.
2. **Occasions are a minority of wants.** Occasions opened 24 wants a world-decade; the engine's own routes (making up, a post, work) still give 64.
3. **A change that reaches the many is invisible to a reading of the busiest.** The paired reading reads the three most eventful adults of each world, who already have pursuits. A round meant to reach the quiet majority has to be read on random adults (exit point 5 asks for random lives too).

It also found, on the readings already on file, what readers score (`reports/36-variety.ts` rebuilds the lives each reading showed, every page line for line the one read; `36-variety.py`, `36-variety.txt`): **how many different kinds of thing happen in a life, not how long its page is.** Over the 84 lives of today's engine in the six readings of rounds 29–34, each kind of line more goes with +0.045 (±0.013) on the score, and once kinds are counted, length adds nothing (−0.011 ±0.011 a line; the round and kinds explain 47% of the spread in scores, the round and length 28%). Within the 46 pairs whose two versions could be rebuilt exactly, the version with a kind more reads +0.05 (±0.02) better. Occasions add 0.9 kinds a life to every adult and none to the three busiest of a world (42.0 against 42.2; `reports/36-kinds.ts`, `36-kinds.txt`). Round 34's aftermath added three kinds a life to the busiest and read no better on those pairs (−0.07), where the kinds alone would predict about +0.15: its reasons were what readers faulted ("wanted to know" for taking up a routine after a loss).

What changes: the plan. The next round brings back round 34's aftermath, giving the reasons readers accept for a change in someone's life (the blow, how hard it hit, the bond) and no trait cue. Who wants things comes back after it, designed for the kinds of happening it adds to a life, with more than one pursuit at a time and doors for later life, and read on random adults beside the busiest.

## Round M36 — the reading reports kinds of thing (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 22:35 Oslo, **after** its code, which changes what the reading reports, not what it shows)

Round 36 found that readers score how many kinds of thing happen in a life (+0.045 ±0.013 a kind of line across 84 lives, +0.05 ±0.02 within 46 pairs), not its length. The reading reports lines (M35); it now reports kinds too. The change (tools and the method only; nothing the engine does, and nothing a reader is shown, changes):

## Round 37 — aftermath, with reasons that fit (pre-registered 20 Oct 2026, 22:22 Oslo, before its code)

The plan's first step (SUMMARY, Next 1; exit points 2 and 5). Readers score a life by how many kinds of thing happen in it (round 36: +0.05 a kind, across lives and within pairs). Round 34's aftermath (a blow changes something that lasts: start again at work, take up a routine, keep away from one) added three kinds a life to the busiest lives and raised the share of blows that change a life within three years from 50% to 67%, in every world; it read no better (−0.12 ±0.41; −0.07 on the pairs that can be rebuilt, where its kinds alone would predict about +0.15). What readers faulted were its reasons: on throwaway worlds 9001–9003 with `ON=aftermath`, of the reasons its 230 decisions gave after the blow itself, 150 are trait cues ("wanted to know" for taking up cards after a loss, "would rather deal with it alone") and 86 the self-story ("that is who Arthur is: rooted in the town, through and through"), the two kinds readers quoted as not fitting a change in someone's life. What they accepted: the blow, how hard it hit, the bond ("had not forgiven it").

Hypothesis: a blow that changes something, given the reasons a reader accepts for a change, reads better than a town where blows change nothing.

The change, behind `OFF=aftermath` (on for this round's test; `aftermath.ts`, the reasons an act gives in `grammar.ts`):

1. **Round 34's aftermath, unchanged in what it decides:** the heaviest blow of a month (a death close to someone, a marriage or friendship ending when they cared more, being let go, passed over, found out or shamed, a want that failed) is faced once, the next month: start again at work (after a blow that came through work), take up a routine, keep away from one, or carry on (no line).
2. **The reasons it gives:** the blow, then how hard it hit, then what ties the change to the blow: that it happened at work (starting again), or that the person the blow came from is there too (keeping away from a routine they share). No trait and no self-story among the reasons shown; the decision record keeps all of them, as every decision's does.

Test (scripts/round.mjs, as round 34): the scoreboard paired on fresh worlds **3337–3356**; lives, the round's own count (`src/tools/diag/aftermath.ts`: blows faced, answers, lives changed within three years, in both arms), the consequence map and demography on **3357–3376**; `npm test`, the coherence counter, props. The blind paired reading (`docs/method/reading.md`; scored by `scripts/pairscore.py`, with lines and kinds of line reported) on **3377–3384**: 24 pairs, three readers on another model, read before any other number of the round.
Expected:

1. `aftermath` off: identical to main (`ident.ts`).
2. Lives changed within three years of a blow at least **+10 points** over the off arm (round 34: +17).
3. No aftermath line gives a trait or the self-story as a reason (counted on the reading's worlds); kinds of line a life, the reading's people, on minus off **+1.5 to +4**.
4. Gates: the scoreboard within ±2 (decisions that are not a couple's, overall beside it); the coherence counter at zero; demography and `npm test`.
5. **Reads better:** the effect with the order as a term, one-sided 90% lower bound above **0**; aftermath lines quoted as an unfit reason **at most 3** (round 34: 9), and quoted as a good reason at least as often.
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 2, 4 and 5 hold. What would change our minds: if 5 fails while its lines are no longer quoted as unfit, the kinds it adds are not the kinds readers want (a routine taken up or dropped is texture, not a turn), and wants (Next 2), which add turns with stakes, come before any more consequences of this sort; if its lines are still quoted as unfit, the fault is in the acts (which change follows which blow), not the reasons, and the round says which.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: hidden states that communicate too little (reasons cut back to what a reader accepts may say less than the decision weighed: the record keeps all of it); events plentiful without changing anything important (a routine taken up is a small change: 2 measures whether lives change).

The game lens: after a blow, a player sees someone change, and why: Ralph stopped going to the choir after Terry tore into him at the garage, because Terry sings there too.

The pre-registered "what would change our minds" fires as written: the round's lines are no longer quoted as unfit, and 5 fails, so the kinds of change it adds (a routine taken up or dropped, a job left after a shaming) are texture, not turns, and wants with stakes come before any more consequences of this sort. Two notes for that: in the 17 pairs whose on version shows an aftermath line the mean difference is +0.26, in the other 7 −0.48 (divergence, not the change, on a reading this size); and all three readers said, unprompted, that the strongest lives were those with a rivalry or betrayal that cost something.
**Not kept.** The code stays behind `ON=aftermath` with round 37's reasons, and the hook that lets a situation give an act's reasons (`Instance.reasonsFor`) stays in `grammar.ts` for the rounds that use it.

## Round 38 — wants that collide (pre-registered 21 Oct 2026, 00:04 Oslo, before its code)

The plan's next step (SUMMARY, Next 1; exit points 1, 2 and 5), as Øystein chose it on 20 Oct ("stakes first"). Readers score a life by how many kinds of thing happen in it (round 36), and all three readers of round 37 said, unprompted, that the strongest lives were those with a rivalry or a betrayal that cost something: a headteacher's post schemed for, a feud with the foreman, a café sabotaged by a husband. The engine makes such a contest only in a succession season (a lead announces they will step down; staff go for it; two who are close in record and age become rivals): about two a world-decade, over posts only. Meanwhile people want posts and want to be heard all the time, and say so, without anyone else wanting the same thing in the open.

Hypothesis: when two people who want the same scarce thing learn it of each other, the contest that follows (who works, who runs the other down, who gives way, who wins, what the loser does) gives lives the turns with stakes that readers praise, and they read better.

The change, behind `OFF=collide` (`drama.ts`, `group.ts`, the reasons a contest act gives):

1. **A post, out of season.** Each month a person who wants a post where they work (an open want for it, pursued or not) looks at last month's events they know of: a line by a colleague that gives away the same want (letting it slip, going after it, asking the lead, putting themselves forward). The two may become rivals as they do in a season (more likely the closer their record and age, or with a contest between them before), with that line as the rivalry's first reason. The rivalry is lived as a season's is (working to be seen, running the other down to the lead, picking at the other's work in public, giving way). What they win counts when the post next falls vacant, as a season's campaign does; a season, when it comes, starts from it.
2. **A place in a band.** When a band takes someone in, those around it who want to be heard on the band's instrument and are in no band are in the running. If two or more were, the one not taken is told so ("X was not picked for Red Sky; Y got the place"), with why (the better player, the one the band knew), and answers it as someone passed over for a post does (taking it well, letting it pass, sulking, confronting the one picked), and pride or anger turns it into a grudge.
3. **Reasons said as the situation.** A contest act gives the rivalry's first line, the rival's latest move and who is ahead as its reasons; no trait and no self-story (round 37's hook). The decision record keeps every consideration.

Unchanged: the season, its contest and its decider, the grudge and its acts, peace, and everything a loser does after.

Test (scripts/round.mjs): the scoreboard paired on fresh worlds **3385–3404**; lives, the round's own count (`src/tools/diag/collide.ts`: contests started by kind and source, acts, outcomes, losers' answers, kinds a life; both arms), the consequence map and demography on **3405–3424**; `npm test`, the coherence counter, props. The blind paired reading (`docs/method/reading.md`, scored by `scripts/pairscore.py` with lines and kinds reported) on **3425–3432**: 24 pairs, the three most eventful adults of each world, three readers on another model, read before any other number of the round.
Expected:

1. `collide` off: identical to main (`ident.ts`).
2. **It makes contests:** rivalries over a post or a band place at least **double** the off arm's (about 2 a world-decade), and at least half a band-place contest a world-decade.
3. Reported, not in the keep rule: kinds of line a life, the reading's people, on minus off (expected +1 to +3); contest lines that give a trait or the self-story as a reason (expected none).
4. Gates: the scoreboard within ±2 (decisions that are not a couple's, overall beside it); the coherence counter at zero; demography and `npm test`.
5. **Reads better:** the effect with the order as a term, one-sided 90% lower bound above **0**; contest lines quoted as a good reason at least twice as often as an unfit one.
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 2, 4 and 5 hold. What would change our minds: if contests double and are quoted as good reasons but the lives do not read better, a contest is worth no more than the kinds it adds, and the reading of 24 pairs cannot see a change of a few kinds (+0.1 to +0.2 predicted): the next step is the instrument (more pairs, or a reading that scores each life against its own kinds), not another mechanism; if they read better, contests over other scarce things (a house, a partner, the yearly day) come next; if contests stay under double, people do not learn each other's wants, and telling (round 30) comes first.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: too much forced drama (a rivalry needs two who want the same thing, know it of each other and are close enough to feel it); events plentiful without changing anything important (a contest ends in a winner and a loser, and the post or the place changes hands).

The game lens: a player hears two people want the same thing before either moves, watches who works and who whispers, and sees what the one who lost did about it.

Round 38 withdrawn before its test (21 Oct 2026, 00:15 Oslo). Its code was built and run on throwaway worlds only; 3385–3432 stay unused. The code is kept behind a switch that is off (`ON=collide`).

Built as registered, with two changes made on the smoke worlds before anything else was run: noticing alone made two contests in four worlds, because two colleagues seldom want the same post at once, so one who learns a colleague wants it now decides, as in a season, whether to go for it too (with "@X is after it" weighing for going); and an out-of-season rivalry, which had been lived at a season's pace with nothing about to be decided, ran hot for years (picking at the other's work in public 87 times in four worlds against 8 off), so it is lived at a grudge's pace.

On throwaway worlds 9001–9008, on and off (`src/tools/diag/collide.ts`; `reports/38-smoke-on.txt`, `-off.txt`):

|                                                                                                  | on               | off         |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------- | ----------- |
| contests a world-decade (rivalries over a post, and band places with two or more in the running) | 5.0              | 4.5         |
| of which out of season / in a season / a band place                                              | 2.6 / 2.1 / 0.25 | — / 4.5 / — |
| rivalry and grudge acts on someone's page                                                        | 165              | 143         |
| of them giving a trait or the self-story as a reason                                             | 0                | 136         |
| kinds of line a life: the three busiest of a world / every adult                                 | 41.3 / 23.9      | 42.2 / 22.8 |

Why it was withdrawn: expectation 2, in the keep rule, asks for contests at least double the off arm's, and named that as about two a world-decade. The off arm has 4.5 (season rivalries alone, swinging from 2.8 to 6.3 between sets of worlds), and the round moves contests out of season more than it adds to them: rivals out of season are already in the running when a season comes, so fewer new rivalries form in it. Contests rise by about a tenth, not double, so the test could not keep it. Nor could the reading see much: the busiest lives carry no more kinds of line (41.3 against 42.2) and about a seventh more contest lines.

What it showed: contests out of season are possible and read with fitting reasons (none of 165 contest acts gives a trait); a band place is contested about once in four world-decades, because two who want to be heard seldom stand around the same band at once; and the engine's contests are fewer than its wants because a post is scarce in time, not only in number: two people rarely want the same post in the same years. A round that adds stakes has to add things to contest (posts that open, places in bands, a house two people want, the yearly day), not only ways to notice a contest.

What changes: nothing in the plan's order until Øystein has seen this; see SUMMARY.

## Round M37 — size first, then register (21 Oct 2026, 00:25 Oslo; a change to how a round runs, agreed by Øystein the same night)

Three of the four rounds of 20 Oct (35, 36, 38) were withdrawn before their tests because their registered numbers were guesses made before any code existed: a fold that would take 20–50% off a page took 2–13%; wants that would reach 62–72% of adults reached 56%; contests that would double on a baseline of about two a world-decade moved from 4.5 to 5.0. Each time the mechanism worked as designed and the expectation was what was wrong, and each withdrawal cost a pre-registration, a build and a night's order. What pre-registration protects is the test: fresh worlds, read blind, against a keep rule written before they are run. Sizing a change on throwaway worlds does not touch that.

The change (`CLAUDE.md`, "An experiment", steps 2 and 3; the pull-request template; `docs/method/reading.md`):

1. **Size first.** A round builds its change behind its switch and runs it only on throwaway seeds (9001+), with and without, to learn what it does and how much (its own count, kinds of line a life).
2. **Then register, before any fresh world.** The pre-registration states what the change did on the throwaway worlds and what is expected on fresh ones. The keep rule rests on the blind reading and the gates, with one floor that says the mechanism works; other numbers are reported, not judged. The pushed commit is the timestamp: a fresh world run before it means the round was not pre-registered. After it, the code changes only to fix what breaks, each fix written in BOARD before the test.

Nothing the engine does changes. **Kept** from round 39.

## Round 39 — four together (pre-registered 21 Oct 2026, 00:47 Oslo, before any test world; the first round sized first, by round M37)

The plan's next step, as Øystein chose it on 21 Oct ("bundle, judged on kinds"). Readers score a life by how many kinds of thing happen in it (+0.05 a kind, across lives and within pairs; round 36). Four mechanisms built and kept off since round 30 each add about a kind a life, too little for a 24-pair reading to see: wants that answer what happens (round 36, `ON=occasions`), contests out of season and for a place in a band (round 38, `ON=collide`), a blow that changes something, with the reasons a reader accepts (round 37, `ON=aftermath`), and people telling what they saw (rounds 30–31, `ON=telling`). Each was built, sized or tested alone, and none read worse in a test (aftermath +0.04 ±0.49; telling +0.64 in round 30, −0.13 in round 31). Together they may be a town in which more different things happen to people.

Hypothesis: with the four on together, lives have more kinds of happening, and read no worse; by round 36's finding, more kinds is what readers reward.

The change, behind `OFF=together` (`world.ts`: one switch that turns on all four, each still on alone with its own `ON=<name>`): nothing new in any of the four. Two tools for the test: `src/tools/diag/kinds.ts` (kinds of line a life, every adult and the three busiest of each world) and `pairarm.ts PICK=random` (the reading's people drawn at random among adults in town in both arms).

Sized on throwaway worlds 9001–9008 before this pre-registration (`reports/39-size-on.txt`, `-off.txt`; the screen of each alone, `reports/39-screen.txt`): kinds of line a life, every adult and the three busiest, off 22.8 / 42.2; occasions alone +0.9 / −0.3; contests alone +1.1 / −1.0; aftermath alone +1.2 / +0.7; telling alone +0.6 / −0.6; **the four together +2.4 / +4.3** (lines of the busiest 82.6 against 73.6). With round 39's own switch on the same worlds (`kinds.ts`): every adult +2.35 (sd 2.0 between worlds; seven of eight up), the busiest +4.25 (sd 4.6; six of eight up). `npm test` passes with the four on; its demography check gives births to deaths 0.61 over twenty years in six worlds (the line is 0.6), against 0.63 without, so the battery's demography is watched.

Test (scripts/round.mjs): the scoreboard paired on fresh worlds **3433–3452**; lives, kinds a life (`kinds.ts`, both arms), each mechanism's own count (`occasions.ts`, `collide.ts`, `aftermath.ts`), the consequence map and demography on **3453–3472**; `npm test`, the coherence counter, props. Two blind paired readings, read before any other number of the round, by `docs/method/reading.md` and scored by `scripts/pairscore.py` (lines and kinds reported): **the busiest**, 24 pairs, the three most eventful adults of each of **3473–3480**; **random adults**, 24 pairs, three adults drawn at random from each of **3481–3488** (`PICK=random`). Three readers on another model, each reading the four parts at separate sittings.
Expected:

1. `together` off: identical to main (`ident.ts`).
2. **It adds kinds (the floor):** kinds of line a life, every adult, on minus off on 3453–3472, at least **+1.0** (sized +2.4); reported beside it, the busiest (sized +4.3).
3. Gates: the scoreboard within ±2 (decisions that are not a couple's, overall beside it); the coherence counter at zero in both arms; demography and `npm test`.
4. **Reads no worse, on both packs:** the effect with the order as a term, one-sided 90% lower bound above **−0.3** for the busiest and for the random adults. Reported beside it: whether either reads better (lower bound above 0), the effect with the difference in kinds as a term, and how each mechanism's lines are quoted (good or unfit).
   Keep rule: kept if 1, 2, 3 and 4 hold; then the four are on in the default engine. What would change our minds: if kinds rise and either pack reads worse (lower bound at or below −0.3), the kinds these mechanisms add are not the ones readers reward, and the quotes say which mechanism's lines cost it; if both packs read better, round 36's finding holds as a cause and not only as a correlation, and later rounds can aim at kinds directly; if the random adults read better and the busiest do not, the four are for the quiet majority, and the busiest lives need stakes of another kind.

The warnings in `WHY.md` this round risks: complexity mistaken for progress (four mechanisms at once, kept only if lives gain kinds and read no worse on two packs); too much forced drama (contests, wants and blows' changes added together; the random pack is where forced drama would show).

The game lens: a town where people want things because of what happens around them, contest them, change after blows, and talk about each other; a player meets more of it in an ordinary life, not only in the busiest.

2. Kinds of line a life, every adult: **24.79** on against **23.80** off, **+0.99** (paired by world ±0.72, 14 of 20 worlds up); the floor was at least +1.0 ✗, missed by 0.01. Reported: honest whys 71% of decisions on, 74% off; wants resolved 69% against 62%.
4. The blind readings (bar: one-sided 90% lower bound above −0.3 on both) ✓, and both read better (above 0):
   - **the busiest:** the effect with the order as a term **+0.38 ±0.30**, lower bound **+0.19** (majority on in 16 of 24: 12 of 12 where on was read second, 4 of 12 where first; the lean toward the second version is +0.60, and the order term takes it out);
   - **random adults:** **+0.33 ±0.49**, lower bound **+0.02** (majority on in 13 of 24).
     Kinds of line on the pages read: +7.5 a life among the busiest, +4.2 among random adults (sd 16.4 between pairs, so within the noise of +0.99). With the difference in kinds as a term the effects fall to +0.16 ±0.38 and +0.09 ±0.30, and a kind more is worth +0.03 and +0.06. The four's own lines (telling, a blow's change, contest reasons, a want's occasion) were quoted as good reasons 27 and 17 times, as unfit 2 and 7, as false 6 and 2 (a want that lapsed "and never went after it", said of someone who had). Over all lines, unfit reasons rose (67 against 50 among the busiest, 74 against 48 among random adults) and repeats fell (38 against 49, 50 against 51).

Read by the rules set beforehand: 1, 3 and 4 hold, 2 misses by a hundredth of a kind. **Not kept**: the rule asked for all four, and a floor that counts only when it is met is no floor. What the round showed stands beside that. Its "what would change our minds" fires as written: both packs read better, so round 36's finding holds as a cause and not only as a correlation: a life in which more different things happen reads better, and once the difference in kinds is a term little effect is left. The random pack clears zero by two hundredths, so the cause is shown for the busiest lives and only just for the quiet majority. The floor was the weakest number in the design, set from eight worlds whose spread allowed anything from +0.7 to +4.

What the readers faulted that the four brought or made more common (nothing is changed after the result; these are for the next round):

- a want's lapse said of someone who had gone after it ("Freya no longer talks about wanting to be heard singing [had wanted it since 2027, and never went after it]");
- a contest's reasons after it was settled: the winner "still sore about the skipper's job (2029)" when offering peace or picking holes, a rival conceding while "is ahead", a rival still working to be seen after the other had left the workplace;
- declining a job in the city for "had wanted out since 2013; had no wish to see anywhere else" (an escape want and its refusal side by side);
- and in both arms: "thought it needed saying" for drifting apart, leaving a band "quietly: there was a baby at home" years after the birth, "(and once more between)", "1 years", "no progress for 1.3 years".

`together` is off unless asked for (`ON=together`), as for every round not kept, and identical to main with it off (`reports/39-ident.txt`); the four stay behind their own switches. What comes next is Øystein's to order (SUMMARY).
